# Google

This is a digital version of a book that was kept for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for its copyright to expire, allowing the book to enter the **public domain** <note>A work enters the public domain when its intellectual property rights have expired, been forfeited, or are inapplicable, making it free for anyone to use.</note>. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has ended. Whether a book is in the public domain can vary from one country to another. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture, and knowledge that is often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations, and other marginalia <note>Handwritten notes or marks made in the margins of a book by previous readers or librarians.</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive; therefore, to continue providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying <note>The use of software or "bots" to automatically search or download large volumes of data.</note>.

We also ask that you:

+ **Make non-commercial use of the files** We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.
+ **Refrain from automated querying** Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system. If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>OCR: Technology used to convert different types of documents, such as scanned paper documents or PDF files, into editable and searchable data.</note>, or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.
+ **Maintain attribution** The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.
+ **Keep it legal** Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we cannot offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

## About Google Book Search

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web at http://books.google.com/

# Google

## About this book

This is a digital copy of a book that has been preserved for generations on library shelves before being digitized by Google as part of a project aimed at making the world’s books available online.

It has survived long enough for its copyright to expire and for it to enter the **public domain** <note>A work enters the public domain when it is no longer under copyright protection, allowing the public to use it freely.</note>. A public domain book is a book that was never protected by copyright or whose legal copyright terms have expired. Whether a book is classified as being in the public domain may vary from country to country. Public domain books are a link to the past, representing a heritage of history, culture, and knowledge that is often difficult to discover.

Comments, notes, and other marginal annotations <note>original: "annotazioni a margine"</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file as a testament to the long journey traveled by the book, from the original publisher to the library, and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them universally available. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. However, this work is expensive; therefore, in order to continue offering this service, we have taken steps to prevent illicit use by commercial entities, including the imposition of restrictions on automated querying <note>The use of automated software to send requests to a server.</note>.

We also ask that you:

+ *Do not make commercial use of these files* We designed Google Book Search for use by individual private users and we ask that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *Do not send automated queries* Do not send automated queries of any kind to Google. If you are conducting research in the fields of machine translation, optical character recognition (OCR), or other areas where you need to use large amounts of text, we invite you to contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ *Keep the watermark* The Google "watermark" <note>original: "filigrana"</note> that appears in each file is essential for informing users about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Do not remove it.

+ *Use it legally* Regardless of how you use it, remember that it is your responsibility to ensure that your use is legal. Do not assume that because a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, it is also in the public domain for users in other countries. The criteria establishing whether a book is protected by copyright vary from country to country, and we cannot offer guidance on whether a specific use of a book is permitted. Do not assume that because a book appears in Google Book Search it means it can be used in any way and in any country in the world. The penalties for copyright violations can be very severe.

## TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS

### COUNT

## ALESSANDRO AGUCCHI

->*KNIGHT OF THE IRON CROWN*<- <note>original: "Cavaliere della Corona Ferrea." This was an order of merit established by Napoleon I in 1805 in the Kingdom of Italy, and later continued by the Austrian Empire. It signifies Agucchi's high social and civic standing.</note>

---

To you, Sir Knight, who were among the first to encourage me when it occurred to me to include this work by Leonardo in the *Collection of Italian authors who treat of the motion of waters*, I have decided to dedicate it. And I am certain that this will be a welcome gesture to you, when I consider your profound intelligence in these matters, and the supreme value of a work that was so highly esteemed by a celebrated man who was numbered among your friends.

Giambatista Venturi <note>Giambattista Venturi (1746–1822) was a renowned Italian physicist and historian. He is famous for discovering the "Venturi effect" in hydraulics and was instrumental in bringing Leonardo da Vinci's scientific manuscripts to light after centuries of neglect.</note>, in a text published in Paris in 1797 regarding the manuscripts of Leonardo da Vinci, has

<summary>This page features a formal dedication to Count Alessandro Agucchi, acknowledging his expertise and encouragement in publishing Leonardo da Vinci’s work on water. It establishes the scholarly lineage of the edition by citing the influence of the celebrated physicist Giambatista Venturi.</summary>
<keywords>Alessandro Agucchi, Giambatista Venturi, Leonardo da Vinci, hydraulics, motion of waters, Iron Crown, dedication, 19th-century science</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the editorial introduction, quoting the physicist Giambatista Venturi regarding the scientific methodology found in Leonardo da Vinci's manuscripts.</meta>

made it known *that from the reading of those codices one finds that Leonardo was always guided by a geometric spirit, whether he wished to analyze an object, link together a chain of reasoning, or generalize his own ideas.* He always desired that *experience precede the reasoning about things.* I shall treat such a subject, he says himself, but first I will conduct some experiments, as it is my principle to cite experience first, and then demonstrate why bodies are compelled to act in this or that manner. This is the method to be followed in the research of the phenomena of nature. It is true that nature begins with reasoning (1), and ends with experience; but no matter: we must take the opposite path; we must, as I said, begin with experience and, by means of it, discover the reason behind it. *Thus spoke Leonardo a century before Bacon <note>Francis Bacon (1561–1626), the English philosopher often credited with developing the scientific method. The author is highlighting that Leonardo practiced empirical science long before it was formally established.</note>. In mechanics he knew, among other things, the theory of forces applied obliquely to the lever arm; the respective resistance of ships; the laws of friction <note>original: "strofinamento." Leonardo conducted the first systematic studies of friction, though his work remained unpublished for centuries.</note> given to us later by Amontons <note>Guillaume Amontons (1663–1705), a French physicist who rediscovered the laws of friction in 1699.</note>; the influence of the center*

---

(1) Leonardo may be forgiven for this erroneous proposition <note>The author refers to Leonardo's statement that "nature begins with reasoning." In the 19th-century view, nature is seen as governed by laws that must be discovered, rather than starting with an abstract logic.</note>, which he perhaps uttered so as not to directly oppose the prevailing opinion of his times. Today, everyone knows that reasoning is based upon abstract propositions, and that abstract propositions are not true unless they rest upon the observation of facts; thus, reasoning cannot precede experience.

<summary>This page discusses Leonardo da Vinci's scientific method, emphasizing his commitment to experimental observation over abstract reasoning centuries before Francis Bacon. It notes his early discoveries in mechanics, specifically friction and leverage.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, scientific method, empiricism, Francis Bacon, Giambattista Venturi, mechanics, friction, Guillaume Amontons, 19th-century science</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the list of Leonardo da Vinci’s scientific discoveries as recounted by the physicist Giambattista Venturi, transitioning from mechanics into optics and hydraulics, and then into the editor’s description of the manuscript’s provenance.</meta>

*of gravity on bodies at rest or in motion: the application of the principle of <term>virtual velocities<note>A fundamental concept in mechanics where the work done by all forces during an infinitesimal displacement is zero for a system in equilibrium; Leonardo anticipated this principle centuries before it was formally defined.</note></term> to many cases that advanced mathematics has generalized in our own day. In optics, he described the so-called <note>original: "camera ottica"</note> camera obscura before Porta <note>Giambattista della Porta (1535–1615), often credited with the invention of the camera obscura, though Leonardo's notes prove he understood it much earlier.</note>: he explained, before Maurolico <note>Francesco Maurolico (1494–1575), an Italian mathematician and astronomer who studied optics.</note>, the shape of the sun's image through a polygonal aperture: he taught us aerial perspective: the nature of colored shadows: the movements of the iris: the effects of the duration of visible impressions, and many other phenomena of the eye that are not found in Witelo <note>original: "Vitellione." Witelo was a 13th-century Polish friar whose work on optics was a standard text for centuries.</note>. In short, Leonardo had not only observed all that Castelli <note>Benedetto Castelli (1578–1643), a mathematician and student of Galileo.</note> wrote a century after him on the motion of waters, but it seems to me further that the former has in this area surpassed the latter, whom Italy has nonetheless considered until now as the founder of <term>Hydraulics<note>The branch of science and engineering concerned with the mechanical properties of liquids.</note></term>.*

*The work that now for the first time sees the light of day confirms, in my opinion, what Venturi asserts. The manuscript of the aforementioned work exists in Rome in the Barberini Library, and I have been able to obtain a copy of it by means of the most distinguished Dr. Francesco Tassi, a resident member of the <note>original: "Accademico residente della Crusca"</note> Accademia della Crusca and former librarian to His Imperial and Royal Highness the Grand Duke of Tuscany, in whose possession is found a codex by Leonardo himself, entitled = Treatise on the nature, weight, and motion of waters, and observations on the course of rivers, which he has courteously offered to me for the purpose of publishing it.*

*In sending this work to the press, I have believed it necessary to adhere faithfully to the manuscript, and without correcting any*

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page concludes the editor Francesco Cardinali’s preface to his collection of Leonardo da Vinci's scientific writings. Having previously discussed the groundbreaking nature of Leonardo's discoveries in mechanics and optics, the editor now addresses the stylistic "roughness" of the manuscript and the technical challenges of the printing process.</meta>

...anything, except for the spelling. At times the phrasing is not the clearest, and the turns of phrase are not the most proper or exact, as you, Sir Knight <note>Likely referring to the physicist Giambattista Venturi, mentioned on the previous page, who held the title of Knight of the Iron Crown.</note>, will recognize; but attribute this to the usual writing style of Leonardo <note>original: "Lionardo"</note>, who devoted all his care to the subject matter he was treating, neglecting the style.

Regarding the imperfections that you, Lord Count, will find in this printing, I will say only that I trust I shall find kind indulgence not only in your own soul, but in the hearts of all those who know how great a difficulty it is to commit to the press, without error, works transmitted from <term>copyist<note>A person who manually transcribed manuscripts before the age of digital scanning; errors often "mutated" or accumulated as each new scribe copied the work of the previous one.</note></term> to copyist. With this confidence, I remain, full of esteem and consideration,

->*Your most humble and obliged servant*<-
->**FRANCESCO CARDINALI**<-

<summary>The editor Francesco Cardinali concludes his preface by excusing Leonardo da Vinci's unpolished writing style as a result of the artist's focus on scientific substance over rhetoric, while also apologizing for any printing errors inherent in transcribing old manuscripts.</summary>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Book One of the treatise. It follows the blank flyleaf that separated the introductory table of contents from the main text, establishing the foundational terminology for the study of water and its movements.</meta>

# BOOK ONE
## OF THE SPHERE OF WATER

---

->CHAPTER I.<-

->*Definitions of the names and terms most commonly used in the subject of Water.*<-

A **pelago** <note>original: "Pelago"; traditionally referring to the open sea, here used to describe a deep, calm basin</note> is that which has a wide and deep shape, in which the waters remain with little movement.

A **whirlpool** <note>original: "Gorgo"</note> is of the nature of a *pelago*, except for a variation in one part; and this is, that the waters entering the *pelago* do so without impact, whereas those of the whirlpool enter with great falls, boilings, and surgings created by the continuous revolutions of the waters.

All lakes, and all gulfs of the sea, and all inland seas <note>original: "mari mediterranei"; referring generally to land-locked seas</note> are born from rivers which spread their waters into them, and from the impediment to their downward slope, so that they are gatherings of the waters of rivers.

A **river** is that which occupies the site of the lowest part of the valleys and flows continuously.

A **torrent** is that which flows only because of rains; it too descends into the depths of the valleys and joins with the rivers.

A **canal** is the term for waters regulated between embankments by human aid.

A **source** <note>original: "Fonte"; can also mean spring or fountain</note> is called the birthplace of rivers.

A **lake** is that where the waters of the rivers take on great width.

**Ponds** are places, or rather receptacles of drainage or rainwater, where, because their bottoms are impermeable <note>original: "stagni"; a play on the words for "pond" and "watertight"</note> and dense, the earth cannot drink nor dry such waters.

**Deep pools** <note>original: "Pozzi"; literally "wells," but here referring to deep pits in a riverbed</note> are the sudden depths of rivers.

**Chasms** <note>original: "Barratri"</note> are also places of sudden depth.

An **embankment** <note>original: "Argine"</note> is that which, with its sudden height, resists the spreading of rivers, canals, or torrents.

A **steep bank** <note>original: "Ripa"</note> shall be higher than the embankment. A **shore** <note>original: "Riva"</note> shall be lower than the embankment.

A **beach** <note>original: "Spiaggia"</note> shall be at the ultimate low point of the places that end at the waters.

**Caverns** are like ovens <note>a reference to the rounded, hollowed-out shape created by erosion</note> reaching deep under the embankment, in which the waters swirl forcefully and always increase in size.

<note>Glossary of terms defined above: Pelago, Whirlpool, River, Torrent, Canal, Source, Lake, Ponds, Deep pools, Chasms, Embankment, Steep bank, Shore, Beach, Caverns.</note>

<summary>This introductory chapter establishes a precise vocabulary for hydraulic science, defining various water features based on their depth, movement, and relationship to the surrounding landscape.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, water terminology, rivers, whirlpools, erosion, embankments, lakes, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical definitions of hydraulic phenomena started in Chapter I of the previous page. While the previous page focused on large bodies of water like lakes and rivers, this page moves toward the physical components of the riverbed and the specific terminology for water's movement.</meta>

CENTERED TEXT: ## LEONARDO DA VINCI

**Hollows** <note>original: "Grotte"; here referring to the cavities or undercuts carved into the side of a riverbank</note> are caves made in the banks of rivers by the current of the rivers; these have a length following the line of the water's course; they have some depth, and even push beneath the foundation of the bank, and their shape tapers off toward the ends of their length.

**Tempests** <note>original: "Procelle"</note> are water storms.

**Gravels** are created by the course of rivers, and in the end are consumed <note>worn down into smaller particles</note>.

Gravels are smaller the closer the river that generates them is to the **pelago** <note>the sea or a deep basin</note>.

**Sand** is extremely minute gravel.

**Stones** <note>original: "sassi"; can refer to rocks or large pebbles</note> are composed by the courses of the rivers, and they are arranged in layers, or rather in steps, according to the discharge of the turbulence carried by the course of the rivers.

Stones are not found where there was never a sea or a lake.

The **rubbing** <note>original: "confregazione"</note> of stones against one another in the courses of rivers wears away the angles of the stones <note>explaining why river stones are rounded</note>.

---

### CHAPTER II.

->*Other definitions, and terms used in the subject of waters.*<-

**Submerging** is understood as things that go beneath the waters. **Intersection** of water occurs when one river cuts across another. Rebounding, circulation, revolution, turning over, swirling, leaping back, submerging, surging, declining, elevation, hollowing, consuming, impact, ruining, colliding, rubbing, waving, furrowing, boiling, falling back, slowing, gushing, pouring, overturning, re-plunging, meandering, currents, murmurs, noises, gorging back, ebb and flow, ruins, shatterings, abyss, caverns, swirling, precipices, overflowing, tumults, confusions, clashing, boilings, submergence of surface waves, slowing, breaking, dividing, opening, swiftness, vehemence, fury, impetuosity, mixed convergence, jolting, and the erosion of banks.

---

### CHAPTER III.

->*What water is.*<-

Water is the second least heavy among the **four elements** <note>according to the classical science of Leonardo's time: earth, water, air, and fire</note>, and of the second highest mobility; this never has rest until it joins with its maritime element, where, if not disturbed by winds, it becomes stable.

<vocab>hollows, tempests, gravels, deep basin, sand, discharge, rubbing, intersection, submergence, boilings, mobility</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the physical materials of a river—such as gravel, sand, and stone—explaining their formation through water action, and provides an extensive list of terms describing the chaotic movements of water.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, riverbanks, erosion, gravel formation, water terminology, four elements, sedimentation</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of water's properties and its behavior as an element. While the previous page provided a glossary of hydrological terms like 'submerging' and 'hollowing,' this section begins mid-sentence to conclude a list of physical characteristics before moving into a geometric proof of water's spherical nature.</meta>

LEONARDO DA VINCI 275

...and rests with its surface equidistant from the center of the World <note>original: "centro del Mondo"; in Leonardo's cosmology, this refers to the center of the Earth as the center of gravity</note>. This is the source of growth and the vital fluid <note>original: "umore"; referring to the moisture or "humors" essential for life in plants and animals</note> of all living bodies. No sublunary <note>a traditional term for the Earthly realm below the orbit of the moon</note> thing maintains its primary figure and form without it. It binds and increases bodies, and gives them growth. Nothing lighter than water can penetrate it without violence. It readily rises through heat as a thin vapor through the air. Cold freezes it; stillness corrupts it; it takes on every odor, color, and flavor, though by itself it has no flavor, nor color, nor odor. It penetrates all porous bodies. Against its fury, no human defense is of any use, and if it is, it will not be permanent. In its swift course, it becomes a supporter of things heavier than itself. It can, with motion and leaping, lift itself on high. When it falls, it submerges with it, in its ruins, things lighter than itself. It governs its course, sometimes in the middle, sometimes at the bottom, sometimes at the top; one volume of water climbs over the intersecting course of another; and if it were not so, the surface of flowing waters would be without ripples <note>original: "globi"; literally "globes," describing the rounded, mound-like shapes of water in turbulent flow</note>; every small obstacle, whether on the bank or on the bottom, will cause damage to the opposite bank or bottom; shallow water does more damage to the bank in its course than it does when it flows full <note>meaning when the river is at its full capacity, the flow is more stable than when it is low and erratic</note>. Its parts exert no weight upon the parts beneath them, and its upper parts do not press down <note>original: "non danno granella"; likely a transcription error for "gravezza" (heaviness or pressure)</note> on the lower ones.

### CHAPTER IV.

->*That the element of water is spherical.*<-

Every flexible and liquid element necessarily has a spherical surface, as does the element of water. And this is proven, but first, it is necessary to establish some concepts, which are:
1. That thing is higher which is farther from the center of the World.
2. That thing is lower which is closer to said center.
3. Water does not move by itself unless it descends.
4. In moving, water descends.

These four concepts serve me to prove: that water, which does not move of its own accord, has its surface equidistant from the center of the world—not speaking of droplets, or other small quantities that attract one another like steel attracts its filings <note>an early observation of surface tension compared to magnetism</note>, but of large quantities. I say that no part of the surface of the water moves by itself unless it descends. Therefore, the sphere of water, having no part of its surface that can descend, must—according to the first concept—not move by itself. And if you carefully consider every tiny particle of such a surface, you will find it surrounded by other similar particles, which are at an equal distance from the [center]...

<summary>Leonardo details the physical and life-sustaining properties of water and begins a geometric argument proving that the surface of a large body of water is spherical and equidistant from the Earth's center.</summary>
<keywords>hydrostatics, center of gravity, spherical water, surface tension, vital humors, fluid dynamics, erosion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s geometric and physical arguments regarding the nature of water. The previous page ended mid-sentence while describing the effects of obstacles on water flow; this page transitions into a formal proof concerning the equilibrium of the "sphere of water" and why its surface must be perfectly equidistant from the center of gravity.</meta>

LEONARDO DA VINCI 276

...center of the World <note>In Leonardo's physics, "centro del Mondo" refers to the center of the Earth, which he understood as the center of gravity for the heavy elements.</note>, and at the same distance as that particle which is surrounded by it from said center. Therefore, by the third conception <note>referring to a previously established axiom or "common notion" in his scientific method</note>, such a particle of water will not move of its own accord because it is surrounded by banks of equal height, and thus every circle of such particles becomes a vessel for the particle enclosed within that circle, which vessel has the circumference of its lips at equal height. And for this reason, such a particle, together with all other similar ones that compose the surface of the sphere of water, will by necessity be motionless in itself; and consequently, since each is at an equal height from the center of the World, necessity makes that surface spherical. And below, it is not necessary to be spherical, as reason and experience show <note>Leonardo acknowledges that while the surface of the ocean or a lake is curved due to gravity, the seabed or the "bottom" of the water element is irregular.</note>.

### CHAPTER V.

*Another proof that the sphere of water is perfectly round.*

Water does not move of its own accord, as was said in the previous [chapter], unless it descends; and if it moves of its own accord, it follows that it is descending. Therefore, no part of the sphere of water is to move of its own accord, and this is because any part is surrounded by water of equal height, which encloses it and cannot be surpassed by it in any direction. Below the proof is shown (fig. 1, plate I.). Let ABH be the sphere of water, let C be a quantity of water surrounded and enclosed by water A and B. I say, by the previous conclusion, that water C will not move because it finds no descent by the definition of a sphere, because parts A and B of the surface of the water are equally remote from the center of the World, just as C is a part of the same surface of the water, and thus it follows that C remains immobile.

### CHAPTER VI.

*That if the element of water were not spherical, the water would move of its own accord.*

Given, for example <note>original: "verbi-grazia"; a Latin-derived phrase meaning "for example" or "for the sake of the word."</note>, a plane of water on the surface of the sphere of water, the extremes will move to the middle of such a plane. This is proven with the figure that follows (fig. 2, plate I.). And let the plane ABC be given in the sphere ACMD: I say that water A and water C will move toward water B. For if water does not move (as was said for the fourth [chapter]) unless it descends; and it does not descend unless it is higher; and that which is further from the center of the World is higher, it follows that the water...

<summary>Leonardo uses geometric proofs to demonstrate that the surface of water must be spherical because water only moves when it can descend toward the center of gravity.</summary>

...A, and the water C, which are further from the center D than is the water B—which is closer to center D—are higher than water B; for this reason they descend, and for this reason they move toward water B.

### CHAPTER VII.

->*That a spherical heavy body placed upon the sphere of water is motionless.*<-

A spherical heavy body <term>grave sferico: a solid, heavy sphere used as a theoretical weight in physics experiments</term> placed upon the sphere of water will not change its position. The truth of this proposition is manifest through the fourth and fifth [propositions] of this work. Because if the water is spherical—since it cannot move of its own accord within its own sphere, as its parts are equally distant from the center—it follows by necessity that the spherical heavy body, placed in any part of the sphere of water, cannot move; this is because, regardless of where it is placed, it is equally distant from the center of the World <note>original: "centro del Mondo"; in Leonardo's cosmology, the center of the Earth is the center of gravity for the universe</note>. Let ABCN, then, be the sphere of water (fig. 3, plate I); let A be the spherical heavy body, and let N be the center. I say that A will not move toward point B, nor toward point C, because point A is at an equal distance from the center N as are point B and point C.

### CHAPTER VIII.

->*Whether a spherical heavy body placed at the edge of a plane of water will move?*<-

A spherical heavy body placed at the edge of a perfect plane <note>In this context, a "perfect plane" refers to a flat, straight surface. Leonardo argues that on a round Earth, a "flat" surface is actually higher at its edges than at its center</note> of water will not remain still, but will move immediately to the middle of that plane. This is proven by the sixth [proposition]; for if a flat plane of water were placed on the curved surface of the sphere of water, the edges would move toward the middle of such a plane, because the middle is lower <note>closer to the Earth's center</note> than the edges. Therefore, we conclude that the same will happen to a spherical heavy body placed at the edge of the plane of water, because the edge of such a plane is higher than the middle. As appears in the following figure (fig. 4, plate I), where A is a spherical heavy body placed at the edge A of the plane ABC of the sphere ACMD; because it is higher—that is, more distant from the center D—than is point B, which is closer to the same center, it is a necessity that it moves toward point B.

<summary>Leonardo demonstrates that on a spherical Earth, a "flat" surface is actually uneven relative to gravity, causing a sphere to roll toward the center of that plane, whereas it remains stationary on a curved surface that follows the Earth's circumference.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, fluid mechanics, center of the World, spherical heavy body, equilibrium, geometry of water</keywords>

### CHAPTER IX.

->*On the center of the sphere of water.*<-

The center of the sphere of water is the true center of the roundness of our World, which is composed of both water and land in a round shape. However, if you wished to find the center of the element of land, this is determined by a distance equidistant from the surface of the Ocean sea, and not from the equidistant surface of the land; for it is clearly understood that this globe of the earth has no point of perfect roundness except in those parts where there is the Sea, or marshes, or other still waters; and any part of the land that rises out of that sea moves away from its center.

### CHAPTER X.

->*That the earth cannot share a common center with the water.*<-

We see the Nile starting from the southern regions and marking various provinces, flowing toward the North for a distance of 3000 miles, and emptying into the Mediterranean Sea of Egypt. And if we wish to give this a drop of ten cubits <term>brazza: an old Italian unit of measurement, or "cubit," which Leonardo used to calculate the gradient or slope of rivers</term> per mile—which is the amount commonly granted to the general flow of rivers—we will find that the end of the Nile is ten miles lower than its beginning. Furthermore, we see the Rhine, Rhone, and Danube starting from the German regions, almost at the center of Europe; one flows to the East, another to the North, and the last to the southern seas. If you consider everything well, you will see that the plains of Europe form a gathering point that is much more elevated than the high peaks of the coastal mountains. Now think how much higher their peaks must be than the sea shores. Therefore, etc. <note>Leonardo is arguing that the land must be significantly "higher" than the sea level for rivers to flow such distances, meaning the center of the solid earth's mass is not the same as the center of the water's sphere.</note>

### CHAPTER XI.

->*Whether the surface of flowing rivers is spherical or not, and whether it is necessary for the surface of water in steady motion to be straight or curved.*<-

It can be both straight and curved, provided that in its motion it moves closer to the center of the World; for if it did not do so, that water would remain motionless, unless it were a reflux <note>original: "corso reflesso"; referring to a backward flow or tide</note>, and I do not take this into account because it is a forced motion <term>moto violento: in Renaissance physics, a "violent" or forced motion is one caused by an external power, as opposed to "natural" motion caused by gravity</term>. It is proven that if OCR <note>These letters refer to a geometric diagram in the original manuscript</note> is a mountain, from the...

<summary>Leonardo argues that the true center of the world's roundness is defined by the surface of the seas rather than the land. He uses the long, descending flow of major European and African rivers to prove that land is an irregular elevation above the perfect sphere of the oceans.</summary>

...from the top of which a river descends, and it runs down to the depth of its sphere. This water can descend along the straight line (fig. 5, plate I) CON, and along the curved line ABC. Because its height from the Center of the World N is greater along the line CN than along AN, or along ON. And the same applies to the curve, which is higher at CN than at BN, or at AN; thus, one concludes that it is not necessary for the water's path to be more curved than straight, etc. <note>Leonardo is arguing that gravity—the "pull" toward the Center of the World—is the primary driver of water's motion, regardless of whether the path is a straight incline or a curve following the Earth's arc.</note>

### CHAPTER XII.

->*Whether a river passing through a lake disrupts the lake’s sphericity.*<-

The question is asked whether a river, in passing through a lake, disrupts the uniform distance that the surface of such a lake had from the center of the World before the aforementioned river passed through it (fig. 6, plate I). This is a fine question; and you will show that such a surface does indeed ruin that uniform distance from the center of the World to make room and passage for the said river, based on the fourth [principle] of this work, which shows that water does not move unless it descends. 

And here one must understand if the outlet of such a river has a width similar to its entrance. If it does, then it is necessary that such water has a uniform flow, as according to the 39th [proposition] of the 8th [book]; which says that the motion of every river, in an equal amount of time, delivers an equal weight of water in every part of its length. Now, if the river brings in water that requires a drop of one cubit <term>braccio: an Italian unit of measurement, literally meaning "arm," roughly 23 inches or 58 centimeters</term> per mile, and the width of the outlet is equal to the width of the entrance as stated, it is necessary that the entire river passing through the lake also has a drop of one cubit per mile. Consequently, the surface of such a lake will be at varying distances from the center of the World. And the water will have such a course: that part of the lake water will have the slowest motion which is found furthest from the shortest line connecting the entrance to the exit of the river passing through that lake. <note>Leonardo correctly observes that for water to flow *through* a body of water, there must be a slight slope or pressure gradient, meaning the lake is no longer perfectly "flat" or equidistant from the Earth's center.</note>

### CHAPTER XIII.

->*On the growth of the sphere of water.*<-

The sphere of water increases and decreases, perceptibly or imperceptibly, according to the greater or lesser, more universal or less universal floods of water returned to that sphere by the water of rivers, and rains, etc.

## CAPITOLO XIV.

### *Experiment on many various cases of the sphere of water.*

In a perfectly round drop of dew, one can consider various cases regarding the function of the <term>sphere of water</term> <note>Leonardo uses "sphere of water" to describe both a literal drop and the theoretical shell of water that he believed surrounded the Earth in the medieval cosmological model.</note>, such as how it may contain within itself a solid body without the destruction of its surface (fig. 2. plate I.). First, let a cube of lead be taken, the size of a <term>grain of millet</term> <note>original: "grano di panico." A common Renaissance unit of measurement for very small objects.</note>, and being joined to a very fine thread, let it be submerged within such a drop; you will see that such a drop does not lose its initial sphericity, etc.

## CAPITOLO XV.

### *Of the position of water.*

Every part of the surface of water desires to be situated at an equal distance from the center of its element. And that part of the surface which rises above the rest happens because of <term>opposing motions</term> <note>original: "contrari moti." This refers to turbulence or waves where water is forced upward against its natural tendency to seek the lowest point.</note> that overlap between it and the bottom, etc.

## CAPITOLO XVI.

### *Of the rest of water.*

Every part of water within other water that is without motion lies in equal rest with that which is situated at its same level. Here, experience shows us that if there were a lake of very great width, which lay within itself without motion from wind, or from inflow or outflow, and you were to remove a very small part of the height of that <term>embankment</term> <note>original: "argine." Here referring to a dam or the natural containing wall of a lake.</note> which is found from the surface of the water downwards, all that water which is found from the edge of said cut in the embankment upwards will pass through that cut; but it does not move, nor pull out of the lake with it, any part of the water found from the cut downwards. 

In this case, nature—constrained by the reason of her law—wills that all parts of the surface of the water which are supported by embankments without any inflow or outflow, be equally supported by the <term>Center of the World</term> <note>Leonardo refers to the center of gravity of the Earth. He believed water naturally forms a spherical shell around this center.</note>. The demonstration is held here below. Let us say that the water of the said lake is supported by the embankments (fig. 8. plate I.) NOAF, and that NMRA is oil spread upon that water, and that the cut in the embankment is MN; I say that all the oil found from N upwards will pass through the break without moving any part of the water situated beneath it, etc.

<summary>Leonardo examines how surface tension allows a dewdrop to hold a weight and explains the principle of equilibrium, arguing that water only flows when its surface is higher than its surroundings relative to the Earth's center.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s exploration of hydrostatic principles. Following the discussion on the behavior of water droplets and equilibrium on page 280, Leonardo now scales these observations up to the global level, applying the laws of "natural motion" and gravity to the Earth’s oceans, rivers, and the physical relationship between land and sea.</meta>

LEONARDO DA VINCI 281

### CHAPTER XVII.

->*Of the natural motion of water.*<-

Of waters joined together, all those that exceed the height of the Ocean will have gravity and will be in <term>natural motion</term> <note>Natural motion, in the physics of Leonardo's time, refers to the movement of an object toward its "natural place"—for heavy things like water, this means moving downward toward the center of the world.</note>. This is proven because all waters situated higher than the surface of the <term>sphere of water</term> <note>Leonardo envisions the Earth as a globe where the water forms a theoretical perfect sphere or "shell" around the center.</note> are more remote from the <term>center of the World</term> <note>In the geocentric cosmology Leonardo studied, the center of the Earth was considered the center of the universe.</note> than that surface; therefore, by the fourth [proposition] they will have gravity and natural motion.

### CHAPTER XVIII.

->*How water descends to its center.*<-

The motions of <term>heavy elements</term> <note>original: "elementi gravi." Specifically earth and water, which were believed to possess an innate desire to move toward the center of the universe.</note> are not toward the center for the sake of reaching that center, but because the medium where they are located cannot resist them; and when the element finds resistance in its own element, its body no longer weighs, nor does it seek any longer to go to the center. Therefore, when water descends, it does not descend to go to the center, but because it finds no resistance in its medium [the air]. Since water weighs in the air, it descends by the shortest path, and divides and opens the air that lies below the center of its gravity with all its parts equally; it does not divide the air that is at its sides, because it is not situated above it. Because of this, a "hole" of the shortest length is made through the air until the water reaches that which resists it. This resistance being water, that water which was falling through the air into this water no longer seeks to go to the center, because it no longer divides the water as it did the air.

### CHAPTER XIX.

->*Why the water of the sea seems higher than the land to many simpletons.*<-

Naturally, no part of the land uncovered by water will ever be lower than the surface of the sphere of the waters. D B (fig. 9 plate I.) is a plain from which a river flows to the sea; and because in truth this uncovered land is not in a position of equality—since if it were so, the river would have no motion—wherefore this site, being in motion, should rather be called a slope than a plain. And thus this plain D B terminates in such a way with the sphere of the water that if one continues it in a <term>straight line</term> <note>original: "continua rettitudine."</note> from B to A, this A would enter beneath the sea; and from this arises the fact that the sea seems higher than the uncovered land, etc.

<summary>Leonardo explains that water moves toward the center of the Earth only when its medium (like air) fails to provide resistance, and clarifies that the visual illusion of the sea being "higher" than the land is due to the Earth's curvature and the necessary slope of riverbeds.</summary>
<keywords>gravity, natural motion, sphere of water, center of the world, heavy elements, resistance, curvature of the earth, sea level, hydraulics</keywords>

LEONARDO DA VINCI 282

## CAPITOLO XX.
### *Another proof of the same.*

We see clearly that if the barrier to the sea is removed, it will clothe the earth and make it of perfect <term>roundness</term> <note>original: "rotondità." Leonardo refers to the "sphere of water" which would naturally cover the globe if the land were not pushed up by other forces.</note>. Now consider how much earth would have to be removed to make the marine waves cover the World. Therefore, whatever was removed would be higher than the shore of the sea.

## CAPITOLO XXI.
### *On the gravity of water.*

Earth is <term>heavy</term> <note>original: "grave." In Renaissance physics, gravity was seen as an innate tendency of certain elements—earth and water—to move toward the center of the world.</note> in its own sphere, but it is more so when it is in a lighter element. Fire is light in its sphere, and more so when it is in a heavier element. Water is both heavy and light; it is heavier when it is in a lighter element, and lighter when it is in a heavier element; thus, no simple element has its own <term>gravity</term> or <term>levity</term> <note>original: "levità," or the quality of being light/rising. Leonardo is describing what we now call buoyancy: an object's weight changes depending on whether it is in air (a light medium) or water (a heavier medium).</note> within its own sphere. And if a bladder full of air weighs more on the scales than when it is empty, this is because that air is <term>condensed</term>; and fire could be condensed so that it would be heavier than air, or equal to air, and perhaps heavier than water, and perhaps equal to earth, etc.

## CAPITOLO XXII.
### *Which water is heavier.*

I say that the water of the sea and of <term>turbid</term> <note>original: "torbidi," meaning muddy or clouded with sediment.</note> rivers is heavier than other waters, and this happens because sea water is mixed with the weight of salt, and the water of turbid rivers is mixed with the silt of the earth; and from this it follows that sea water and turbid river water offer more resistance to the weights they carry <note>Meaning they are more buoyant and can support heavier loads.</note>. But sea water resists more because the weight of the salt, which is mixed and <term>dissolved</term> within it, is inseparable from it without heat to evaporate the water; however, the silt in water is separated by heat and by the stillness of that water, etc.

## CAPITOLO XXIII.
### *Where earth weighs more, under water or exposed.*

If that heavy body weighs more (fig. 10. plate I.) which is in a lighter medium, according to the 21st [chapter]; then the earth that is covered by air is heavier than—

<summary>Leonardo argues that the Earth would be a perfect sphere if water were allowed to cover it entirely, and explains how the weight of an element (gravity) changes depending on the density of its surrounding medium.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s investigation into the physics of equilibrium and fluid dynamics. Building on the previous page's discussion of how an object's weight changes based on its medium (air vs. water), Leonardo now applies these principles to the geometry of the Earth and the behavior of rising air bubbles versus sinking weights.</meta>

LEONARDO DA VINCI

...that part which is covered by water, given that water is <term>heavier</term> <note>original: "più grave." In this context, Leonardo uses gravity to mean density and the downward pressure an element exerts.</note> than air; therefore, if a pyramid were placed with its center of gravity at the <term>center of the World</term> <note>original: "centro del Mondo." In the Aristotelian worldview Leonardo worked within, the center of the Earth was considered the center of the universe and the natural destination for all "heavy" things.</note>, I say that its center of gravity would shift if it were then covered by the sphere of water. I give an example with two equal and similar columnar weights, one of which is half-submerged in water and the other entirely submerged; I say that the one remaining half out of the water is heavier, as has been proven.

### CHAPTER XXIV.

*On the descent of the heavy, and the elevation of the light in water.*

In <term>flexible elements</term> <note>original: "elementi flessibili." Leonardo refers to fluids, specifically air and water, which can change shape to accommodate moving bodies.</note>, the descent of a heavy body through a lighter one always occurs along the shortest possible line [a straight line]. However, the elevation of a light flexible element through a heavier one occurs along a long and <term>winding</term> <note>original: "revertiginosa." Leonardo is describing the spiral or zigzag path an air bubble takes as it rises through water.</note> line. Experience teaches the cause of the first proposition; for in the fall of a heavy body through the air, one sees no resistance that prevents it; rather, the air opens and makes way for the impetus of the heavy body (fig. 11. plate II).

The reason for the second is: let **A** (fig. 12. plate II) be air rising from the bottom of the sea, which would wish to go by the shortest line to the surface of that water; but the weight of the water above it wishes to descend, and the water at the sides also wishes to fill that space. Each of these forces is of equal power—that is, the lateral waters are of equal height—and therefore the air goes winding about, fleeing from the waters pressing from above and from the sides, as seen in the first figure. But if one of the lateral sides is higher, it follows that the higher side, which corresponds to the area above the center of the enclosed air, is the one that weighs more; thus, that air always goes thinning itself out into various shapes, fleeing from the heavier water. And because in the second figure the water **C F G H** presses the air **C F G** more than the water **A C D F** or the water **A B C F**, it will therefore move toward **A C F**, and so on.

### CHAPTER XXV.

*How the earth will remain covered by water.*

Every heavy thing tends toward the bottom, and high things will not remain at their height, but with time all will descend; and thus, with time, the World will remain spherical, and consequently will be entirely covered by waters, and the <term>subterranean veins</term> <note>original: "vene sotterranee." Leonardo often compared the Earth to a living body, viewing underground water systems as "veins" that circulated moisture like blood.</note> will remain motionless, etc.

<vocab>gravity, pyramid, descent, elevation, flexible elements, sea, spherical, waters, center of the World</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains why air bubbles rise in a spiral path through water due to competing pressures and predicts that erosion will eventually result in a perfectly spherical Earth entirely submerged in water.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s exploration of fluid dynamics, moving from the buoyancy of objects to the fundamental physical differences between air and water as mediums. He focuses on compressibility and the mechanics of motion within these two elements.</meta>

LEONARDO DA VINCI

### CHAPTER XXVI.

*What difference there is between the properties of water and those of air.*

Air <term>condenses</term> <note>original: "si condensa." Leonardo observes that air is compressible, meaning it can be squeezed into a smaller space by a moving object.</note> in front of bodies that penetrate it with speed, becoming more or less dense depending on whether the speed is of greater or lesser fury.

Water, in itself, is neither <term>compressible</term> nor <term>rarefiable</term> <note>original: "condensabile, nè rarefabile." Modern physics confirms that water is nearly incompressible, unlike air.</note>; there is as much water in front of the fish that penetrates it as there is behind it; it opens in front of the one penetrating it exactly as much as is reserved behind such a penetrator. And the <term>impetus</term> <note>original: "impeto." A pre-Newtonian term for the force or momentum an object carries through its motion.</note> of a fish is of shorter life than that of a bird in the air, even though the muscles of the fish are very powerful relative to their size—for a fish is almost entirely muscle. This is necessary for it to exist in a body [water] that is much denser than air.

But even though water is not in itself compressible, it has the nature to acquire <term>gravity</term> and <term>levity</term> <note>original: "gravità e levità." Here, Leonardo refers to the downward weight of a falling wave and the upward force of rising water.</note>. It acquires gravity during the destruction of the impetus that lifts it into the air during the creation of a wave, and it acquires levity during the creation of the impetus that lightens the water and moves it against the natural course of heavy things.

But air condenses by itself, as has been said; this is demonstrated in the breadth of sunbeams. If the wind moves the <term>atoms</term> <note>original: "atomi." Leonardo uses "atoms" to describe the tiny motes of dust visible in a shaft of light.</note> within them through various swirlings, you will see such atoms compose themselves into shimmering waves, like the patterns found in tubes or <term>moiré fabric</term> <note>original: "ciambellotto." This was a type of fine cloth, often made of mohair, known for its wavy, watered appearance.</note>, etc.

---

### CHAPTER XXVII.

*On the motion of water.*

Water of equal height has no motion of its own. However, on a <term>convexity</term> <note>original: "convessa." Leonardo is likely referring to a curved or sloped surface where the water's surface height becomes uneven.</note>, that water which is of unequal height will have motion, with greater or lesser speed depending on whether the inequality is of greater or lesser difference; this is made manifest by the fourth proposition. Here, the adversary says that if water pours out through the bottom, the surface will be of equal height and yet it will move. He is contradicted by the thirteenth proposition of the 8th book, which says that the surface of the water which pours through the bottom will be lower the closer it is to the <term>perpendicular</term> of its pouring, etc.

<summary>Leonardo distinguishes between air, which is compressible, and water, which is not, while explaining how water moves based on differences in surface height.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, compressibility, air vs water, impetus, gravity and levity, wave motion, moiré patterns, water flow, equilibrium</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the physical properties of air and water to the specific mechanics of tides (flow and ebb). Leonardo applies his observations on water pressure and momentum to explain why oceans rise and fall, and how these movements affect the Earth’s balance.</meta>

LEONARDO DA VINCI

## CAPITOLO XXVIII.

->*On the flow and ebb of water.*<-

Every motion of water creates a flow and ebb <term>flow and ebb</term> <note>original: "flusso e riflusso." This refers to the tidal cycle of rising and falling water levels.</note> in every part of it where the speed of its course is slowed. This is proven because where the water’s course is more rapid and faster, [it clears away], and where it is flatter, it slows down. Therefore, a flat basin receives more water than it discharges. For this reason, it is necessary that the water in such a basin piles up to such a height that its weight overcomes the water pushing it; then, the water pushed by such water descends from its height around the base of the aforementioned mound; and that part which descends against the said current blocks the torrent in such a way that the upper water of the same current is delayed until the succeeding water overcomes the wave and generates a new ebb.

---

## CAPITOLO XXIX.

->*On the same subject.*<-

The sea under the <term>equator</term> <note>original: "equinoziale." The equinoctial line, where the sun's heat is most intense.</note> rises due to the heat of the sun and takes motion from every part of the mound—or rather, the part of the water—which rises to level out and restore the perfection of its <term>sphere</term> <note>Leonardo views the Earth and its waters as a perfect sphere; any local rising of water must be balanced by movement elsewhere to maintain this shape.</note>; this cannot happen, as stated above, without flow and ebb.

---

## CAPITOLO XXX.

->*That the flow and ebb of the sea moves the elements from their own center.*<-

The flow and ebb of the sea continuously moves the earth with all the elements from the center of the elements. This is proven because although the <term>center of the world</term> <note>original: "centro del mondo." In Leonardo's geocentric context, this means the center of the Earth, which was considered the center of the universe. He is arguing that the Earth's center of gravity shifts.</note> is immobile in itself, nevertheless, the site where it is found is always in motion due to various aspects. The site of the center of the World changes with two mutations, one of which has a slower motion than the other. One varies every six hours, and the other is made over many thousands of years. The six-hour movement arises from the flow and ebb of the sea; the other derives from the wearing away of mountains by the movements of water, born from rains and the continuous course of rivers. Thus, the site of the center of the World changes, and not the center to the site, because such a center is...

<vocab>flow (flusso), ebb (riflusso), basin/sea (pelago), equator (equinoziale), center of the world (centro del mondo), mutations/changes (mutazioni)</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains the mechanics of tides as a cycle of water slowing down and piling up due to its own weight. He further theorizes that both these daily tidal shifts and long-term geological erosion constantly change the Earth's center of gravity.</summary>
<keywords>tides, flow and ebb, fluid dynamics, equator, center of gravity, erosion, hydrology, planetary motion</keywords>

...is immobile, and the location moves continuously in a <term>rectilinear motion</term> <note>original: "moto rettilineo." Movement in a straight line, as opposed to a curved path.</note>, and it will never be curvilinear.

### CHAPTER XXXI.

#### *That water has no weight within water.*

No part of an <term>element</term> <note>Leonardo refers to the four classical elements: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire. Here, he explains that a substance does not exert weight when surrounded by itself.</note> has weight within its own element, unless it is thrust into it with force, or if it were to fall back into it after being extracted into another element. What has been said occurs because each part of the running water exerts less weight upon its bed than the length of its course exerts upon the object it strikes. This is proven in (Fig. 13, Plate III). Since, regarding the weight of the water AB, it would only be at B in proportion to the space AB; but because the element does not weigh within itself, and weighs even less upon something heavier than itself, we shall conclude that such water does not wear away its bed because of its <term>gravity</term> <note>original: "gravità." The natural tendency of heavy objects to move toward the center of the world.</note> tending toward the center of the world, but rather by as much as it enters the sphere of the air. This would be like the course AD, which had entered with its upper part into the sphere of air as far as line EA in space NA, and the length DE as far as DM; therefore, the entire course DE is taken as that much water on the bed D, as would be a similar thickness of water extending from F to D. Since the weight of such water at D is in continuous motion, it continually lifts and leaps upward, and then falls back into DG.

### CHAPTER XXXII.

#### *Why lagoons are formed so close to the sea, and why they flow into the sea through such a narrow channel.*

The <term>storms of the sea</term> <note>original: "fortune del mare." Specifically referring to the violent surges or tempests that displace coastal materials.</note> cast a great quantity of sand onto the shore, which rises up all along that shore, as much over the mouth of the lagoon as elsewhere. Once the storm has ceased, the mouth of the lagoon remains closed by the aforementioned material cast up by the sea. Then, the water that the lagoon receives from surrounding rivers, finding no further exit, begins to rise and gain weight and power. Thus, it either breaks through the embankment interposed between itself and the sea, or it overflows the top. With its pouring out, it wears away as much of that embankment as it touches; and this flow continues until it clears away all that material which prohibited its necessary discharge. It wears away nothing more than what is necessary; and in the beginning, it widens the opening considerably, because...

<summary>Leonardo explains that water is weightless when submerged in water and attributes the erosion of riverbeds to momentum rather than static weight. He also describes how coastal lagoons are formed by storm-driven sandbars and how river pressure eventually carves narrow drainage channels back to the sea.</summary>

...the water that pours over the lock is thin, and at the end, the course of such water narrows, because that course has become more <term>heavy</term> <note>original: "grave." And this is the reason why the exit of ponds into the sea is always narrow.

## CHAPTER XXXIII.

**Whether air escapes from under the water by its own nature, or by being squeezed and driven out by the water.**

The answer is that because a <term>heavy thing</term> <note>original: "il grave." Leonardo is applying the Aristotelian principle that heavier elements (water/earth) naturally displace lighter ones (air/fire) to reach their "natural place."</note> cannot be supported by a light one, that heavy thing will fall, seeking someone to support it. Because every natural action seeks its rest, that water which is above the air, and which surrounds such air on its sides, finds itself entirely founded upon the air enclosed within it. All the water that is above it (Fig. 14, Plate III) **DENM** pushes that air downward and would keep it below, were it not that the lateral water **ABEF** and **ABCD**—which surrounds the air and rests at its sides—becomes greater in weight than the water above it. Consequently, the air escapes by passing through the angles **N** and **M**, or from one side or the other, and it goes winding in its elevation through the water. From this it follows that the air which sometimes emerges from the bottom of marshes in the form of <term>bubbles</term> <note>original: "sonagli," literally "little bells." Leonardo uses this charming term to describe the spherical shape of bubbles rising through liquid.</note> comes to the surface of that water with a sinuous and curvilinear motion, etc.

## CHAPTER XXXIV.

**Of air under the water.**

If you take a <term>bellows</term> <note>original: "mantice." A device used to blow air, usually into a fire. Leonardo uses it here as a tool for pneumatic experiments.</note> with a long pipe (Fig. 15, Plate III) and with such a bellows you push air all the way to the bottom of the water, all the power that such compressed air exerts in returning to its own <term>element</term> <note>The "sphere of air" located above the water.</note> shall be equal to the weight or force that presses the bellows. And if the weight was more excessive than was appropriate for such a submersion of air, the fury of that air becomes more powerful and lifts more weight, etc.

## CHAPTER XXXV.

**In what manner water can be supported by air, being divided and separated from it.**

I want to show you in what manner water can be supported by air, being divided and separated from it. Certainly, if you have in you...

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s exploration of buoyancy and hydrostatics. Following the previous discussion on air bubbles escaping water, he now proposes a thought experiment involving an airtight skin at the bottom of a well and then moves into calculating the equilibrium of elements (air, water, earth) and the geometric nature of "level" surfaces on a spherical Earth.</meta>

<page-num>288</page-num>
<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

...reason, I see that you will not deny me that if there were a <term>skin</term> <note>original: "baga," referring here to a leather bag or bladder filled with air.</note> (Fig. 16, Plate 3) at the bottom of a well's water, and this skin touched all the sides of the well in such a way that water could not pass beneath it; if this skin were full of air, it would exert no less force to reach the surface of the water and reunite with the rest of the air, than the water exerts in its desire to touch the bottom of the well. And if this skin seeks to go upward, it will push up the water placed above it; by lifting that water, it relieves the pressure on the bottom of the well, so that by this reasoning, the well could almost exist without a bottom, etc.

### CHAPTER XXXVI.

->*I want the air at the level of the water.*<-

I have air that weighs two, water that weighs four, and earth that weighs eight. Now, I want the air and the earth to remain at the level of the water; and this cannot happen unless such a mixture is made equal to the weight of the water. Therefore, since the air weighs two less than the water, and the earth weighs four more than the water, I will double the quantity of the air. I will then have four parts of <term>levity</term> <note>original: "levità," the Renaissance concept of "lightness" as a positive force acting opposite to gravity.</note> more than the water, and the earth will have four parts of <term>gravity</term> <note>original: "gravità," or downward weight.</note> more than the water. We shall say, then, that in this case the air is as much lighter than the water as the earth is heavier; by this means, I will create a mixture equal to the weight of the water, because I will cancel out the lightness of the air and the weight of the earth—that is, the excesses they possess in levity and gravity compared to the water. By removing these excesses, there remains a medium equal to the weight of the water; therefore, the surface of the air and the water shall be at a level, etc.

### CHAPTER XXXVII.

->*How water does not rise back to the level from which it departs.*<-

Every <term>level line</term> <note>original: "linea livellaria." Leonardo is distinguishing between a straight line (tangent) and a "level" line that follows the Earth's curve.</note> (Fig. 17, Plate 3) begins above the perpendicular line that stands toward the <term>center of the world</term> <note>original: "centro del mondo," meaning the center of the Earth.</note> in such a way that the level line and the central line intersect and meet at a right angle. Because of this, it is necessary that the level line proceeds in a continuous <term>rectitude</term> <note>original: "rettitudine," or a straight, linear path.</note>; however, the further it extends from its beginning, the more distant it becomes from the center of the world. In this way, if you wish to say that water which descends can rise by itself to the "level" height from which it started, you are mistaken. Now see below: the water that you call "flat" actually moves three miles outside its level over a fourth part of the world, and 350 miles over a distance of 5,000 or 6,000 miles of a direct line, etc.

<vocab>skin (baga), levity (levità), gravity (gravità), level line (linea livellaria), center of the world (centro del mondo), rectitude (rettitudine)</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explores the physics of buoyancy through a thought experiment with an air-filled bladder and calculates how to balance air and earth to achieve neutral buoyancy in water. He also notes that because the Earth is a sphere, a perfectly straight line eventually moves "upward" away from the center, meaning water cannot follow a straight "level" line over long distances.</summary>

### CHAPTER XXXVIII.

->*Of the veins of water upon the lines of the mountains.*<-

It appears clearly that the entire surface of the Ocean, when it is not agitated by a storm, is at an equal distance from the center of the earth, and that the summits of the mountains are as much farther from that center as they rise above the surface of that sea. Therefore, if the body of the earth did not have a similarity to the human body, as is proven below, it would be impossible for the water of the sea—being so much lower than the mountains—to be able by its own nature to rise to the summit of those mountains. Whence it is to be believed that the same cause that holds the blood at the top of a man's head, also holds the water at the summits of the mountains, etc.

### CHAPTER XXXIX.

->*That the machine of this earth has a similarity to man.*<-

Man was called by the ancients a <term>microcosm</term> <note>original: "mondo minore," literally "lesser world." This was a common Renaissance belief that the human body mirrored the structure and functions of the universe.</note>; and certainly the use of this name is well placed; because, just as man is composed of earth, water, and fire, this body of the world-machine is similar. If man has within himself bones as supporters and framework for the flesh, the world has stones as supporters of the earth; if man has within himself the lake of blood, where the lungs increase and decrease in breathing the body, the earth has its Ocean sea, which also rises and falls every six hours to <term>breathe for the world</term> <note>original: "alitare il mondo." Leonardo is comparing the tides of the ocean to the rhythmic breathing of a living being.</note>. If from the said lake of blood there derive veins that go branching through the human body, similarly the Ocean sea fills the body of the earth with infinite veins of water, etc.

### CHAPTER XL.

->*Of the cause why water is drawn to the top of mountains.*<-

Heat is the cause that water is drawn to the highest summits of the mountains. This is proven; because it is seen in effect that the heat of the element of fire always draws to itself moist vapors, thick mists, and dense clouds, which it pulls from the seas and other marshes, rivers, and damp valleys. Drawing those little by little even up to the cold region, the first part stops there, because heat and moisture do not agree with cold and dryness; hence, the first part having stopped there, it then...

<summary>Leonardo argues that the Earth functions like a living organism (a microcosm), comparing mountains to bones and the tides to breathing, to explain how water defies gravity to reach mountain peaks.</summary>

...[they] arrange the other parts; and thus, adding part to part, they become thick and dark clouds, and often they are moved and carried by winds from one region to another, where because of their density they create such a heavy weight that they fall as thick rain. And if the heat of the sun increases toward the power of the <term>element</term> <note>Leonardo refers to the "element of fire," which in the Aristotelian worldview sat above the air; he believes this heat draws moisture upward.</note>, the clouds will be pulled higher and find more cold, in which they freeze, and cause stormy hail. Now that same heat, which holds such a great weight of water—as one sees raining from clouds—lifts it from below to the heights from the base of the mountains, and carries it; and it holds it within the peaks of the mountains, which, finding some fissure, ascends there continuously, causing the rivers etc.

### CHAPTER XLI.
->*On the same subject.*<-

I say that just as natural heat pulls the blood in the veins to the top of a person, and when a person is dead this cold blood settles in the low places; and when the sun heats a person's head, the blood <term>multiplies</term> <note>original: "moltiplica." Leonardo likely means the blood increases in volume or pressure through heat expansion.</note> and so much blood arrives that, forcing the veins, it often generates a headache. Similarly, the veins branch through the body of the earth, and through the natural heat that is spread everywhere, the water stays in the veins raised to the high peaks of the mountains. Also the heat of the element of fire, the day, and the heat of the sun have the power to awaken the moisture of the low places of the mountains, and pull it high in the same way that it pulls the clouds and awakens their moisture from the sea; and as an experiment of this, if you take the instrument R F (fig. 18. plate 4) and heat the water from above, it will depart from R F and exit through A etc.

### CHAPTER XLII.
->*On the drop of water.*<-

A drop is that which does not detach from other water unless the power of its weight is greater than the power of the <term>attachment</term> <note>original: "collegazione." Leonardo is observing the physical property we now call surface tension or cohesion.</note> it has with the water to which it is joined.

That drop is generated more slowly which has a slower motion of water at its creation etc.
