<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

## CHAPTER XLIII.

### *On the sphericity of the droplet.*

The droplet will be of more perfect sphericity when it is of a smaller quantity. This is because if two spherical liquids of unequal quantities come into contact with one another, the larger draws the smaller to itself and immediately incorporates it, without destroying the perfection of its sphericity. This is a difficult proposition; but for this reason, I will not refrain from stating my opinion. Water, <term>clothed</term> <note>Leonardo uses the term "vestita" (clothed) to describe how the air surrounds the water, creating a boundary we would today call surface tension.</note> by the air, naturally desires to remain united in its sphere, because in such a state it is deprived of gravity. This gravity is twofold: first, that its whole has gravity in relation to the center of the elements; and second, the gravity in relation to the center of the water's own sphericity. If this were not so, the water would only form a half-sphere, which is the part from the center upward.

But I do not see a way for the human intellect to provide a scientific explanation for this, other than to say—as is said of the <term>magnet</term> <note>original: "calamita." In Leonardo’s time, the magnet or lodestone was the primary example of a "hidden force" acting at a distance.</note> that draws iron—that such a virtue is a <term>hidden property</term> <note>original: "occulta proprietà." This does not mean "magical," but rather a natural force whose mechanism is not yet visible or understood.</note>, of which there are infinite examples in nature.

But we shall ask: why is there more perfection in the smallest sphere of liquid than in the large one? To this, it is answered that the smallest drop has a lightness more similar to the air that surrounds it than the large droplet has; and because of this slight difference, it is supported more by the air from the middle downward than the large one. And as proof of this, one may cite the smallest drops, which are of such a minute figure that they are almost invisible on their own. Yet many, in great quantity, are visible; and these are the constituent particles of clouds, mists, rains, etc.

## CHAPTER XLIV.

### *How rains are generated.*

The air interposed between the <term>sphere of fire</term> <note>In the Aristotelian worldview, the "sphere of fire" was the highest layer of the atmosphere, sitting above the air.</note> and the water participates in both water and fire; but it participates more in one than the other according to its proximity to each. It follows that it participates less in each as it becomes more remote from them, which removal occurs in the middle region of the air. Therefore, this middle region is of the highest degree of coldness. Because of this, it follows that the part of the cloud that most approaches this middle region will be the coldest.

Thus, the heat of the sphere of fire—which acts as an attractor to such a cloud—is of lesser power there. Because of this, it follows that the movements of the <term>moist particles</term> <note>original: "granicoli umidi." Literally "moist little grains." Leonardo is conceptualizing water vapor as tiny individual units of matter.</note> composed of the matter of the clouds are of a slower motion. And for this reason, in the elevation of these moist particles, those which rise highest toward the vicinity of such...

<summary>Leonardo investigates the physics of water droplets, attributing their spherical shape to a "hidden property" similar to magnetism, and explains how coldness in the middle atmosphere slows the movement of water particles to form clouds.</summary>
<keywords>surface tension, droplets, magnetism, atmospheric layers, gravity, sphere of fire, cloud formation, moist particles</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s investigation into the mechanics of water in the atmosphere. Having previously discussed why water forms into spherical droplets due to "hidden properties" (surface tension), he now explores how these droplets grow through accretion, fall as rain, and how they behave internally as they move through the air.</meta>

the region in the middle is more delayed, and that which follows it is faster than it, whereby it catches up to it, and it often happens that it strikes it from below and incorporates into it; thus its quantity and weight increase. Because of this, the air—unable to support it—gives way to its descent, which strikes all the droplets that impede the motion of the descent, and incorporates many of them into itself. By acquiring heaviness, it acquires velocity of descent; for which reason, once it has penetrated its entire cloud, at every stage of its descent, it will acquire a degree of diminution <note>Leonardo likely refers to the droplet losing mass to evaporation or breaking apart, though he notes that often "these small grains will not reach the ground."</note>, and many will be the times that such small grains do not conduct themselves to the earth. But if such small grains in the highest part of their height acquire so much gravity that their weight generates a fast descent, without doubt such motion will increase their size, since such velocity will catch up to the droplets that descend below them and will incorporate them into itself; and this will be the increase of weight in every stage of its descent, etc.

### CHAPTER XLV.

#### On water falling from the cloud.

The water that falls from the cloud is sometimes resolved into such lightness by the <term>friction</term> <note>original: "confregazione." Leonardo views air as a fluid medium that exerts resistance and "rubs" against falling bodies.</note> it has with the air, that it cannot divide the air and is converted into that same air; at other times, however, it multiplies in descending as has been said, because it finds minute particles of water which, by their lightness, were slow in their descent, and it incorporates with them, and in every part of its descent it acquires a quantity of water. Sometimes the winds bend the rain and make the descent oblique, whereby for such cause the descent becomes slow and long, and it is often converted into such minute particles that it cannot descend, and thus remains within the air, etc.

### CHAPTER XLVI.

#### On the motion of the droplet within the air.

The sides of the droplet that descends through the air move in a motion contrary to that of the droplet, and they create a continuous circular wave from the edges to the middle of its upper part; and such a wave does not reflect in the middle of the circumference, but <term>submerges</term> <note>Leonardo is describing the internal circulation of a fluid within a falling drop—a sophisticated observation of fluid dynamics where the external air friction causes the water inside the drop to circulate in a vortex-like motion.</note> in the middle of its circle, and dives deep, and emerges from the bottom part, and returns up to the highest part from where it first descended, and there it regenerates the circular wave again, and again submerges in the middle of it, etc.

<summary>Leonardo describes how raindrops grow by merging with smaller droplets during their fall (accretion) and notes how air resistance can cause rain to evaporate or stay suspended. He also provides a remarkably advanced description of the internal vortex-like circulation of water within a falling droplet.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s observations on the physics of fluids. Having previously discussed the formation and growth of raindrops within clouds, he now moves to the mechanics of their collision, the internal distribution of weight within a single drop, and the behavior of water when contained in a vessel subject to movement.</meta>

LEONARDO DA VINCI

## CHAPTER XLVII.

->*On the motion of droplets among themselves.*<-

The descent of droplets that collide with each other without wind will not be straight, but will be angled. This is proven because if two bodies collide within the air, the one that is smaller in quantity will be moved further out of its straight path. And if two different weights of dew or <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "argento vivo." Leonardo frequently compares water to mercury (quicksilver) to study surface tension and fluid weight.</note> join together, both will be moved from their original positions, and the proportion of their movements will be similar to the proportion of their size, etc.

## CHAPTER XLVIII.

->*On the weight of the droplet.*<-

In every thing, the part retains within itself the nature of the whole. If a droplet is placed upon a <term>horizontal plane</term> <note>original: "piano equigiacente," literally an "equally-lying" or level surface.</note>, its parts will be equal in weight around the center of its base, like droplet A (**fig. 19. plate 4**); but if the plane were slanted, you will find that just as much as one end is higher than the center of its base compared to the horizontal, so much more weight it acquires on the side beyond the center.

## CHAPTER XLIX.

->*On water placed in a vessel, and its motion.*<-

Water placed in an <term>open vessel</term> <note>original: "vaso spanso." This suggests a wide-mouthed or shallow container where the surface area of the water is significantly exposed to the air.</note> appears to have a firmer contact with the air than with the bottom of the vessel, both in the circular motion of that vessel and in its straight motion. The proof of this is seen in the sudden movement of the vessel, because a sudden wave is then generated in the water contained within it; and the reason for this is that the water does not have weight upon its bottom in the same way it has at the contact point with the air, where it finds more resistance than at the contact with the bottom of such a vessel. For this reason, it creates a wave during the sudden movement of the vessel, because it is held back more by the air than by the bottom, etc.

## CHAPTER L.

->*On water in the vessel, and its capacity.*<-

Among vessels of equal air capacity, that which descends to the <term>lowest depth</term> <note>original: "infima bassezza." Leonardo may be suggesting that atmospheric pressure or the density of the medium at lower elevations affects how much liquid a vessel can hold or how it is displaced.</note> will receive within itself a greater quantity of water.

<note>Vocabulary used in this section: droplets (gocciole), dew (rugiada), quicksilver (argento vivo), horizontal plane (equigiacente), open vessel (vaso spanso), lowest depth (infima bassezza), circular motion (moto circolare).</note>

<summary>Leonardo examines the physics of fluid displacement and surface tension, noting how colliding droplets deflect based on mass and how air resistance affects the surface of water in a moving container.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, droplets, quicksilver, surface tension, air resistance, atmospheric pressure, momentum, vessels</keywords>

### CHAPTER V.

->*What is the circumvoluble motion of water.*<-

<term>Circumvoluble motion</term> <note>original: "moto circonvolubile." This refers to a revolving or swirling motion, essentially a vortex.</note> is that which is caused by the reflected motion as it meets the nearby water; by striking it, the water begins to whirl around within itself, as seen in the first figure (**fig. 22. plate 5**). The water BH, reflected from B toward H, becomes circumvoluble due to the percussion coming from the incident water OH; and the reflections BL and BM do the same; and in the second figure, the waves EV and EX.

### CHAPTER VI.

->*On straight circumvoluble motion.*<-

Here the two courses of water meet together at line AB (**fig. 23. plate 5**) and, from such percussion, they go about swirling between each other and through their whole depth; because they strike each other from the surface to the bottom. Once such a revolution is created, it is pushed out from the site where it was formed by the <term>impetus</term> <note>Leonardo uses "impeto" to describe the momentum or driving force behind moving water.</note> of the succeeding waters. In such a movement, this revolution has acquired two motions: first, its natural circumvoluble motion around its center; and second, the motion it acquires from moving from place to place by the shortest path. Therefore, this shall be called "straight circumvoluble motion."

### CHAPTER VII.

->*On the motion of running water.*<-

Running water contains within itself infinite motions, some greater and some smaller than its principal course. This is proven by the objects held between the <term>two waters</term> <note>original: "infra le due acque." This likely refers to objects suspended mid-stream, between the surface and the bed, or between two different currents.</note>; and the true motions of the water that carries them are clearly demonstrated in clear water. This is because sometimes the fall of the wave toward the bottom carries objects with it to the percussion of that bottom, and would reflect them back with it to the surface of the water if the floating body were spherical; but many times it does not bring it back because it may be wider, or more...

<summary>Leonardo defines and classifies the swirling motions of water (vortices), explaining how they are formed by the collision of currents and how they can simultaneously rotate and move forward. He also notes that running water is composed of many internal, secondary currents rather than a single uniform flow.</summary>

...narrower in one direction than the other, and its uniformity is struck on its wider side by another reflected wave, which goes about turning that <term>moving object</term> <note>original: "mobile." In Leonardo's physics, this refers to any body or portion of matter in motion, such as a floating seed or a specific volume of water.</note>, which moves only as much as it is carried; this motion is sometimes fast, sometimes slow, and sometimes it turns to the right, and sometimes to the left, now turning upwards, now downwards, spinning within itself, now in one direction, now in another, obeying all the forces that move it, and in the battles waged by such <term>motors</term> <note>original: "motori." ### ->CHAPTER VIII.<-

->*That the incident motion of water is more powerful than the reflected motion.*<-

The motion of an incident moving object will be more powerful than its reflected motion, and for this reason the incident motion of water will be more powerful than its reflection. This is because the <term>percussion</term> <note>original: "percussione." The impact or collision of the water against a solid surface.</note> of the incident [water] against the dense object of the bank or the bottom diminishes part of the <term>impetus</term> <note>original: "impeto." For Leonardo, impetus is the "impressed force" that keeps an object moving after the initial push has ceased.</note> joined to that moving object; this diminution does not leave the reflected motion as powerful as the aforementioned incident motion. And although in every degree of motion the impetus of the moving object diminishes by itself without hitting a dense object, this does not mean that such a percussion does not diminish it much more. For if you measure the motion that such a moving object would have made without <term>incidence</term> <note>original: "incidenza." The act of striking a surface.</note>, and measure the motion born from many incidences, the motion continued toward the same place will be longer than that which is interrupted so many times by the incident [impact], even if at the beginning of the impetus of each they were of equal power. Therefore, let AB (**fig. 24. plate 5**) be the incident motion, and BGR be the reflected motion; I say that motion AB is more powerful than motion BGR; both because the impetus of motion AB is diminished by the percussion it makes at B, where the reflected motion BG is generated, and then the reflected motion GR is much more diminished by the second percussion; and also because, assuming that the impetus of the reflected motion BGR at its start B is of equal power with the impetus of the incident motion AB at its start A, if you measure both in a given time, you will see that motion AB results in being longer than motion BGR.

### ->CHAPTER IX.<-

->*That water always ends its course along the line of incidence.*<-

Every moving object that generates a reflection ends its course along the line of incidence, and this occurs because of the previous rule which states that motion...

LEONARDO DA VINCI 298

...incident motion is of greater power than reflected motion, and that which is more powerful has more durability than that which is less powerful.

### CHAPTER X.

->On incident and reflected motion.<-

Every stream of water that is struck against some object divides into four various and principal motions, namely: right and left, upward and downward. The downward motion strikes the bottom and damages it, etc.

### CHAPTER XI.

->On the same.<-

Of the four principal motions made by water as it divides during its reflection, that which reflects at the most <term>acute angle</term> <note>An angle of less than 90 degrees; a "sharp" turn.</note> will be the fastest. This occurs because where the angle is more acute, the water rises higher, and where it rises higher it acquires greater power, and consequently greater velocity. A is the acute angle, B is the <term>obtuse</term> <note>An angle greater than 90 degrees; a "wide" turn.</note> angle, etc. (fig. 25. plate 6).

### CHAPTER XII.

->On the same.<-

<term>Incident motion</term> <note>original: "moto incidente." The initial movement of the water before it hits an obstacle.</note> does not transform into <term>reflected motion</term> <note>original: "moto riflesso." The movement of water bouncing back or away after hitting an obstacle.</note> without the <term>percussion</term> <note>The physical impact or "strike" of the water against a solid surface.</note> of the bottom or of the riverbank. This is demonstrated by the fourth and tenth propositions of this treatise.

### CHAPTER XIII.

->On the same.<-

After the reflected water reaches its maximum height, the beginning of incident motion is generated. This occurs because after the maximum height of the reflected water, the <term>impetus</term> <note>The momentum or "stored force" that keeps the water moving against gravity.</note> of the reflected motion ceases; incident motion is then generated according to the ninth proposition of this work, which states that every <term>moving object</term> <note>original: "mobile." Any body or mass in motion.</note> that generates reflection ends its course along the line of incidence, etc.

<summary>Leonardo examines how water behaves when it hits an obstacle, noting that it splits into four directions and explaining how the angle of impact determines the height and speed of the resulting splash.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, incident motion, reflected motion, percussion, acute angles, river engineering, hydraulics</keywords>

LEONARDO DA VINCI 299

## CHAPTER XIV.
### *On the power of reflected motion.*

Reflected motion will be so much weaker as it is shorter. That reflected motion will be shorter which is caused by a greater <term>percussion</term> <note>original: "percussione." The physical impact or strike of the water against a solid surface.</note>. That percussion will be of greater power which is made between more equal angles <note>Leonardo is observing that a "head-on" strike (where the angles of approach and departure are similar) creates a more violent impact than a glancing one.</note>. And among percussions made between equal angles, that which is caused against a denser <term>object</term> <note>original: "obietto." Here, a solid obstacle or a more compact riverbank.</note> will have greater <term>strength</term> <note>original: "valetudine." This refers to the force, vigor, or "health" of the water's movement.</note>. We shall say then (fig. 26. plate 6) that the reflected motion BC is weaker than the motion EF because it is shorter, being caused by a greater percussion between more equal angles, and because it is more obstructed at the site of the percussion, and more varied from the <term>motion of incidence</term> <note>original: "moto dell’incidenza." The initial path of the water before it hits the bank.</note>; whereby it is perhaps diminished by the power of the first motion, and also because it is crossed by the incident motion of the current.

## CHAPTER XV.
### *On the same subject.*

Reflected motion will have greater strength when it is longer; and it will be longer when it is caused by a weaker percussion; and that percussion will be weaker which is caused between more diverse angles. And this happens because the reflected motion, being longer, is less obstructed, as it varies little from the motion of incidence and percussion; and such percussion is less forceful, whereby it loses little of the power of the first mover. We shall say then that the reflected motion EF (fig. 26. plate 6) is of greater strength than the reflected motion BC; because it loses little of the power of the incident motion DE, which strikes the bank at point E between more unequal angles—that is, between the <term>acute angles</term> <note>Angles of less than 90 degrees; "sharp" angles.</note> HEI and GEF, and the <term>obtuse angle</term> <note>An angle of more than 90 degrees; a "wide" angle.</note> DEF—which makes it less obstructed.

## CHAPTER XVI.
### *On the motion of rising water.*

All water that <term>rises</term> <note>original: "sorge." Referring to an upwelling, a spring, or water bubbling up from below.</note> divides at the surface and flows toward different directions; it does so all the more when the <term>pool</term> <note>original: "pelago." A large body of water, basin, or deep area.</note> is calmer, since it then finds itself equidistant from the center, and such a rising is higher from every perspective.

<summary>Leonardo explains that the "strength" of water bouncing off a bank depends on the angle of impact: glancing blows preserve more energy and travel further, while head-on strikes lose power. He also notes how upwelling water spreads evenly across a calm surface.</summary>
<keywords>reflected motion, percussion, incident motion, fluid dynamics, angles of impact, hydrodynamics, rising water, riverbanks</keywords>

LEONARDO DA VINCI

### CHAPTER XVII.

*On the motions of water both above and below the surface.*

All the motions that occur on the surface of the water are also made at every level of its depth, and similarly across every degree of its width. And you may learn this from the weeds attached to the bottom of the river <note>Leonardo often used the swaying of aquatic plants as a visual tool to track the invisible currents of deep water.</note>. This is because every <term>impetus</term> <note>original: "impeto." In Leonardo’s physics, this is the derived force or momentum transmitted to a body.</note> is consumed by the movement of the moving body over the time and length of the motion made in whatever direction.
The weed that had consumed its impetus and remained slanted is pushed by the <term>wedge of water</term> <note>original: "conio dell’ acqua." Leonardo visualizes the pressure of moving water as a physical wedge or "cone" that exerts force on an object.</note> N M A (fig. 27. plate 6), which drives A from its position and moves it with a reverse impetus toward B; it moves as long as that impetus lasts, and once it is consumed, the wedge of water acts here as it does there, and so it continues as long as the moving bodies are accompanied [by the flow]. And the same occurs from the surface to the bottom, and thus in any direction, etc.

---

### CHAPTER XVIII.

*On the straightness of rivers.*

The water of broken rivers never runs in a straight line, because the river is faster the further its water is from the <term>bank</term> <note>original: "argine." The side or embankment of the river which creates friction.</note>, which is its impediment; and also because after the current has passed, gravel is deposited, and after that another current is generated, the motion of which will direct itself either to the bank, or to the middle, or in as many varied places as there are varied <term>spurs</term> <note>original: "pennelli." In hydraulics, these are usually man-made jetties, but here Leonardo refers to the natural ridges or "hills" of gravel that redirect the water.</note> of the gravel hills left on the bottom after the aforementioned currents, etc.

---

### CHAPTER XIX.

*Height of the river’s course.*

The courses of straight rivers are always higher in the middle of their width than at the sides, and [higher] between the middle of their width and those sides. This arises because, as will be said, between one wave and another is the lowest depth of the water, and the waves are generated by the <term>percussion</term> <note>original: "percussione." The physical impact of the water striking an obstacle.</note> of the water, both in the current and against the bank. Therefore, between the current and the bank is the lowest depth, etc.

<summary>Leonardo explains that water moves consistently at all depths, using the swaying of river weeds to prove deep currents, and observes that rivers naturally curve and vary in height due to friction and gravel deposits.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, river currents, impetus, river banks, gravel deposits, waves, percussion, aquatic plants</keywords>

LEONARDO DA VINCI

### CHAPTER XX.
->*On the course of the river.*<-

Water that descends through a straight river always moves in an oblique course from the middle toward the opposite banks, and from those banks back to the middle of the river. This occurs because of what was previously stated <note>original: "per la passata." This refers to Leonardo's earlier geometric proofs regarding the curvature of water surfaces.</note>; for if the currents of straight rivers are higher in the center than at the sides, necessity dictates that the water must descend and run from the center to the sides, and from the sides back to the center in an oblique path.

### CHAPTER XXI.
->*On the velocity of river water, and its slowness.*<-

In a straight river of equal width, depth, and slope of the bed, the water acquires a degree of velocity for every degree of motion. This is evident through the proportion of motion, by which an object becomes faster the further it moves along its natural course, as we have proven in other subjects <note>Leonardo is applying the laws of falling bodies to the horizontal flow of water, suggesting that gravity causes constant acceleration as the water "falls" down the slope of the riverbed.</note>, etc.

### CHAPTER XXII.
->*On the same.*<-

In a straight river as described above, although the water acquires a degree of velocity with every degree of motion, it nevertheless slows down and becomes less fast the more impediment it finds in its course. This too is evident from what we have proven elsewhere: that a thing moving by nature or by violence <note>In the physics of Leonardo's time, "natural" motion was an object moving to its rightful place (like water falling), while "violent" motion was caused by an external force (like a thrown stone).</note>, no matter how long and fast the motion may be, the more resistance is opposed to its path, the more it tires and the more it slows.

### CHAPTER XXIII.
->*On the same.*<-

The water of straight rivers is faster the more remote it is from the <term>bank</term> <note>original: "argine." The side or embankment of the river.</note>, which is its impediment. This arises from the two points mentioned previously, and is proven by the fourth chapter of this treatise, which says: where <term>incident motion</term> <note>original: "moto incidente." This refers to the primary current striking a surface.</note> is impeded, there <term>reflected motion</term> <note>original: "moto riflesso." The water bouncing back or recoiling after hitting an obstacle.</note> is born. Therefore, if reflected motion is generated at the bank—which, according to the eighth chapter, is weaker than the incident motion—the water will be as much faster as it is further away from the bank, etc.

<summary>Leonardo explains that river water does not flow in a simple straight line but oscillates between the center and the banks, and that while water naturally accelerates as it flows, friction against the banks acts as a constant "impediment" that slows it down.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, river currents, velocity, fluid dynamics, incident motion, reflected motion, friction, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

### CHAPTER XXIV.
->*On the same subject.*<-

In a river with a consistent slope <term>obliquity: the degree of the riverbed's downward incline</term>, the more it widens, the more it slows down. This will be clearly proven in the forty-first proposition of the eighth book, etc.

---

### CHAPTER XXV.
->*On the same subject.*<-

Where the river bank suddenly gains width, a sudden current is generated; this arises because of the sixtieth proposition, which states that water that spreads out also becomes shallower in depth. Because water falls with greater impetus <note>In Leonardo’s physics, "impetus" refers to the force or energy an object possesses due to its motion; here he suggests that as water "falls" from a higher level to a lower, wider level, it gains speed.</note> when it drops to a lower level, it consequently moves with greater velocity.

---

### CHAPTER XXVI.
->*On the same subject.*<-

Where the water becomes fast, its impetus does not allow the eye to discern the features of various riverbeds. The reason for this is that the motion of the water, being successive in every instant, is different from what it was a moment before; for this reason, as soon as the eye attempts to observe a specific movement on the bottom, that movement has already vanished from the spot where the eye is looking, etc.

---

### CHAPTER XXVII.
->*On the same subject.*<-

The motion of the water becomes faster the greater its slope <term>declination: the downward pitch or descent of the river</term>. This arises from the fourth proposition of the first book, which shows that water does not move unless it descends; therefore, it will move with greater force where it has a steeper descent, etc.

---

### CHAPTER XXVIII.
->*On the same subject.*<-

The more turbid <note>original: "torbida." Referring to water filled with sediment, mud, or silt.</note> the water is, the faster it moves, because the heavier it is, the faster it flows. According to the twenty-second proposition of the first book, turbid water is heavier than other types of water <note>Leonardo is applying the Aristotelian idea that heavier objects are more strongly compelled by gravity to reach their "natural place" at the center of the earth, thus moving faster.</note>.

<summary>Leonardo outlines the proportional relationships between a river's speed, its width, and the steepness of its descent, while noting how water density affects velocity.</summary>

### CHAPTER XXIX.
->*On the same subject.*<-

That part of the water will be faster and have greater motion which is most distant from the part that moves the least. This is demonstrated by the nature of the motion of a <term>continual quantity: a philosophical and mathematical concept referring to things that are unbroken or continuous, like a line or a flow of water</term> which, as the philosophers show, participates all the more in the condition of the <note>original: "termine, *ad quem*"</note> point of arrival, the further it is distant from the <note>original: "termine *a quo*"</note> point of departure, and the reverse is also true, etc.

---

### CHAPTER XXX.
->*On the same subject.*<-

A river has a faster course where it has less friction against the bottom—provided the bottom is firm and of uniform width—and conversely, it is slower where it has more friction against the bottom. This is proven because greater impacts against the riverbed cause more <term>vortices: Leonardo uses the word "bollori" (boilings) to describe the turbulent, bubbling motion of water</term> in the water, which delay the current.

---

### CHAPTER XXXI.
->*On the same subject.*<-

Always, both behind obstacles on the bank and behind obstacles in the middle of the water that rise above the surface, the water becomes less swift and is delayed. This happens because—as will be proven in the fourth book—after all the aforementioned obstacles, a <term>swirling reflected motion: a backflow or eddy caused by water hitting an object and turning back on itself</term> is generated, which, according to the eighth proposition of this book, is slower than the <term>incident motion: the primary, oncoming flow of the water</term>, etc.

---

### CHAPTER XXXII.
->*On the same subject.*<-

When rivers are straight, they flow with much greater impetus in the middle of their width than they do at their sides. This is because the water strikes the sides of the rivers with equal impact, and finding a narrower part of the river, it leaps back toward the middle; these waves will create a new impact among themselves. From there, they return again toward the banks equally, and that water of <note>Leonardo often visualized the convergence of forces or flows as "pyramidal" or triangular in shape.</note> pyramidal figure, which is enclosed between the first impact made against the bank and the second...

He describes the physics of water as a "continual quantity" and notes how reflected currents create specific geometric patterns of flow.</summary>

## CHAPTER XXXIII.

->Whether the current is faster above or below.<-

The current is faster above than below. This happens because the water above borders the air, which offers little resistance, being lighter than water; and the water below borders the earth, which offers great resistance, being immobile and heavier than water. Because of this, it follows that the part furthest from the bottom has less resistance than the part below which borders the earth—which is resistant and immobile—and consequently, the water is faster above than below. This is proven by the following rule which states that rivers of equal depth and width, which etc.

## CHAPTER XXXIV.

->On the same subject.<-

Rivers of equal depth and width, which <term>plunge at their terminus: Leonardo is referring to the point where a river reaches a drop-off or a waterfall</term>, run faster above than below. This is demonstrated by the forty-eighth proposition of the first book, which states that if the plane of a <term>drop: Leonardo often uses the "drop" (gocciola) as a fundamental unit to analyze the gravitational behavior of liquid</term> were oblique, the more one extremity is higher than the center of its base compared to the <term>equidistant: original "equigiacente," referring to a level or horizontal plane</term>, the more weight it acquires beyond the center. Therefore, rivers of equal depth and width, plunging at their end, run faster above than below; because at the end of the river, the water above is faster in falling than the water below; wherefore it is necessary that the upper water, which successively rests upon the lower, possesses such motion as was described, etc.

## CHAPTER XXXV.

->Of the power of running water.<-

Running water is more powerful above than below, and this arises from the two preceding chapters, which state that the current is faster above than below, etc. <note>Leonardo correlates "power" (potenza) directly with velocity; since the surface water moves faster due to lack of friction with the riverbed, he concludes it possesses more force.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains that river currents are faster at the surface than at the bottom because air offers less friction than the solid riverbed. He further notes that this increased velocity at the surface results in greater physical power or force.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s exploration of river hydraulics. While the previous page established that water generally moves faster at the surface due to less friction with the air, this section examines exceptions and complexities, specifically how obstacles on the riverbed create turbulence and how external forces like wind can actually reverse the speed gradient, making the water move faster at the bottom than at the surface.</meta>

LEONARDO DA VINCI
305
39

### CHAPTER XXXVI.

*That the current has fewer eddies above than below.*

Waters have more <term>revolutions: Leonardo uses this term to describe "eddies" or "vortices" where water swirls back on itself</term> from the middle of their depth downward than from the middle upward. This is due to the many obstacles found at the bottom of rivers, whereas there are few at the surface, except for those objects resting on the bottom that are tall enough to break through the surface, etc.

### CHAPTER XXXVII.

*Where the current is faster above than below.*

Water that descends with a downward motion over a <term>bumpy bed: original "globuloso fondo," referring to a riverbed made of rounded stones or uneven silt</term> will have a faster flow at the surface than underneath. This is proven by the previous rule, because where the water has greater eddies, it is slowed down more, etc.

### CHAPTER XXXVIII.

*Where the current is faster below than above.*

Rivers that flow against the direction of the winds will have a current that is faster below than above, to the same degree that the surface is slowed down by being pushed by the winds more than before. The reason for this is that, given a river of equal depth and width with an equal flow at the bottom and the surface, it is necessary that the <term>resistance: original "ricalcitrazione," literally a "kicking back"</term> offered by the wind against the surface current makes that surface water turn back; yet it is not enough for these waves to simply rise upward, for in the end, as they fall, they go beneath the other water and reach the bottom. There, they meet the other current and join with it; and because the <term>embankment: original "argine," the riverbank</term> cannot contain this increase in volume, it is necessary for that flow at the bottom to double its speed, otherwise the water would rise far beyond the banks of the river. **A** represents the surface water, and **B** comes to be the water flowing over the surface of the bottom (fig. 29, plate 6).

### CHAPTER XXXIX.

*On the same subject.*

Water which, with a downward course, strikes the flat surface of other water at a diagonal angle, will follow its initial path toward the bottom in a straight line...

<summary>Leonardo analyzes how riverbed obstacles and wind resistance alter the speed of water, noting that wind blowing against a current can force surface water downward, accelerating the flow at the bottom.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, eddies, riverbeds, wind resistance, surface tension, hydraulic volume, Leonardo's observations</keywords>

...line down to the bottom, and by creating a "cover" over the impacted water, it will flow faster below than above. This occurs because with such an inclination it gains speed and power, and continues its initial course with that momentum until that <term>impetus: the force or energy with which a body moves; a central concept in Leonardo's physics</term> is exhausted (fig. 30, plate 6).

### CHAPTER XL.

->*Where the current is of equal speed above and below.*<-

The movement of water between the bottom and the surface will be of equal speed if the bed of that river is straight and uniform in its slope and width. This is demonstrated because where no <term>reflected motion: the secondary movement of water that "bounces" back after hitting an object</term> is generated, the <term>incident motion: the primary forward flow of water</term> is not slowed down; therefore, if the bottom is straight without <term>irregularities: original "globulenze," referring to lumps, mounds, or unevenness on the riverbed</term>, no reflection is generated according to the definition of reflected motion. This definition states: reflected motion is that which arises from the impact of incident motion against specific objects on the bottom or the bank, and consequently it is not slowed, and thus it will be equal in velocity, etc.

### CHAPTER XLI.

->*On the same subject.*<-

A shallow current is just as fast above as it is below; and the reason is that in shallow water, the surface is so close to the bottom that although the water at the bottom might be slow and the surface fast by its nature, nevertheless, through their <term>proximity: original "contiguità," the physical contact between layers of water</term>, the surface shares in the slowness of the bottom, and the water at the bottom shares in the speed of the surface, so that they remain equal in speed, etc.

### CHAPTER XLII.

->*To know whether a body of water flows more below than above.*<-

Take a rod that is threaded through a <term>buoy: original "baga," typically a floating bladder or leather skin</term> at the top and anchored to a stone at the bottom. Regarding the part of the rod that extends above the buoy: if it leans toward the approaching water, the water is flowing faster at the bottom than at the surface. If said rod leans toward where the water is flowing away, the river is flowing faster at the surface than at the bottom; and if the rod remains straight, the course will be of equal speed at the bottom and the surface (fig. 31, plate 7).

<summary>Leonardo explains that a perfectly smooth, straight riverbed creates a uniform current and describes how shallow water equalizes speed through friction. He also details a practical tool—a weighted rod with a float—to visually determine if a river is moving faster at its surface or its bed.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, water velocity, riverbeds, incident motion, reflected motion, fluid dynamics, experimental instruments, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

### CHAPTER XLIII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

If you wish to see where the water is faster—whether in some place above the surface or below the surface—throw colored water mixed with oil onto the running water. Observe at the end of the course which arrives first; that is, if the oil arrives first, the water is running faster above than below. If the dyed water arrives first, the river is running faster below than above.

<note>Leonardo suggests using oil because it is less dense than water and remains on the surface, while "colored water" (likely dyed with a heavy pigment) would mix or sink, acting as a marker for the lower currents.</note>

### CHAPTER XLIV.

->*Rule for judging the movements and courses of rivers.*<-

If you wish to properly judge all the patterns of the movements and courses of water, observe clear water of shallow depth under the rays of the sun. Through the sunlight, you will see all the shadows and all the highlights of the said movements, as well as the <term>submerged objects: original "cose ipostate," referring to things situated or sedimented at the bottom</term> of the water.

<note>Leonardo often used the play of light and shadow on a riverbed to visualize complex fluid dynamics that are otherwise invisible in deep or murky water.</note>

### CHAPTER XLV.

->*Why the movement of water, although it is slower than that of a man, always appears faster.*<-

The reason for this is that if you look at the movement of water, your eye cannot remain fixed, but behaves similarly to things seen in your shadow when you walk. For if the eye attempts to contemplate the quality of the shadow, the <term>bits of straw: original "festuche," referring to small debris or stalks</term>, or other things contained within that shadow, appear to move rapidly; and it will seem that those things are faster at fleeing from the said shadow than the shadow is at walking, etc.

<note>This is a sophisticated observation of relative motion. Leonardo explains that our perception of speed is skewed because our eyes track small floating objects against a moving reference point, making the water seem swifter than its actual physical pace.</note>

->*On the lateral obliquity of rivers.*<-

The <term>lateral obliquity: the side-to-side or diagonal movement of water rather than a perfectly straight forward flow</term> of straight waters is greater or lesser according to the greater or lesser velocities. This is proven by the eighteenth [proposition] of this [work], which says: the water of straight rivers never flows in a straight line; and by the twentieth, which says: water that descends through a straight river always moves in an oblique course; and by the thirty-second, which says: rivers flow, when they are straight, with much greater <term>impetus: the force or momentum of the flow</term> in the

### CHAPTER XLVII.
->*On the motion of rivers.*<-

Water with an uneven bottom creates contrary movements at the surface and at the bed. This arises from the reflected motion of the water striking the inequality of the bottom, etc. <note>Leonardo is describing turbulence; as water hits rocks or rises on the riverbed, it "reflects" upward, creating swirls that move in the opposite direction of the main current.</note>

### CHAPTER XLVIII.
->*On the same subject.*<-

Where the slant of the bottom varies while the width remains the same, the slant of the course and its velocity will also vary. This is proven by the previous [chapter], because where contrary movements occur from the surface to the bottom and from the bottom to the surface, the current is delayed.

### CHAPTER IL.
->*On the height and depth of water.*<-

Water moving through a channel of equal width and bed will have as many different thicknesses as there are slants in the bottom over which it runs. For it is clear that the water fills every hollow of its bed before it flows over as an excess, etc.

### CHAPTER L.
->*On the same subject.*<-

Where the water is faster, it is of lesser depth; and conversely, it will be deeper where it has less motion, provided the river is of equal width throughout. This is proven by the fortieth [proposition] of the eighth [book] <note>Leonardo often cited his own numbered notebooks or theoretical propositions to maintain a logical structure in his scientific writings.</note> which shows that the river allows passage in every part of its width in equal time and with an equal quantity of water, regardless of how much the river varies in its width, slant, depth, or tortuosity <note>the degree of twist or winding in the river's path</note>, etc.

### CHAPTER LI.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Water moving through a channel of equal width and bed will be shallower <note>original: "sottile," literally "thin." In Leonardo's hydraulic observations, this refers to the vertical thickness or depth of the water column.</note> in proportion to how much faster it moves in one place than another. This is proven by the previous rule, which states: where the water is faster, it is of lesser depth, provided the river is of equal width, etc.

### CHAPTER LII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Water that runs over a bed of equal slant will have less depth where it has greater width. This follows from the sixtieth [proposition], which says that water which spreads out becomes lower in depth, etc.

### CHAPTER LIII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

A river that flows with an unequal course creates unequal depth and width; the reason is that the part of the river that moves faster is driven by a steeper bed. After this descent, the water strikes the area where the slope ends and lifts the gravel <note>original: "ghiara," referring to the pebbles and sediment carried by the current.</note> that was carried along, creating a resistant bank against its own impetus. From this, it happens that the water becomes still between the current and the bank, and the surface of the incoming water strikes the surface of the still water. Thus, the surface of the incoming water finds resistance in the surface of that water which has less impact than itself, and it immediately turns into a cross-current, etc.

### CHAPTER LIV.

->*Sign of the depth of the river.*<-

Where one sees mounds rising in flowing waters like <term>boils</term> <note>original: "bollori." Leonardo uses this term to describe the turbulent upwellings or "boiling" appearance of water on the surface caused by deep obstructions.</note>, it is a sign of great depth. These "boils" spring up after the water strikes the bottom; because of the speed of its rebound, it surges upward, penetrates the other water, and turns toward the

### CHAPTER LV.

*On water that runs over a great depth, and its motion.*

Water that runs over a great depth, if it does not enter with a strike <note>original: "colpo," referring to a sudden plunge or impact, like a waterfall or a steep rapid.</note>, does not reach the bottom; for this reason, the water at the bottom undergoes little change, and therefore remains colder in summer and warmer in winter than the rest, etc.

---

### CHAPTER LVI.

*On the motion of water of unequal width.*

Water that is found before the narrow places of rivers raises its surface and runs with fury through the narrow strait. At the end of this course, it strikes against water of slower motion, which, finding some resistance, changes into circular movements <note>These "circular movements" refer to the formation of eddies or vortices where a fast current meets a stagnant pool.</note>, and striking the cross-banks, it widens further (fig. 32. plate 7.).

---

### CHAPTER LVII.

*On the motion of water exiting from an opening of unequal width or height.*

Water that exits from an opening of unequal width and height will see its course bend, and it will strike the bank on the side of the lowest part of its exit; having struck the bank, it will jump to the opposite side with less fury. Let us say that the mouth AB (fig. 33. plate 7.) from which the water exits is four <term>braccia</term> <note>A Renaissance unit of measurement based on the length of an arm, approximately 23 inches or 58 centimeters.</note> high on side B, and one braccio high on side A; if the mouth remains always full, because water always declines toward the lowest places, it will run toward the low part of the opening, etc.

---

*Proportion of the motion of two rivers.*

Of two straight rivers of equal width and slope, the one that is deeper will be faster. This arises because the water which is more...

### CHAPTER LIX.

*On the depth of the river.*

Water that narrows becomes deeper, and where it widens, its depth decreases, provided the bottom has an equal slope. This happens because where the water narrows, it rises as it is slowed in its course <note>Leonardo is observing the "backwater effect." When water is forced into a narrower channel, it can "pile up" or rise in elevation as it encounters the constriction, effectively increasing its depth at that point.</note>, and where it widens, it finds a greater space than the water occupies, and thus, spreading through that space, it lowers itself, etc.

### CHAPTER LX.

*Proportion of the motion of two rivers.*

If two bodies of water flow through the same width, depth, and slope of riverbed, from one beginning to the same end, with an equal volume of water, the proportion between their courses will be as the proportion of their width: this arises from the 37th and 38th [propositions] of the eighth [book], which state: as much as you increase the width of the river, so much the velocity decreases, and as much as you decrease the width, you increase the velocity, etc. <note>This is a foundational observation of fluid dynamics known as the Principle of Continuity: in a continuous flow, the speed of the fluid is inversely proportional to the cross-sectional area of the channel.</note>

### CHAPTER LXI.

*On the motion of still water.*

A horse, a man, or anything else walking through still water <term>acqua morta: literally "dead water," referring to stagnant or non-flowing water such as a pond or lake</term> of medium depth, will cause that water to rise and cover much of the shore toward which the animal is heading. This principle is clearly proven, for if you take a step into such water, you will find the water creates a wave which directs itself and moves toward the place where the traveler is heading; it does not stop until it fulfills its momentum and occupies some of the bank. The second step makes another wave, which produces a similar effect, and so with the third; and each of his steps does the same on its own, so that the bank, which was previously exposed, is found to be covered by water for a long distance. And once you have come out of the water, you will see it return to its original place with a swift current, etc.

<vocab>depth, river, slope, width, velocity, motion, still water</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo defines the inverse relationship between a river's width and its velocity—a precursor to the law of continuity—and describes the displacement of waves caused by movement through still water.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, river depth, velocity, channel width, wave displacement, stagnant water, hydraulics</keywords>

### CHAPTER LXII.
*On water running through convex or concave channels.*

It is impossible for water to flow with equal thickness through a convex channel, even if such a channel is uniform in width (fig. 34, plate 7). <note>A convex channel is one that bows upward like the exterior of a circle. Leonardo observes that as water passes over a "hump" in the riverbed, gravity causes it to accelerate and thin out.</note>
It is possible for water to flow with equal depth through a channel that is concave along its length, etc. <note>A concave channel curves inward like a bowl; here, the water can maintain a more consistent depth as it follows the depression.</note>

### CHAPTER LXIII.
*On the proportion of the water's course.*

The shorter the course of the rivers, the greater their velocity will be; this is proven by the twenty-seventh [proposition] of this [work], which says: the motion of the water becomes as much faster as it has a greater declination <term>declination: the angle of the slope or the "drop" of the riverbed</term>, and from this follows the converse: the course of rivers will be as much slower as they have a greater length, etc. <note>Leonardo is applying a geometric principle: if two rivers drop from the same height, the one with the shorter path has a steeper slope, and therefore moves faster.</note>

### CHAPTER LXIV.
*On the same subject.*

Water that moves from a beginning to an end will be as much slower along an arc than along a chord as the arc is longer than the chord. <note>In geometry, a "chord" is a straight line connecting two points on a curve, while the "arc" is the curved path itself. Leonardo is comparing a straight canal to a winding river.</note> This is proven by the previous [proposition]. But here the adversary <note>Leonardo frequently uses an "adversary"—an imaginary critic—to test his theories through debate.</note> argues that the proposed motions will be completed in equal time; because even if the water flows slower along path AD (fig. 35, plate 7) than along AB, it recovers its motion through DC, which is more sudden than BC. To this one responds that the water at DC is thin and fast, and the water at AD is slow and thicker. And although the end of the water arrives at C from D at the same time as it arrives at C from B, the water from DC is as much thinner and less in volume than the water coming from B, as the line DC is less oblique than the line CD; so that in the same amount of time, the water at DC is less than that at BC.

### CHAPTER LXV.
*The clash of two reflected motions of water.*

The course of a river has two principal reflections <term>reflections: the way water "bounces" or diverts when hitting an obstacle</term>, one of which is from the bottom to the surface, and the other from one bank to the other, and if they...

<keywords>hydraulics, channel geometry, velocity, slope, arc and chord, fluid volume, reflected motion</keywords>

...clash into one another, the impetus of the striking water <term>striking water: Leonardo uses the term *percussore* to describe the active body of water that hits another</term> always joins with that which is struck; and if the striking water is of a greater quantity than the object struck, then a portion of that larger quantity, equal to all the water struck, will reflect backward, while the other portion will follow its initial incident motion <note>The "incident motion" refers to the original path of the water before it hits an obstacle</note>, as will be proven below, etc.

### CHAPTER LXVI.

#### *On the reflected motion from the bank.*

The less curved the bank is where the incident water strikes with its first leap, the further the second leap will be from the place where the first began. This is proven by the fourteenth [proposition], which states: that water will be faster in its reflection which reflects at a more acute angle; and by the fifteenth: that reflected motion will be longer which is caused by a weaker percussion <term>percussion: the force of impact</term>, and that percussion is weaker which is produced between more diverse angles. We shall say then that the water (fig. 36 plate 8) **A B**, striking the bank **R B C** at point **B**, will make its second leap at **D**, further from site **B** than the water **E F** struck by the bank **S F G** at point **F**, rebounding at point **H**, inasmuch as angle **B** is more acute than angle **F**, etc. <note>Leonardo is applying the laws of optics—where the angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection—to the physical movement of water hitting a riverbank.</note>

### CHAPTER LXVII.

#### *On the collision of water.*

The line of water that has greater movement breaks that of the lesser motion and plunges beneath it; and this happens because in the collision of two heavy bodies, the less powerful gives way to the more powerful, etc.

### CHAPTER LXVIII.

#### *On the entry of water into a pool, and its bed.*

If the entry of the water into the pool <term>pool: Leonardo uses the word *pelago*, usually meaning a sea or deep abyss, here referring to a deep basin in a river</term> (fig. 37. plate 8) is circular in shape, the concavity of its bed will be lunar <note>crescent-shaped</note>, receiving the gravel between one or two "horns" of such a shape, etc. <note>Leonardo explains that the force of a circular inflow of water carves out a crescent-shaped depression in the silt or gravel on the floor of the basin.</note>

<summary>Leonardo examines the physics of water striking surfaces, noting that water reflects off banks at predictable angles and that more powerful currents will dive under weaker ones upon impact.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, reflection, percussion, riverbanks, erosion, sediment, momentum</keywords>

LEONARDO DA VINCI

### CHAPTER LXIX.

#### *On the same subject.*

Regarding rivers that have a bed lower than the surface of the sea water, only that water flows which is higher than the sea; and this is demonstrated by the twenty-seventh [proposition] of the first [book], which says: water of equal height has no motion in itself; by the converse, that water which is of unequal height will have motion, with greater or lesser velocity in proportion to whether the inequality is of a greater or lesser difference, etc. <note>Leonardo is applying a basic principle of hydrostatics: water only moves from a higher potential to a lower one. If a riverbed is below sea level, only the "pile" of water above the sea's surface level has the pressure to flow out into the ocean.</note>

### CHAPTER LXX.

#### *On the same subject.*

Turbid running water, if it originates high and enters the sea at a high level, flows for a great distance due to the height of the initial impetus <term>impetus: the momentum or driving force an object possesses</term> before it sinks and mixes with the other water. This is proven by the fifty-sixth [proposition] of this [book], which says that water running over a great depth does not sink if it does not enter with a blow <note>original: "colpo"</note>, and this lasts as long as the impetus of its course remains.

### CHAPTER LXXI.

#### *On the same subject.*

Bodies of water will run one above the other (fig. 38. plate 8.) without mixing for a long space, when the entry of one into the sea is higher and faster than the other, as will be proven below, etc.

### CHAPTER LXXII.

#### *On water that strikes other water.*

Water that strikes other water with great obliquity <term>obliquity: a slanting or indirect angle</term>, that which strikes first is immediately reflected and slowed down, and the water that follows covers it like a thin garment <note>Leonardo uses the poetic term "veste" (garment or clothing) to describe a thin layer of water flowing over another</note>, and runs swiftly over that which was first slowed, and then reflects and slows with it in the same spot as the preceding water; and the water that follows does the same over it, and thus it continues its course successively (fig. 39. plate 9.).

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s investigation into hydraulic dynamics, shifting focus from how rivers enter the sea to how specific currents interact, collide, and bypass one another within a body of water.</meta>

->LEONARDO DA VINCI<-

### CHAPTER LXXIII.
*On water that passes through other water.*

The water that descends from **P** to **H** (fig. 40. plate 9.) and rebounds to **D**, will pass under the dead water <note>Dead water refers to stagnant or still water that has no current.</note> **MD** without mixing with it, and without carrying any part of it away; the water that descends from **P** to **K** will rebound to **D**, without leaving any part of itself at **K**, provided the entire width of such a course is equal.

### CHAPTER LXXIV.
*On the same subject.*

The water that falls from **M** to **H** (fig. 41. plate 9.) will carry with it the water that falls from **K** into **H** by means of the reflected motion <term>reflected motion: the path water takes after striking a surface or another body of water</term> **HN**; this would not happen if the impact <note>original: "percussione"</note> that **KH** makes upon the bottom did not break the course of the water **MH**, etc.

### CHAPTER LXXV.
*On the same subject.*

I ask whether the course of two lines of water, when crossing each other in the middle or in parts of the courses of rivers, pass through one another, or one over the other, or if they rebound backward after the impact (fig. 42. plate 9.). Certainly they bounce backward, because it is impossible for two bodies to pass through one another. But since the two bodies of water are struck together, they will spread out at the point of contact; and once they have struck, they would tend to depart at an equal distance from the center of the impact; that part which goes upward follows its nature <note>In Leonardo’s physics, the "nature" of a displaced fluid is often to seek its level or move where there is least resistance.</note>, and the other part below the center, which would want to go downward, being unable to do so, increases the volume of the water above, etc.

### CHAPTER LXXVI.
*On the collision of water.*

When two bodies of water of whatever thickness clash together, the area of contact will always be of equal size; and this is manifest, otherwise it would follow that, given, for example <note>original: "verbi grazia"</note>, a smaller thickness...

<summary>Leonardo examines the physics of colliding currents, arguing that water cannot occupy the same space and must therefore rebound or deflect upon impact. He explores how descending water can pass through stagnant water without mixing, depending on the force and angle of its course.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, fluid dynamics, reflected motion, impact of water, stagnant water, physics of flow, Leonardo's diagrams</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s analysis of hydraulic dynamics from the previous page. He is specifically examining the mathematical proportions of "power" and "quantity" (volume or width) when two currents collide, and how these factors determine which current prevails or how they deflect one another.</meta>

->LEONARDO DA VINCI<-

...of water half the size <term>subdupla: a mathematical term for a ratio of 1:2, or half</term> of the other, if at the point of contact they were not equal, whether it were greater or lesser than its own quantity. We say, then, that if the larger is one cubit <note>original: "braccio" — a Renaissance Florentine unit of measurement approximately 23 inches or 58 cm</note>, and the smaller is half a cubit, the smaller strikes the larger only in its half, and thus the larger strikes the smaller with its half, etc.

### CHAPTER LXXVII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Between two bodies of water of equal power, but of unequal thickness <term>grossezza: refers to the volume, width, or "bulk" of the water flow</term> that meet together, the one of smaller quantity will be faster. This occurs because of the "pass-through"; for the smaller, with all its power, strikes the larger only in that part equal to its own smaller size. Meanwhile, the larger body is of less power during the impact <note>original: "percussione"</note> in proportion to how much its quantity exceeds the smaller one; for this reason, the smaller will possess greater power and speed, etc.

### CHAPTER LXXVIII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

If, of two bodies of water, one encountering the other is of double the power and double the quantity of the other, their contact will be equal in power and quantity. This is proven by the two previous points. Because if the quantity and power are double, the contact of the larger must be made with only half of its quantity and power, and consequently, the forces must be equal.

### CHAPTER LXXIX.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Of two bodies of water of unequal thickness meeting together, that part of the larger which does not come into contact rises up and passes by, flowing along the straight line of its motion <note>Leonardo is describing how the "excess" water in a wider stream continues forward if it doesn't physically hit the opposing narrower stream.</note>. And this happens because that part of the water receives no hindrance or impact that would delay it or reflect it backward.

<summary>Leonardo explains the physics of colliding water currents, using ratios to demonstrate how the volume (thickness) and force (power) of a stream determine its speed and behavior upon impact.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, water currents, impact, proportions, fluid mechanics, power vs quantity, Leonardo's physics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s exploration of hydraulic dynamics. Having established the ratios of power between colliding currents on the previous page, he now examines the specific results of these collisions—specifically "incident" and "reflected" motion—and how the geometry of a riverbed (its straightness or winding nature) dictates the speed of the flow.</meta>

->LEONARDO DA VINCI<-

## CHAPTER LXXX.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Where the waters join together, the nearby motion that follows will be slow; and this happens because the <term>reflected motion: the movement of water after it has struck an obstacle or another current</term> is slower than the <term>incident motion: the initial, incoming movement of the water before impact</term>, and where the waters join, the reflected motion is generated.

## CHAPTER LXXXI.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Of two bodies of water of equal size, motion, and power clashing together, the angle of the reflection of the one will always be equal to the reflection of the other. This is evident; otherwise, one would be more powerful than the other, based on what was said above, etc. <note>Leonardo is applying a principle of physics similar to the law of reflection in optics: that the angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection when forces are balanced.</note>

## CHAPTER LXXXII.

->*On the speed of the river.*<-

Of rivers of equal origin, which flow through an equal plain with equal width of the banks, and run to the same destination: the one with the more <term>winding: original "torto," referring to a crooked or meandering path</term> path will have a slower movement; and by as much as the width of the straight river enters into the <note>original: "torture" — here meaning the bends, curves, or "tortuousness" of the river's path, rather than physical pain</note> windings of the other, so much faster will its course be. This is clear from what is said in the sixty-fourth and sixty-fifth [chapters] of this work.

## CHAPTER LXXXIII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Where the water encounters some object, or an underwater rock; there it becomes faster, because it rises and <note>original: "acquista peso" — literally "acquires weight." Leonardo often uses "weight" to describe the increased pressure or gravitational force exerted when water is forced upward before falling or pushing forward.</note> gains weight.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the mechanics of water collision, noting that the resulting reflected motion is slower than the initial flow and that river speed is dictated by the directness of its path and the presence of obstacles.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, incident motion, reflected motion, river geometry, velocity, water pressure, fluid dynamics</keywords>
