<meta>This page transitions from the general dynamics of clashing currents to the specific study of wave formation. Leonardo begins Book Three by defining the "wave" not as a simple movement, but as a specific physical reaction to obstacles, continuing his use of "incident" and "reflected" motion as the primary mechanics of fluid behavior.</meta>

# BOOK THREE
## OF THE WAVE OF WATER

---

### CHAPTER I.
#### *Where no wave is generated in the water.*

**The** water that moves between banks and a bottom that are straight and smooth will not produce waves of any kind. This occurs because a wave is not generated except by <term>reflected motion: movement that bounces back after an impact</term>, as will be explained; and reflected motion arises from the <term>percussion: the physical impact or collision of the water</term> of the <term>incident motion: the initial, incoming flow</term>, which occurs against a specific <term>object: original "obietto," referring here to an obstacle, protrusion, or irregularity</term> on the bottom or the sides of the channel. If there are no specific objects in those places, then for the reasons stated, no wave is generated (fig. 43. plate 10.).

### CHAPTER II.
#### *Where wave is generated in water.*

A wave is not generated except where reflected motion is found. This happens because a wave is not produced except through some percussion, as I will state in its definition; and where percussion occurs in the water, reflected motion is generated there. Therefore, the wave does not form except where reflected motion is found.

### CHAPTER III.
#### *What the wave of water is.*

A wave is the impression of a reflected percussion of water, which will be greater or lesser in proportion to the greater or lesser percussion, etc. <note>Leonardo views waves as the "imprint" left by the force of water striking an obstacle and rebounding.</note>

<vocab>reflected motion, incident motion, percussion, wave, object</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo defines the physical origin of waves, arguing they only occur when water strikes an obstacle and creates a "reflected motion." He posits that a perfectly smooth, straight channel would theoretically be waveless.</summary>
<keywords>wave mechanics, reflected motion, incident motion, percussion, hydraulics, riverbed geometry, fluid dynamics</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of "Book Three: Of the Wave of Water." While the previous chapters defined the wave as a reflection caused by striking an obstacle, Leonardo now moves to the "impression"—the lasting pattern or energy signature left in the water by these impacts and movements.</meta>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

### CHAPTER IV.
### *On the impression made in water.*

Every impression in the water is maintained for a long duration, and all the more so the faster it is; because it will be faster to the degree that it is caused by greater <term>percussion: the physical impact or collision of the water</term>, or indeed <term>impetus: the momentum or driving force of a body in motion</term>.

### CHAPTER V.
### *Which impression of water is more permanent.*

The impressions of water’s movements will be more permanent where the water carried by its impetus enters a <term>pool: original "pelago," referring here to a deeper or larger expanse of water</term> of slower motion; and conversely, the impressions of water’s movements will be less permanent where the water carried by its impetus enters a pool of faster motion. And this happens because the impetus is slowed down and destroyed where it finds an impediment, such as is found in water of faster motion, and not so much in water of slower motion, etc. <note>Leonardo suggests that a turbulent or fast-moving body of water "scrambles" or resists the incoming movement more effectively than a still pool.</note>

### CHAPTER VI.
### *On the impression of water within the air.*

The impressions made by water within the air <note>This likely refers to splashes, jets, or droplets thrown above the surface.</note> are destroyed in the first movement they make toward the earth, because the impetus is consumed in the <term>natural motion: the downward movement of objects toward the center of the Earth due to gravity</term>, which is generated in water within the air.

### CHAPTER VII.
### *On the impression of water within water, and within air.*

The impressions of movements made by water within water are more permanent than the impressions that same water makes within the air. This occurs because water within water has no weight—as proven in the first [book]—but only the impetus weighs upon it, which moves this weightless water until that impetus is consumed, etc. <note>Leonardo observes that because water is neutrally buoyant within itself ("water within water does not weigh"), the energy of a current or wave can travel further than a splash in the air, which is immediately pulled down by gravity.</note>

<vocab>percussion, impetus, pool, natural motion, within the air, within water</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo examines the "impression" (the lasting pattern of movement) in water, concluding that energy patterns last longer in still, submerged environments where gravity and opposing currents do not immediately destroy them.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, impetus, momentum, gravity, weightlessness of water, hydrodynamics, natural motion</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s investigation into the physics of water. Following the discussion on "impressions" (the patterns left by motion), he now distinguishes between the movement of the water's substance and the movement of the force—or impetus—traveling through it. He uses the famous analogy of wind blowing across a field of grain to explain how energy can travel while the medium itself remains in place.</meta>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

### CHAPTER VIII.
### *On the impetus of water.*

The <term>impetus: the driving force or momentum</term> is much faster than the water itself. This is because there are many times when a wave flees from the place of its creation, yet the water does not move from its site. This is similar to the wave made in May in the <term>grain: original "biade," referring to standing crops like wheat</term> by the course of the winds; one sees the wave run across the fields, but the grain stalks do not move from their place, etc. <note>Leonardo identifies a key principle of wave mechanics: waves transport energy (impetus), not necessarily the matter itself.</note>

### CHAPTER IX.
### *On the same subject.*

Sometimes the waves are faster than the wind, and sometimes the wind is much faster than the wave. Ships on the sea prove this when they encounter waves that move faster than the wind; for it may be that the waves were stirred up by great winds, and even after the wind has ceased, the wave has still reserved a great impetus, etc.

### CHAPTER X.
### *On the same subject.*

The wave, or rather the impetus of the wave, maintains its path through the unmoving waves created within a very great current of water, not otherwise than a sunbeam does as it passes through the course of the winds, etc. <note>Leonardo observes that different motions in water can overlap or pass through one another without losing their individual direction, comparing it to light passing through moving air.</note>

### CHAPTER XI.
### *On the same subject.*

The wave of impetus is sometimes immobile within a very great current of water, and sometimes it is very fast, namely on the surface of <term>dead waters: still or stagnant water</term>; because a single <term>percussion: a physical impact or strike</term> upon the water creates multiple waves, etc.

### CHAPTER XII.
### *Why the motion of the water’s impression penetrates one another without changing their original shape.*

If you throw two small stones at the same time, at some distance from each other, into a <term>pool: original "pelago," a deep or still body of water</term> without motion, you will see... <note>This chapter begins a famous observation on interference patterns, where circular ripples from two different sources pass through each other without being destroyed.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains that waves are a movement of "impetus" rather than the water itself, using the analogy of wind in a wheat field. He also notes how waves can retain momentum after wind dies down and how ripples can pass through one another without changing shape.</summary>
<keywords>impetus, wave mechanics, grain analogy, wind, current, interference patterns, percussion, dead waters</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s exploration of wave mechanics. While the previous page introduced the concept of "impetus" using the metaphor of wind blowing through grain, this page moves into a specific experiment: the collision of two sets of circular waves created by stones dropped in water.</meta>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

<margin>41</margin>

->LEONARDO DA VINCI<-

...causing around the said two stones two separate sets of circles, which, as they grow, come to strike against one another; I ask whether one circle, in colliding through its own expansion with the expansion of the other, enters into its wave, penetrating the wave of the other. For instance, how <term>N passes into C: referring to a diagram where N is a central point and C and D represent the edges of expanding waves</term> at the same time that N passes into D (fig. 44. tav. 10). Or rather, if such collisions result in them bouncing backward at equal angles. For example, as if C, entering into N, leaped back to D; and likewise D, striking N, leaped back to C. This is a very beautiful and subtle question. To which I answer: if the motion of the <term>impression: the shape or pattern of the wave</term> of the water is accompanied by the motion of the water itself—as would happen if the circles were caused by very great impacts—there is no doubt that, a new reflected motion being created there by the percussion of the wave, a new impression is also caused in such a way that the first ones are destroyed. Thus C, entering into N, does not leap back to D; nor does D, striking N, leap back to C. 

But if the motion of the impression of the water is accompanied only by the <term>impetus: the energy or force of the movement</term>, and not by the movement of the water itself, I say that N passes into C at the same time that N passes into D. And the reason is that although some demonstration of movement appears there, the water does not depart from its site; because the openings made by the stones were immediately closed, and that motion caused by the sudden opening and closing of the water creates in it a certain <term>shuddering: original "riscotimento," a sudden jolt or shaking</term>, which can rather be called a <term>tremor: a vibration or ripple</term> than a movement. 

And so that what I say may be more manifest to you, turn your mind to those <term>bits of straw: original "ferbuche," tiny pieces of light debris or chaff</term> which, because of their lightness, stay on top of the water; and you will see that, because of the wave made beneath them by the expansion of the circles, they do not depart from their site. Since, therefore, this disturbance of the water is a tremor rather than a movement, the waves can no longer encounter and break one another; because the water, having all its parts of one and the same quality, it is necessary that the parts transmit this tremor to one another without changing from their place. For as the water remains in its site, it can easily take this tremor from neighboring parts and pass it to other neighbors, always diminishing its power until the end. 

And because in all cases the motion of water has great conformity with that of the air, I will cite as an example the air. In air, although the <term>voices: sound waves</term> that penetrate it depart with circular movements from their causes, nevertheless the circles moved by different sources strike together without any impediment, and penetrate and pass through one another, always maintaining their causes as their centers. <note>Leonardo correctly identifies that wave phenomena—whether in water, air (sound), or light—can overlap and pass through each other without interference, a principle now known in physics as superposition.</note>

<summary>Leonardo investigates whether waves created by two stones in water bounce off each other or pass through one another, concluding that because waves are vibrations (tremors) rather than the displacement of the water itself, they can penetrate each other perfectly.</summary>

<keywords>wave interference, superposition, physics of water, acoustics, tremor vs movement, impetus, experiments with stones</keywords>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

### CHAPTER XIII.

#### *On the reflected motion of the wave.*

The motion of water within water changes into as many reflected courses in whatever direction as there are various objects at different <term>obliquities: angles of slant</term> that receive the <term>incident motion: the initial movement before impact</term> of such water, etc.

### CHAPTER XIV.

#### *On the objects in the water.*

There are three kinds of objects in the water: either water struck by water, or a flexible object, or a stable one, etc.

### CHAPTER XV.

#### *On the impact of water against objects.*

Water that strikes against objects sometimes leaps up greatly, sometimes a little, and sometimes descends; and this arises from the objects being narrow or wide, or from the greater or lesser descent in front of those objects, or from the more or less powerful current that strikes such objects.

### CHAPTER XVI.

#### *On the power of water in the impact against objects.*

Water, which flows through a channel of equal <term>latitude: width</term> and depth, will exert a more powerful <term>percussion: impact</term> on the object opposed to it, because all elements outside their natural place desire to return to that place (especially fire, water, and earth <note>Leonardo is referencing the Aristotelian view of physics, where elements seek their "natural" level: earth at the bottom, then water, air, and fire above</note>), and the more its return is made by a shorter way, the straighter that way will be, and the straighter the way, the greater the impact in its opposition. Therefore, because the lines of the angle E M N (fig. 45. tav. 10.) are straighter than the lines of the angle A C B, the impact received by the thing placed at the point of angle M will be greater than that of angle C. This same effect will be produced by the wind that flows through streets of equal width. Whence you will see that if you place a piece of wood at angle M, the water striking said object will rise up quite high, touching with its <term>rebound: the upward splash or surge</term> the thing placed opposite because of its height. And if you place said wood 

<vocab>reflected motion, wave, objects, impact of water, channel, width, depth, incident motion</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo classifies the types of obstacles water encounters and explains how the geometry of a channel and the physics of "natural placement" determine the force with which water strikes an object.</summary>
<keywords>hydrodynamics, wave reflection, percussion, Aristotelian elements, channel geometry, fluid dynamics, water impact</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter XVI regarding the power of water impacting objects. Leonardo is concluding his geometric proof about why certain angles of impact result in less force, specifically addressing how the distance traveled and the presence of opposing currents (resistance) affect the water's momentum. It then transitions into Chapter XVII, which compares the relative power of two merging streams based on the length of their respective channels.</meta>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

...at angle C, the water struck in said opposition will rebound much less. And if you were to say to me that line X F is so much longer than line Z F—which, according to what I show you in the <note>Leonardo is referring to his own scientific notes, likely a lost or earlier section of his treatise on water</note> of the second book, the further it moves in its natural course, the faster it will be—as to this, the reason you have alleged will not be denied. However, above this, the twenty-second proposition of the same second book will demonstrate that a straight river, although in every degree of motion it acquires a degree of <term>velocity: speed in a specific direction</term>, the more resistance it meets in opposition to its course, the more it becomes exhausted and the more it slows down. If, therefore, line X F, having arrived at F, has encountered so many crossings of incoming water courses, or reflections from the opposite bank, it will weaken its course so much that the <term>percussion: the forceful impact</term> of line X F will no longer be more powerful than that of line Z F. That this is true, perform the experiment: by placing an object in its course, you will see the water rebound along the line of the length of the opposing object, provided it stands straight. And if line X F were more powerful than line Z F, the water after the impact would rebound toward bank D Q; but since it does not rebound more to one side than the other, it follows that the forces of lines X F and Q F and their impacts are equal to each other, etc.

->### CHAPTER XVII.<-

->*On the same subject.*<-

If two waters flowing through two channels of equal <term>latitude: width</term> and depth, but of unequal <term>longitude: length</term>, converge upon the same object, the rebound of the part of the water from the longer channel after its impact will fall over the part from the channel of lesser length. This is proved by the twenty-first proposition of the second book, which says: a straight river of equal width and depth acquires velocity in every degree of motion; and by the sixty-eighth of the same, which says: *the line of water that has greater motion breaks that of the lesser motion, and thrusts itself beneath it* (fig. 46. plate 10). I say, therefore, that the water of the longer channel A B, and consequently the faster water, converging at object B with the water of channel C B of equal width and depth but of shorter length—and consequently of lesser power—will, after the impact, turn over the part of the water C B. And thus we have proved the intent, etc.

<vocab>rebound, length, object, impact, velocity, motion, channel, width, depth</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains that while water gains speed over distance, it also loses power when encountering resistance. He demonstrates that a stream from a longer channel will overpower a stream from a shorter one due to its accumulated velocity.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, water velocity, channel length, momentum, hydraulic resistance, impact force, hydrostatics</keywords>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

...it follows that the force is equal on both sides.

### CAPITOLO XVIII.

->*Which water is more powerful in the impact against objects.*<-

Turbid water <term>turbid: water that is cloudy, thick, or opaque due to suspended sediment like mud or silt</term> will have a much greater <term>percussion: the forceful strike or impact of a moving body against another</term> when opposing its course than clear water will. This is proven by the twenty-second proposition of the first book, which says turbid water is heavier than other waters, and by the twenty-eighth of the second book which says: *the more turbid the water is, the faster it is*. This is because, as has been proven elsewhere, among bodies of equal motion and size, the one that possesses greater weight will deliver a greater impact upon the object placed against its course.

### CAPITOLO XIX.

->*That a wave is never alone.*<-

A wave is never alone, but is mixed with as many other waves as there are <term>inequalities: irregularities or rough patches</term> on the object where such a wave is generated. This arises from the definition of the wave, etc. <note>Leonardo often uses "ec." (etc.) to indicate that a full proof or definition found elsewhere in his notebooks should be inserted or remembered here.</note>

### CAPITOLO XX.

->*Of the motion of the wave.*<-

The wave will have greater or lesser motion depending on whether its impact was caused by a greater or lesser object. Because water is flexible and can be penetrated by whatever strikes it, etc.

### CAPITOLO XXI.

->*Of the same.*<-

It is the same for one wave to move against another as it is for a wave to move by itself through still water. This is manifest by the twelfth proposition, and is proven again by the eighth, which says: there are many times when the wave flees the place of its creation, yet the water does not move from its site. <note>Leonardo is observing the principle of wave propagation: the energy/form of the wave travels through the medium, but the actual water molecules mostly stay in place, bobbing up and down rather than traveling with the wave.</note> etc.

<summary>Leonardo explains that sediment-heavy water has more striking force than clear water due to its density and speed, and then analyzes how waves propagate as energy through water without the water itself changing location.</summary>
<keywords>hydrodynamics, turbid water, percussion, wave motion, water density, wave propagation, physics of fluids</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo's systematic study of water, specifically the mechanics of wave propagation and the physics of impact (percussion). The title "Of the same" (Del medesimo) throughout this section refers back to the general topic of wave behavior established in Chapters XIX-XXI on the previous page.</meta>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

### CAPITOLO XXII.

->*Of the same.*<-

A wave cannot immediately end its motion and consume itself. This is proven because river waves break against their own current, and those of the sea break against water reflected back from the shore; therefore, water cannot immediately exhaust its wave, because as the water falls from the crest of the wave, it renews its velocity, power, and motion, etc. <note>Leonardo uses "ec." (etc.) to indicate that the logical proof continues or is detailed in another part of his manuscripts.</note>

### CAPITOLO XXIII.

->*Of the same.*<-

Water waves have two principal motions. The first is produced by the motion of water from a higher <term>obliquity: a slant or incline</term> striking against an object. The second is produced by water struck by a "wave" of air above water of equal height. However, the first type has two contrary motions within its height: one at its base and the other at its crest, etc.

### CAPITOLO XXIV.

->*Of the same.*<-

Many waves can be generated at the same time between the surface and the bottom of the same body of water, and these may be oriented in various directions. This is proven because if a wave is the impression of a reflected <term>percussion: the forceful strike or impact of one body against another</term>, every impact of water against an object is turned in different directions—upwards, downwards, this way, that way, higher, lower, further here, or further there. Therefore, various waves can be generated in the manner already described.

### CAPITOLO XXV.

->*Of the same.*<-

Water falls along every line from the crest of its wave. This happens because the water, within its wave, rises up into the air and comes to be surrounded by that air from every side; consequently, from every side, it has a <term>declination: a downward slope or path</term>; therefore, the water falls.

<summary>Leonardo examines the persistence and complexity of wave motion, describing how waves can exist at various depths simultaneously and how the surrounding air and gravity cause a wave crest to collapse in all directions.</summary>
<keywords>wave mechanics, fluid dynamics, percussion, reflection, hydraulics, river currents, wave crests</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s exploration of water dynamics, specifically focusing on the relationship between the slope of a wave, the formation of foam, and the physics of why river waves break against the direction of the current. The phrase "Of the same" continues to link these observations to the study of water waves begun on previous pages.</meta>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

## CHAPTER XXVI.

->*Of the same.*<-

Water falling from the crest of its wave moves more quickly where the fall is more <term>oblique: slanted or inclined rather than vertical</term>. This is proven by the twenty-seventh [proposition] of the second [book], which proves that water becomes faster the greater its declination. <note>Leonardo frequently references his own organized system of books and propositions, many of which are found in the Codex Leicester or the Treatise on Water.</note>

## CHAPTER XXVII.

->*Of the same.*<-

Water falling from the crest of its wave breaks more into foam where it finds more resistance. This is proven because foam, according to the forty-fourth [proposition] of the fifth [book], is caused by air trapped beneath the surface of the water; and because the wave rises higher where it finds greater resistance, and in falling, it traps a greater quantity of air beneath itself; therefore, the falling air, etc. <note>Leonardo concludes with "ec." (etc.), suggesting the reader should apply the logic of the referenced proposition to complete the thought.</note>

## CHAPTER XXVIII.

->*Of the same.*<-

Waves break against the course of the river, and never in the direction of its course. This is proven: water falls along every line from the crest of its wave, and it moves more quickly where such a fall is more oblique, and it breaks more into foam where it finds more resistance, as has been demonstrated in the three previous [chapters]; therefore, for what has been said, waves break against the course of the river, and never in the direction of its course. This is because water falling upon running water cannot generate a <term>rebound: a leap or upward surge</term> upon something that is moving away, as was said in the fifth [book], and thus it receives no impact; but it does receive impact from the opposite fall toward the current of the water. Water falling from the wave toward the direction of the river's course does not find water that flees such an impact, but rather finds water that meets such a fall. Thus, if the fall of the wave has four degrees of velocity, and the water coming to meet it has another four degrees of velocity, the impact of the wave results in eight degrees of velocity; and for this reason, the waves of rivers break against their own current. <note>Leonardo is describing the principle of relative velocity: when two forces move toward each other, their speeds combine to create a much more powerful collision than if one were "chasing" the other.</note>

<note>original vocabulary: wave (onda), falling water (acqua cadente), slope/inclination (declinazione), foam (ischiuma), impact (percussion), velocity (velocità), river course (corso del fiume)</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains the physics of wave velocity and foam production, concluding with a mathematical proof that river waves break against the current because the combined speeds of the wave and the oncoming water create a more forceful impact.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, relative velocity, wave mechanics, foam formation, river currents, physics of water</keywords>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

## CHAPTER XXIX.
*Of the sea wave, and its motion.*

The wave of the sea breaks against the water that retreats from the shore where it was struck, and not against the wind that pushes it; because, as has been said, the wave breaks most where it finds the most resistance, etc. <note>Leonardo observes the phenomenon of "undertow" or backwash. He argues that the incoming wave's collapse is caused more by colliding with the water returning from the beach than by the force of the wind itself.</note>

## CHAPTER XXX.
*Of the same.*

The higher the waves of the sea are above the ordinary height of the water’s surface, the lower are the depths of the <term>valleys: the troughs or low points between wave crests</term> placed between those waves. And this is because the great falls of the large waves create large hollows in the valleys.

## CHAPTER XXXI.
*Of the same.*

The valley placed between the waves is lower than the common surface of the water. This is evident from the previous [chapter], and experience demonstrates it to us, as is seen in water that falls back to fill the places struck by the falling water, etc. <note>Leonardo is describing the displacement of water; as the crest rises, it "pulls" water from the surrounding area, creating a trough that sits below the sea's calm-weather baseline.</note>

## CHAPTER XXXII.
*Of the same.*

At one and the same time, countless other waves will move atop the greatest wave of a <term>pelago: an archaic term for the deep sea or open ocean</term>, and these move in various directions. This is proven by the twenty-fifth [proposition] of this [book], which says: water falls along every line from the summit of its wave, etc.

## CHAPTER XXXIII.
*Of the same.*

The greatest wave is clothed with countless other waves, which move in different directions, as was said in the previous [chapter]; but those so... <note>The text cuts off here, typical of Leonardo's notebooks where a thought may continue on the next leaf or remain an unfinished draft.</note>

...they sink more or less deeply, according to whether they are born from greater or lesser <term>potenza: power or driving force</term>. Because the greater the weight of the water in the largest wave, the greater the force that drives the smaller waves.

## CHAPTER XXXIV.

### Of the same.

From the previous [chapter] it follows that the largest wave is "clothed" in various [smaller] waves, which move in as many different directions as there were different places from which they were separated, etc. <note>Leonardo uses the phrase "clothed" (Italian: *veste*) to describe how the surface of a large swell is textured by secondary ripples and smaller waves.</note>

## CHAPTER XXXV.

### Of the same.

The three waves of the sea which follow one after another are formed near the shore; the first reflects into the one following and increases its size; and that following wave reflects into the third and increases it. But here an opponent might doubt this, saying that each wave should successively reflect back into the other <note>original: "riflettere nell'altra"</note>. To this it is answered that these three waves are not generated in the same spot; therefore, the first reflection does not reach the second wave, and the second reflection does not reach the third wave, etc. <note>Leonardo is attempting to explain how waves gain height as they approach the shore by interacting with the energy of the waves following behind them.</note>

## CHAPTER XXXVI.

### Of the same.

We see the sea send its waves toward the land, and although the wave that ends at the shore is the last of its group and is always overtaken and submerged by the second-to-last, nonetheless the second-to-last does not pass beyond the last; rather, it sinks into the same place as the last one. Since this submerging <note>original: "sommergimento"</note> is always in continuous motion where the sea meets the land, it is necessary that there be a contrary motion along the seabed. Just as much water must return underneath toward the cause of its movement as the driving force pushes out from itself along the surface, etc. <note>This is a remarkably accurate early description of undertow. Leonardo observes that because water is being pushed onto the shore, an equal amount of water must be flowing back out along the bottom to maintain the balance of the sea.</note>

<summary>Leonardo examines the complex movement of waves near the shore, describing how waves "clothe" one another and proposing the existence of undertow to balance the water pushed toward land.</summary>

...always returning to the sea from which it came.

## CHAPTER XXXVII.
### *On the height and depth of waves.*

A wave will be higher where the flow of water terminates with greater <term>impeto: the force or momentum of moving water</term>. This is because where the water encounters such force, it is more likely to stall; and where running water stalls and is delayed, it rises higher.

## CHAPTER XXXVIII.
### *On the same subject.*

The waves of rivers that run against the direction of the winds will be of greater height than others. This happens because the wind increases the momentum; and as previously stated, where the flow of the water terminates with greater force, the wave will be higher, etc.

## CHAPTER XXXIX.
### *On the same subject.*

If, according to the second [proposition] of the sixth [book], <note>Leonardo frequently references his own numbered scientific observations or "books" from his notebooks, which he intended to organize into formal treatises.</note> that thing which is the primary cause for breaking the straightness of the water's established course is most worn away and removed by that water; then we shall say that if the air were the cause of breaking the straightness of the wave’s elevation, the air itself would be worn away by such a <term>percussione: the impact or striking of a fluid against a surface</term> of water. 

However, such air is not the cause of breaking that course; rather, the sole cause is the weight the water acquires as it rises out of its element <note>Leonardo refers to "its element" as the main body of water; once a wave rises above the surface, gravity—or "weight"—pulls it back down.</note>. The water would slow down at that spot if it were a <term>discrete quantity: a substance made of separate, disconnected parts</term>; but because it is a <term>continuous quantity: a fluid where all parts are connected</term>, it is necessary that one part of the water pushes and the other pulls, because they are joined together, etc.

## CHAPTER XL.
### *On the reflected motion of the wave.*

A wave has both reflected and incident motion. Reflected motion is that which occurs during the generation of the wave after striking an object, where the water leaps back and rises toward the air; it is in this motion that the wave acquires its height, etc.

<vocab>waves, momentum, running water, winds, reflected, incident, percussion</vocab>

## CHAPTER XLI.

### *On the incident motion of the wave.*

The <term>incident motion: the falling motion of a fluid under the influence of gravity</term> of a wave is that which the wave makes from the summit of its height to the lowest point of its depth; this is not caused by any <term>percussione: an impact or striking force</term>, but only by the <term>gravità: the weight or downward tendency</term> acquired by the water when it is outside of its <note>Leonardo followed the Aristotelian view that the "element" of water has a natural place; when raised above that level, it seeks to return to it due to its weight.</note> element, etc.

## CHAPTER XLII.

### *Which wave will be of greater power.*

That wave will be of greater power which possesses greater velocity; by "greater power," I do not mean a greater quantity of water, etc.

## CHAPTER XLIII.

### *On the velocity of the wave.*

The water that moves in the composition of a wave will be found to have the same velocity in its elevation as it has in its declination; and the velocity in the center of its lowest depth will be the same as that at its highest peak. If the motion were not equal, the wave would not be of equal depth and width; and if it were indeed of equal width and depth, but not of equal motion, it would create a great height in the place where it was most delayed, etc.

## CHAPTER XLIV.

### *On the same subject.*

If water were a <term>discrete quantity: a substance made of individual, separate parts (like grains of sand)</term>, as it is instead a <term>continuous quantity: a fluid where the parts are connected without interruption</term>, the motion it makes between the highest peaks and lowest depths of its waves would be unequal. This is because any part that rises would acquire a degree of slowness with every degree of motion, such that at the highest peak it would be at its maximum slowness; then, in descending, it would acquire a degree of velocity with every degree of motion, so that at the lowest depth it acquires its greatest motion. Thus, the position that terminates the descent is the one that receives <note>By "detriment" or "lesion," Leonardo refers to the physical wear or impact caused by the force of the water.</note> detriment, while the position that terminates its height—its elevation—receives no lesion at all. But if the quantity is continuous, as it is in effect, it has motions...

<summary>Leonardo defines the "incident motion" of waves as a result of gravity and argues that a wave's power is derived from its velocity rather than its volume. He also examines the symmetry of wave motion, concluding that water acts as a continuous rather than discrete medium.</summary>

# CHAPTER XLV.

## On the same subject.

The side of the wave in <term>incident motion: the downward or falling movement of water due to gravity</term> is fast, and the end of the <term>reflected motion: the upward or rebounding movement of water after its fall</term> is slow. This is proven by the eighth [proposition] of the second [book], which says that incident motion is more powerful than its reflected motion, etc.

### CHAPTER XLIX.

->*On the penetration of one wave with another.*<-

Although all the impressions of <term>percussions: impacts or strikes upon the water's surface that create ripples</term> made upon the water can penetrate one another without their own destruction, as was proven above; a wave never penetrates another, but they only reflect from the place of their impact. This is proven by the seventy-eighth [proposition] of the second [book], which proves that when two lines of water meet, each bounces backward, etc. <note>Leonardo distinguishes here between "impressions" (ripples or circular wavelets that seem to pass through each other) and "waves" (larger masses of moving water), which he believes collide and rebound like solid objects.</note>

### CHAPTER L.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Waves of whatever size they may be, when striking together, the contact of one and the other shall be of equal size. This is proven because if it is true that every part retains within itself the nature of the whole, necessity requires that waves—which are part of the water—when clashing together, their contact shall be of equal size, as I said of the water in the seventieth [proposition] of the second [book], etc.

### CHAPTER LI.

->*On the same subject.*<-

When two waves of unequal height clash together, the higher part of the one that does not come into contact with the other surmounts it and continues straight. And this is because it finds no impediment, etc. <note>Leonardo observes that if a tall wave hits a short one, the "extra" height of the tall wave simply passes over the top of the collision because there is no opposing mass of water to stop its forward momentum.</note>

## CHAPTER LII.

### *On the motion of the wave created by the impact of water against the bottom.*

The wave created by the <term>percussion: the physical strike or impact</term> of water against the bottom will produce a motion underneath opposite to the motion on the surface; this is made manifest by the <unclear>...</unclear> of the fourth [book], etc. <note>Leonardo is likely describing the formation of an eddy or a "back-roll" where the water hitting an underwater obstacle is forced to circulate in a direction contrary to the surface flow.</note>

## CHAPTER LIII.

### *On the wave of a moving object thrown into still water.*

A stone thrown into <term>still water: 'acqua morta,' literally 'dead water,' referring to a pond or stagnant pool</term> will create a perfectly circular motion, provided the water is of uniform depth. In such a case, there is no obstacle to prevent its ripples from being equidistant from their center, as observation proves (fig. 47, plate 10).

## CHAPTER LIV.

### *On the oscillating wave.*

An <term>oscillating wave: 'onda titubante,' literally a 'hesitating' or 'staggering' wave</term> is one that strikes against opposite banks and reflects from them, diminishing through so many repetitions that the waves eventually merge together and cease along with the <term>impetus: the initial force or momentum</term> that moves them, as seen in figure C above, etc.

## CHAPTER LV.

### *On the same subject.*

If you throw a stone into a <term>basin: 'pelago,' which usually means a sea or abyss, but here refers to a confined experimental tank</term> with several sides, all the waves that strike those sides will reflect back toward the point of impact. In their encounter with other incoming waves, they never obstruct one another's course. This is clear from what was stated in the twelfth [proposition]... <note>Leonardo is observing the principle of wave superposition—the ability of waves to pass through one another without losing their individual shape or energy.</note>

## CHAPTER LVI.

### *On the same subject.*

A wave generated in small basins often travels back and forth to the point of impact; the wave will come and go more times depending on whether the basin where...

### CHAPTER LVII.
#### *On the same subject.*

The same wave, generated in a small basin, acquires a much greater number of other waves layered upon itself the more impacts and reflections it makes against the opposite shores, etc. <note>Leonardo is observing the phenomenon of wave interference, where reflected waves overlap with new ones to create a complex surface pattern.</note>

### CHAPTER LVIII.
#### *On the wave caused by a long-shaped moving object.*

The wave caused by a <term>long-shaped moving object: 'mobile di lunga figura,' such as a stick or a plank</term> forms a more perfect circle the closer it gets to its <term>dissipation: 'consumazione,' the point where its energy is exhausted</term>, etc. <note>This identifies a principle of fluid dynamics where the specific shape of the source matters less as the wave travels further away, eventually regularizing into a circle.</note>

### CHAPTER LIX.
#### *On the wave created by a moving object in a current.*

A stone thrown (fig. 48, plate 10) into <term>flowing water: 'corrente acqua'</term> will create an oval-shaped ripple made of two motions: namely, straight and circular. The reason for this is that such a circular ripple is hindered on the upstream side of the river by the incident motion of the current which pushes it downward; at the sides, it is squeezed by the reflected motions from the riverbank; and on the downstream part of the river, because it is not hindered in its straight motion, it continues to circulate until its <term>impetus: 'impeto,' the initial force of the throw</term> is spent, etc.

### CHAPTER LX.
#### *On the wave made by a moving object on the surface and at the bottom of the water.*

The wave created by an object moving ahead of itself through the air, and between the surface and the bottom of the water, takes the shape of a hemisphere. The wave created by the object on the surface of the water is in the shape of a semicircle, and at the bottom, it has the shape of a spherical quarter, etc. <note>Leonardo is attempting to map the three-dimensional displacement of water, visualizing how the pressure wave expands differently depending on the boundaries of the environment.</note>

### CHAPTER LXI.
->*On the same subject.*<-

I ask why a <term>moving object: 'mobile'</term> in its motion on the surface of the water creates a wave in front of itself, yet does not create a wave when that same object moves between the surface and the bottom. This occurs because the water at the surface borders the air, whereas the water between the surface and the bottom borders the water above it and below it. <note>Leonardo identifies that waves are a surface phenomenon requiring a "free surface" (the interface between two different fluids, like air and water) to manifest as we typically see them.</note>

### CHAPTER LXII.
->*On the columnar wave.*<-

Those waves that are created over such objects (fig. 49. plate 11), provided no other objects of any kind intervene, no matter how small they may be—and especially at the edges of the surface's width, where even the smallest grain exists—create a <term>columnar wave: 'onda colonnale'</term>, etc. <note>By "columnar wave," Leonardo likely refers to a stationary wave or a ripple that maintains a vertical ridge-like shape as it passes over or around a small obstruction.</note>

### CHAPTER LXIII.
->*On the intersections of semi-columnar or longitudinal waves.*<-

Where (fig. 50. plate 11) the water in the river has little motion, the semi-columnar wave will make a direct <term>intersection: 'intersegazione'</term>. And this happens because where the <term>incident motion: 'moto incidente'</term> <note>original: "moto incidente" — the incoming force or flow of the water</note> is weak, the reflection is straighter and less impeded, etc.

### CHAPTER LXIV.
->*On the same subject.*<-

Where the flowing water is faster, the longitudinal waves will curve (fig. 51. plate 11). And the reason for this is that such waves are pushed downward by the motion of the incident water of the current, which is faster than the said waves, etc.

### CHAPTER LXV.
->*On the same subject.*<-

Where the speed of the current is unequal, the curvature of the <term>longitudinal waves: 'onde longitudinali'</term> will vary across the river (fig. 52. plate 11). This variation occurs on the side where the strongest current is found. The reason is that, just as waves curve toward the middle when the current is straight and centered, so too will the same current cause waves to curve toward the sides if the flow is concentrated there, etc. <note>Leonardo is observing how a "velocity gradient"—the difference in speed between different parts of the water—distorts the shape of waves.</note>

### CHAPTER LXVI.
->*On the same subject.*<-

There are as many longitudinal waves created in the water’s channels as there are <term>bulges: 'globosità'</term> in its banks. This is because there are as many <term>reflected impacts: 'percussioni riflesse'</term> as there are objects and <term>irregularities: 'globulenze'</term> in the bank, as is evident from what was stated above and seen in the preceding figures, etc. <note>Leonardo treats the riverbank as a series of obstacles; each bump in the dirt or stone acts as a point of reflection that generates a new wave pattern.</note>

### CHAPTER LXVII.
->*On waves with a square base.*<-

Waves with a square base are generated by the <term>intersection: 'intersegazione'</term> of the longitudinal waves born from the banks of the rivers. Examples are the longitudinal waves AC and BC, which intersect at the points CD and CE, as seen in the preceding figure, etc. <note>When waves reflecting off the left bank meet those reflecting off the right bank at an angle, they create a grid-like or "square" interference pattern on the surface.</note>

### CHAPTER LXVIII.
->*On the concavity of square waves.*<-

The <term>hollows: 'concavità'</term> of square waves are also square themselves, as shown by the square wave ABCD (fig. 53. plate 11). And the hollow area surrounding the square wave in four places is formed as you see in BFD, CDH, ABG, and ACI, etc. <note>Leonardo notes the symmetry of fluid dynamics: if the peak of the wave is square, the valley (concavity) created between the peaks will mirror that geometric form.</note>

### CHAPTER LXIX.

*On the waves generated in the collisions of columnar waves.*

In the collision of <term>columnar waves: 'onde colonnali'</term> a third wave is always created; this is because, upon striking each other, the water rises and then descends toward the <term>downstream flow: 'fuga della corrente'</term>, as happens in the collisions A, B, C, etc. (fig. 54, plate 11). <note>Leonardo uses the term "columnar" to describe waves that form long, cylindrical ridges on the surface of the water, resembling the shafts of architectural columns.</note>

### CHAPTER LXX.

*How the aforementioned waves are dissipated.*

The <term>square-based waves: 'onde quadre'</term> are confused within the curvature of the columnar waves, which over a long distance are converted back into the straightness of the common course of the water. Furthermore, they are destroyed by the many various thicknesses of the columnar waves that originate at the bank, and by various objects along that bank, etc. <note>Leonardo is observing the process of 'attenuation,' where complex wave patterns eventually smooth out as they move further from the obstacle that created them.</note>

### CHAPTER LXXI.

*On the simple semi-columnar wave.*

The simple semi-columnar wave is generated by any tiny object attached to the bank; the water that strikes it creates a long wave in the shape of a half-column, which extends diagonally toward the opposite bank, and there it dies and is reborn. Let object A (fig. 55, plate 11) be placed in the bank N M of the channel N O P M. I say that the water striking this object will create a wave which, by its continuous creation, will itself become continuous; it would always remain so if it were not interrupted either by the bank (as seen at point B located on bank O P) or by the common course of the water in the channel. This current strikes the wave and pushes it continuously along every degree of its length, until eventually it straightens it out to follow its ordinary course. This is seen in the second figure, where in the channel M N O P the wave S F, generated by the object F and directed toward the bank, is pushed so much by the common course that it is overcome, and at point F it follows the rest of the water, etc. <note>This describes the "V-shaped" wake or stationary wave seen behind an obstruction, which eventually aligns with the main current due to the force of the downstream flow.</note>

### CHAPTER LXXIII.
#### On the same subject.

When two <term>columnar waves: 'onde colonnali'</term> collide fully with equal size and power, they then fully turn back without any penetration of one into the other. <note>Leonardo is arguing that waves act as distinct physical entities that reflect off one another rather than merging like a single body of water.</note> This is proven by the . . . . of the twentieth [book/proposition], and by the . . . . of this one; which says: one wave never penetrates another, but they only reflect from the place of their <term>impact: 'percussione'</term>. As in (fig. 57, plate 12), the wave generated by object F, meeting at point H with the wave generated at the opposite bank by object G, is reflected at point I, and wave G is reflected at point K, etc.

### CHAPTER LXXIV.
#### On the same subject.

If the columnar waves are of unequal size, the larger and the smaller do not follow their usual law, because the larger does not bend, and the smaller joins with the larger one. And this happens because of the excessive height of the larger wave, which <term>surmounts: 'sormonta'</term> the [smaller] <note>The original Italian repeats 'maggiore' (larger), likely a transcription error for 'minore' (smaller).</note> with as much weight as its height provides; it consumes it and draws it behind its own course, as is seen with the larger wave MO (fig. 58, plate 12) acting upon the smaller N, etc.

### CHAPTER LXXV.
#### On the same subject.

If the birth of two equal waves occurs one after the other, their collisions will not be of <term>equal power: 'potenza eguale'</term>; therefore, the course of the first will bend before that of the second. And this happens because the first, before it reaches the meeting with the second, is already lowered and spread out; thus, being overtaken by the second, which is higher and more united, it is defeated and is the first to give way and bend, as you see in (fig. 59, plate 12). <note>Leonardo observes that as waves travel, they lose energy and "flatten out" (dilatata), making older waves weaker than newly formed ones.</note>

<summary>Leonardo examines how waves of the same or different sizes interact, concluding that waves reflect rather than mix, and that the taller or newer wave will physically dominate the older or smaller one.</summary>
<keywords>wave mechanics, hydraulics, columnar waves, reflection, wave impact, fluid dynamics, energy dissipation</keywords>

## CHAPTER LXXVI.

### *On the same subject.*

The <term>columnar wave: 'onda colonnale'</term> lowers the further it moves; it also spreads wider and increases in speed. This is proven because if the wave proceeds from a <term>reflected impact: 'percussione riflessa'</term>, and the reflected motion completes its course along the <term>line of incidence: 'linea dell'incidenza'</term>, then according to the ninth [proposition] of the second [book], the wave must necessarily lower and expand over a long distance. <note>Leonardo is applying geometric optics to water; just as light reflects at an angle, he believes waves follow predictable geometric paths that cause them to lose height as they spread across a surface.</note> In doing so, it transitions from reflected motion back into incident motion, gaining greater speed than before, because according to the eighth [proposition] of the same [book], the incident motion of water is more powerful than its reflected course (fig. 60. plate 12).

## CHAPTER LXXVII.

### *On the same subject.*

Regarding unequal columnar waves, where the larger one is born before the smaller one: the smaller wave intersects and passes over the larger one (fig. 61. plate 12). This happens because the larger wave, having been born first, has already expanded by the time it meets the smaller one; finding no obstacle as high as itself, the smaller wave first strikes the lower part of the larger one, then flows over it, collapsing on the opposite side and continuing with its initial <term>impetus: 'impeto'</term>. <note>Leonardo observes that a fresh, steep wave has enough energy to "climb" over an older, flatter wave that has already begun to dissipate.</note>

## CHAPTER LXXVIII.

### *On the same subject.*

If, among unequal columnar waves, the smaller one originates higher up the river than the larger one, then the larger wave follows its natural course, and the smaller one follows the course of the larger. This is evident from the seventy-fifth [chapter] of this work (fig. 62. plate 12).

## CHAPTER LXXIX.

### *On columnar waves generated by the widths and narrownesses of the banks.*

If the course of the river is narrowed on one of its sides, a columnar wave is generated which will be fast (fig. 63. plate 12). This occurs for the reasons stated above regarding columnar waves hitting obstacles...

<note>The text ends mid-sentence, leading into a discussion of how the physical shape of the riverbank—specifically narrowing channels—creates high-velocity waves similar to those formed by rocks in the stream.</note>

### CHAPTER LXXX.

*On the same subject.*

If the <term>banks: 'argini'</term> are narrowed equally on each side of the <term>current: 'corrente'</term> directly opposite one another, then the <term>columnar waves: 'onde colonnali'</term> <note>These are the standing, V-shaped waves that form when water is constricted.</note> strike each other. After this impact, they will turn and reflect back toward their respective banks with greater speed (fig. 64. plate 12). This increase in <term>speed: 'velocità'</term> occurs because that portion of the water that meets and would naturally tend to move downward cannot do so due to the resistance of the riverbed; consequently, the portion of water above it grows. Thus, in such an <term>impact: 'percussione'</term>, the height of both waves increases; the higher they are, the more powerful and fast the motion at the end of their fall will be, etc.

### CHAPTER LXXXI.

*On the same subject.*

If the narrowings of the banks are lower than one another <note>Meaning they are staggered or at different elevations relative to the water's surface</note>, then it is possible for the upper columnar wave to pass beneath the lower columnar wave (fig. 65. plate 12); this can occur according to the seventy-sixth [proposition] previously discussed, etc.

### CHAPTER LXXXII.

*On the same subject.*

Based on what has been said regarding columnar waves, and what will be said in the following book regarding <term>swirling waves: 'onde circonvolubili'</term> and <term>eddies: 'retrosi'</term>, one may infinitely multiply various other propositions, according to whether the distances between the banks and the obstacles are greater or smaller, and whether the obstacles are thicker or thinner. This variation can be infinite due to the nature of <term>continuous quantity: 'quantità continua'</term> <note>A philosophical and mathematical term referring to things that can be infinitely divided, such as space or water, as opposed to "discrete" quantities like integers.</note>, which applies to the banks, the obstacles, and their distances. And experience will suggest infinite more [propositions] to you, etc.

<summary>Leonardo explains how riverbanks narrowing at the same point cause waves to collide and bounce back with increased speed, while also noting that the infinite variability of nature allows for endless unique hydraulic conditions.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, river banks, wave reflection, water speed, continuous quantity, hydraulics, standing waves</keywords>

## CHAPTER LXXXIII.
### *On waves beneath the water covered by the surface.*

Water that runs on the surface, in the act of covering the waves situated beneath it, does not deviate from its straight path, even though those submerged waves are transverse <note>Positioned crosswise or at an angle to the direction of the flow.</note> to its course. This is seen in the surface **A B C D** in the figure below, which surmounts the transverse waves **E F** and **E G** with its straight course (fig. 66. plate 13). This occurs because the momentum of its primary path, where it borders the air, has more power within that surface layer than the resistance of the water enclosed and subjected beneath it. The proof of this is seen by light objects that float and run upon the water.

->FOURTH BOOK ON THE EDDIES OF WATER<-

### CHAPTER I.

->*What an eddy is.*<-

**A**n eddy <note>original: 'retroso'; in Leonardo's hydraulics, this refers to a vortex, backwater, or whirlpool where the current turns back on itself.</note> is the impression of a revolving, reflected impact of water, created either within the body of the water, or against obstacles along the bank or the bottom.

---

### CHAPTER II.

->*On the same subject, and its cause.*<-

Universally, all things desire to maintain themselves in their own nature; <note>Leonardo often personifies nature, suggesting that elements like water have a "desire" or "will" to continue their current state—a precursor to the law of inertia.</note> hence, a moving course of water seeks to maintain its motion according to the power of its cause. If it encounters a contrasting opposition, it completes the length of its intended course through a circular and eddying movement.

---

### CHAPTER III.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Every movement driven by force must follow such a path as is determined by the relationship between the thing moved and the thing that moves it. If it finds a resistant opposition, it will complete the length of its destined journey through circular motion, or through various other leaps and bounds; when the time and the journey are calculated, it is as if the course had proceeded without any contradiction at all. <note>Leonardo is suggesting that the energy of the water isn't lost when it hits a wall; instead, it "pays its debt" of distance by spinning in a circle or bouncing rather than moving in a straight line.</note>

---

### CHAPTER IV.

->*On the water of the eddy.*<-

Eddies are always a mixture of two waters: the incident <note>incoming</note> and the reflected <note>rebounding</note>; this is because every wave has a reflected and an incident motion, as was stated in the fortieth proposition of the previous book.

<summary>Leonardo defines the "retroso" (eddy) as a circular movement caused by water's momentum meeting resistance, arguing that the water completes its intended travel distance through rotation when blocked. He describes eddies as a combination of incoming and rebounding currents.</summary>

<keywords>eddy, hydraulics, momentum, fluid dynamics, reflection, resistance, vortex, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

->CHAPTER V.<-

->*How many revolving motions an eddy has.*<-

Every revolving wave has a motion turned upward or downward, or to this side or that; for every mass of water that strikes <note>original: 'percossa'; refers to the impact or collision of the current against an object.</note> against some object is divided into four various and principal motions: right and left, upward and downward, as is established in the tenth [proposition] of the second [book], etc.

->CHAPTER VI.<-

->*On the same subject.*<-

Of eddies, some are turned toward the flow <note>original: 'fuga'; literally the "flight" or discharge of the river's current.</note> of the river's water, and some are turned against that same flow of water. The eddies that turn backward are those of the swiftest water; and those which are turned toward the flow are those of the water that slows down in its course along the river.

->CHAPTER VII.<-

->*On the same subject.*<-

Of the eddies in water, some are filled with air, and some are filled with water. All those that have a rim <note>original: 'argine'; here referring to the raised edge or "wall" of the vortex visible on the surface.</note> on the surface are filled with air, and those that have their rim beneath the water are filled with water.

->CHAPTER VIII.<-

->*Which eddies are more permanent.*<-

Those eddies are more permanent <note>In this context, "permanent" refers to how long the vortex maintains its shape and energy before dissipating.</note> which are filled with water. And those that are filled with air have little permanence. This happens because water within water does not weigh <note>Leonardo is observing a principle of hydrostatics: that a fluid submerged in the same fluid is in a state of neutral buoyancy and does not "weigh" downward in a way that collapses the structure of the vortex.</note> as water does when it is above the air; for this reason, the eddies of water surrounded by air have weight, and they die quickly.

->CHAPTER IX.<-

->*On eddies created by the impact of water within water.*<-

The question is asked why the impact of water within water creates a line of circular and eddying motions, and does not leap straight forward, like other water that leaps... <note>The sentence ends abruptly, suggesting a continuation on the following page regarding the fluid dynamics of submerged jets.</note>

<term>circonvolubile</term>: revolving or swirling; describing the spiral motion of a vortex.
<term>retroso</term>: an eddy or backwater; a point where the current turns back on itself.
<term>percussione</term>: the impact or striking of the water against an obstacle or another body of water.

<summary>Leonardo classifies eddies by their direction of rotation relative to the current and explains why submerged vortices last longer than surface ones due to the effects of weight and buoyancy.</summary>

It is proved: the eddies are sometimes many and occur in the midst of a great watercourse, and the closer they get to the end of the course, the larger they become; they are created on the surface by the waters that turn backward after the impact <note>original: 'percussione'; refers to the collision of different currents.</note> they make against the swifter current. Because the fronts of such waters are struck by the fast motion, and being sluggish themselves, they are immediately transformed into that velocity, whereby the water that is adjacent and attached behind them is pulled by force and torn from the rest. Therefore, all the water would turn successively, one after the other, with such velocity of motion, were it not that the primary current cannot receive them unless they rose above it; and this being impossible, it is necessary that they turn back and consume those rapid motions. Thus, through various circulations, these eddies gradually consume the momentum they have begun, and they do not remain still, as is proved in the second [book]. Rather, once they are generated, they are carried by the momentum of the water in that same shape while spinning: thus they come to have two motions; one within itself due to its revolution, and the other following the course of the water, which transports it until it is undone.

->### CHAPTER X.<-

->*On the eddies of water falling into water and their power.*<-

The eddies created by falling water are as much more powerful as they are closer to the bottom, and conversely, they are as much weaker as they are closer to the surface. ### CHAPTER XII.

->*On the same.*<-

If the courses of the water are not equal, their impacts will strike the turnings of such water toward the bank of the water of lesser power <term>potenza: the force, momentum, or kinetic energy of a current</term>. ### CHAPTER XIII.

When the waters are unequal, their encounters continue to revolve, and the less powerful water enters with the branches <note>original: 'rami'; refers to the individual streams or paths within an eddy's structure.</note> of its lower eddies beneath the branches of the higher eddies—that is, those born from the more powerful waters, etc.

### CHAPTER XIV.

->*On the same.*<-

When the water of greater power strikes the water of lesser power, then the line of the eddies curves, entering with its convex side into the body of the water of lesser power, etc.

### CHAPTER XV.

->*On the same.*<-

When the curvature of the line of the eddies enters with its convexity into the water of lesser power, then that lesser water remains at such a boundary without motion; there it is pushed back, rises, and acquires weight <note>Leonardo uses 'gravità' (gravity/weight) to describe the pressure or potential energy created when water is forced upward or compressed by a stronger current.</note>.

->### CHAPTER XVI.<-

->*On the eddies formed by the impact of water against obstacles along the bank.*<-

Eddies are always generated between the columnar wave <term>onda colonnale: a wave that rises in a column-like shape, often where currents meet or hit an obstacle</term> and the bank where such a wave has grown. This is proven because the water falls from the crest of the columnar wave toward the acceleration of the current, but it is pushed and pulled into the general flow of the water by the power of that current. The water likewise falls from the crest of the wave toward the bank (fig. 69, plate 13); but there, because the current is obstructed and delayed by the transverse motion of the same wave, it descends toward the bank, penetrating it with greater force, and by striking it <term>percotendola: the act of the water hitting or "hammering" the bank</term>, creates an eddy.

->### CHAPTER XVII.<-

->*On the same.*<-

In every impact <term>percussione: the forceful striking of water against a surface</term> of water against a narrowed bank or an obstacle in the bank, eddies are generated from the surface to the bottom. This is because, upon impact, the water rises up the height of the bank or obstacle, and gaining weight, it descends to the base. In the middle of this descent, it meets the water that had previously turned toward the bottom and is pushed back to the surface; there, overtaken by new arriving water and driven to the bottom in various swirls, it is carried along behind the main course of the water.

->### CHAPTER XVIII.<-

->*On the same.*<-

A bank that is set back to provide greater width to the canal will cause the immediate generation of an eddy. This is proven because the river, which acquires a sudden width, also acquires a sudden breadth of water, and the water that spreads out also decreases in depth;

He also notes that widening a channel creates immediate turbulence due to changes in water depth and breadth.</summary>

## CHAPTER XX.

->On the same.<-

If the channel should suddenly acquire width on every side, it generates eddies on every side, of which the first will be more powerful (fig. 71 plate 13). This is evident from the two previous chapters.

## CHAPTER XXI.

->On the same.<-

If the columnar wave <term>onda colonnale: a wave that rises in a column-like shape, often where currents meet or hit an obstacle</term> strikes the eddies generated in one of the widened banks, then those enclosed eddies will become more constricted and will acquire great power <term>potenza: the force, momentum, or kinetic energy of the water</term>. This happens not only because the columnar wave in such a case raises the water level, but also because by striking the bank itself, the force is multiplied, and the eddies remain more upright (fig. 72 plate 13).

## CHAPTER XXII.

->On the same.<-

If an object is placed in one of the widened banks beneath the columnar wave created by that object, the eddies that are generated will be of great power. This happens (fig. 73. plate 14) because where the water is most constricted by the object, the generated wave rises higher; for this reason, it descends toward the bank with greater impetus <term>impeto: the momentum or surge of the water's movement</term>, and there eddies of greater power are created, etc.

## CHAPTER XXIII.
### *On the same subject.*

Wherever an obstacle is placed in a widened bank <term>argine: the side, levee, or embankment of a watercourse</term> following its columnar waves <term>onde colonnale: vertical surges or column-like waves formed by converging currents</term>, the eddies <term>retrosi: backward-flowing or circular currents, such as whirlpools or vortices</term> that strike the bank will be of greater power <term>potenza: the force or kinetic energy of the water</term> than those that came before (fig. 74, plate 14). This is because the wave rises not only due to the narrowing of the channel, but also from the collision <unclear>of</unclear> one current with another; from this height, it descends toward the bank with greater impetus.

## CHAPTER XXIV.
### *On the same subject.*

If a side obstacle is placed that occupies a large part of the channel, not only will the aforementioned eddies strike its own bank, but eddies will also run from the surface to the bottom along the opposite bank. This happens because the water rises so high in the narrow mouth of the passage that, upon exiting, it does not only dash toward the bank—where the usual eddies are generated—but also falls to the bottom due to its own weight. There, in its rebound, it is oppressed by new falling water, and it revolves now below and now above, behind the path of the current.

## CHAPTER XXV.
### *On the same subject.*

If two equal side obstacles are placed, not only are lateral revolutions created at the banks after the water's discharge, but various circulations from the surface to the bottom also go revolving in the middle; this is manifest from the seventeenth [rule] and the previous one.

The water that falls from such a depth swirls in a backward-spinning motion <note>original: "revertiginosamente"; Leonardo describes a complex, turbulent spiral that turns back against the main flow.</note> both below and above, because...

### CHAPTER XXVII.

***Eddies created by water flowing from a narrow opening into wide and slow water.***

Water that pours through a narrow opening, rushing with fury into the slow currents of large pools <term>pelaghi: deep, wide stretches of water or basins within a river system</term>, creates massive eddies <term>retrosi: circular currents, whirlpools, or back-flows</term>. This happens because in the greater quantity of water there is greater power <term>potenza: the force or kinetic energy of the water's mass</term>, and the greater power offers resistance to the lesser. In this case, the water arriving at the pool strikes its slow-moving water; because this slow water is supported by the rest of the pool, it cannot give way with the necessary speed. Those incoming waters, unwilling to slow their course, instead turn backward after making their impact <term>percussione: the physical collision of the water currents</term>. Following the initial movement with eddying circles, the water fulfills its drive <note>original: "desiderio" (desire). Leonardo often personifies natural elements, describing their physical momentum as a "desire" to reach a certain state or direction.</note> at the bottom. In these eddies, the water encounters nothing but its own motion, with which the turns accompany each other, one inside the other; in this circular revolution, the path becomes longer and more continuous, because it finds nothing to oppose it but itself (fig. 78. plate 14).

---

### CHAPTER XXVIII.

***Concerning eddies created by the impact of water against a curved bank.***

An eddy of water is always formed where the current is divided by the angle that bends it. For example, if the water S A (fig. 79. plate 14) were bent from A toward D, the angle A would divide it: one part would follow its ordinary course along the path A D, while the other part would turn into an eddy along the path A B <term>argine: the bank or embankment of the river</term>.

---

### CHAPTER XXIX.

***Concerning eddies created by obstacles.***

An obstacle <term>obbietto: any physical object, such as a rock or piling, placed in the flow</term> always changes the natural order of the established waves and eddies. The current A B consisted of a specific order of eddies, but the obstacle that receives their impact disrupts them all and converts them into another form (fig. 80. plate 15).

<summary>Leonardo explains how water creates whirlpools and eddies when passing from narrow channels into large pools or when striking curved banks and obstacles. He focuses on the physics of momentum and resistance, noting how water's force is redirected into circular motions.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, eddies, fluid dynamics, river banks, water pressure, momentum, Leonardo da Vinci, vortex movement</keywords>

### CHAPTER XXX.

->On the same subject.<-

All eddies, and all the water in river currents that slows down behind obstacles in those courses, have no other way to exit except by coming into contact with the main current of those rivers.

---

### CHAPTER XXXI.

->On eddies created by obstacles rising from the bed above the water.<-

If the obstacle that divides the course of the water is placed at an equal distance from its banks, the line of its eddies <term>retrosi: circular back-flows or whirlpools created when a current is interrupted</term> will be directed toward the middle of its current. This is proven by the second [rule] of this [treatise], which states: when the courses of the waters are equal, the revolutions and eddies formed where the waters meet run in a straight line, as seen in the preceding figure.

---

### CHAPTER XXXII.

->On the same subject.<-

If the obstacle dividing the course of the water is placed further from one bank than from the other, the eddies generated by the water in the area of greater distance flow toward the opposite bank with their revolutions. This arises from the twelfth [rule] of this [treatise], which says: if the courses of the water are not equal, the collisions of the water will carry the swirls <term>raggiramenti: the twisting or rotating motions of the water</term> toward the bank of the water with less power <term>potenza: the force or kinetic energy of the water's mass</term> (fig. 81. plate 15).

---

### CHAPTER XXXIII.

->On the same subject.<-

If an obstacle placed in the middle of the river with a varying slant <term>obliquità: the angle at which an object is tilted relative to the flow</term> is positioned equidistant from its banks, the eddies created at the lower part of the obstacle will move toward the opposite bank. This also occurs because of the twelfth [rule] cited above. Since the water on the lower side of the obstacle possesses greater power than the water on the other side—being present in a greater quantity—the result is predictable. Just as if the obstacle A B C D (fig. 82. plate 15) were placed at an angle across the path of the river M N O P, it is clear that in the...

<note>Leonardo's descriptions here are foundational to fluid dynamics. He is observing how the symmetry or asymmetry of a riverbed obstacle determines the path of the resulting turbulence. By "lower part of the obstacle," he likely refers to the side of a slanted barrier that is further downstream or creates a wider opening for the water to pass through.</note>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes how the placement and angle of obstacles in a river determine the direction and force of eddies, establishing that water with greater volume and "power" dictates the movement of these swirls toward the weaker side.</summary>
