->CHAPTER XXXIV.<-

->*On the same subject.*<-

Regarding the thickness of the water striking the obstacle: the lower part is the first to strike the bottom and immediately reflects back to the surface. The middle part above does not reflect off the bottom, but instead, encountering the first part that is reflecting upward, it strikes against it and is jolted. Thus, it also reflects along those same lines and vortices <term>revertigini: turbulent, twisting movements or small whirlpools</term>. When these two bodies of water clash—the lower with the upper—they unite and swirl together where they come into contact.

->CHAPTER XXXV.<-

->*On the same subject.*<-

Why is the surface of flowing rivers always filled with various clusters <note>Leonardo uses the word "gruppi" to describe the knots or bunches of waves and ripples on the surface.</note>? The reason for this is that, just as the stockings that clothe the legs reveal on the outside what they hide within, so the surface of the water demonstrates the quality of its bed. For (fig. 83. plate 15) that part of the water that scrapes along the bottom, finding various obstacles and the humps of stones, strikes them and leaps upward, carrying with it all the other water lying above it.

->CHAPTER XXXVI.<-

->*On the same subject.*<-

Why is it that in a level river, if there is a single stone <term>sasso: a stone or rock on the riverbed</term> on the bottom, the water forms many globes <note>referring to rounded swells or billows of water</note> after it? This happens because the water that strikes said stone falls down behind it and creates a bit of a hollow. Having sought out this cavity with a turbulent <term>repertiginoso: characterized by swirling, vortex-like motion</term> course, it leaps upward again. Having fallen back to the bottom once more, it does the same thing, and thus it repeats this many times, similar to a ball struck against the ground, which makes many leaps—each smaller than the last—before its motion ends, as is seen by experience (fig. 84. plate 15).

<summary>Leonardo explains how the topography of a riverbed is reflected on the water's surface, comparing it to fabric revealing the shape of a limb. He also describes the physics of water "bouncing" over obstacles like a ball.</summary>

352

### CHAPTER XXXVII.

*On the same subject.*

Water that falls along an oblique line will bounce at equal angles <note>Leonardo is applying the optical and mechanical law of reflection—where the angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection—to the movement of water striking a surface.</note>, and it will make many such bounces one after another. These bounces will be similar to one another in size, yet the individual particles within each wave will be unequal to one another, according to the unevenness of the riverbed—due to gravel or other objects that receive the impact of the water's wave. For example, in the impact of the wave against stone A (fig. 85. plate 15), its peak will be at M, and if it strikes stone B, its peak will be at N. Because of this, a wave will be full of humps <note>original: "gobbi"</note> that are larger or smaller than one another; however, if the place where the wave strikes were a horizontal column, the wave would be without humps, etc.

### CHAPTER XXXVIII.

*Which is the eddy of double power.*

That eddy <term>retroso: a back-flow, whirlpool, or vortex</term> which, in addition to its rotation, strikes against either the bank or an object and, leaping into the air, falls back down upon the other water, will be of double power <term>potenza: the force or kinetic energy of the water's mass</term>. This is because, in addition to the drilling motion <term>trivellamento: literally "boring" or "drilling," referring to the vertical, screw-like downward force of a vortex</term> caused by the aforementioned rotation, the impact of the falling water is also added.

### CHAPTER XXXIX.

*On the power of eddies, and their velocity.*

An eddy that has a large circumference is slower in the middle than at its sides. This happens because such eddies simply swirl around while the water remains in its own place, without descending to the bottom in a drilling motion.

### CHAPTER XL.

*On the same subject.*

The eddy that is fast in the center of its circulation carries air and water down to its bottom. The reason for this is that such eddies, in addition to their circular motion, possess a drilling motion directed toward the bottom.

<vocab>eddy (retroso), drilling motion (trivellamento), impact (percussione), wave (onda), circulation (circolazione)</vocab>

### CHAPTER XLI.
#### *Of the eddy full of air.*

If water situated higher than the air acquires weight—as was shown in the first <note>Leonardo frequently refers to his own numbered propositions or "books" within his notebooks; "the first" likely refers to his first book on the elements of water.</note>—what cause makes the water at the sides of the eddies <term>retrosi: whirlpools or back-flows</term> stay higher than the floor of said eddy, which is filled with air all the way to the bottom?
You have the sixty-first [proposition] of the seventh [book] which proves that every ship weighs only along the line of its motion, and nowhere else; and from this you see eddies deepening in the manner of a great well, the sides of which are water, which everywhere is higher than the air within that eddy; and such embankments of water have no weight, except along the line of their motion, during the time they possess the power given to them by their motor <note>original: "motore." In Leonardo’s physics, the "motor" is the force or cause of movement—in this case, the momentum of the water.</note> (fig. 86. plate 16).

---

### CHAPTER XLII.
#### *Of the power of eddies.*

The eddies and revolutions of reflected water <term>acqua reflessa: water that has bounced off an obstacle or bank to flow against the main current</term>, when returning to the current of its river, always penetrate it more in its lower part than at the surface. This arises because the current, according to the thirty-third [proposition] of the second [book], is faster above than below, and consequently is more powerful above; for this reason, it is less penetrated by the impact of such reflected and revolving <note>original: "revertiginosa"</note> water above than below, A B C D (fig. 87. plate 15), as well as the thickness of the current with its width E A F B, and the weight and width of the reflected and revolving water, etc.

---

#### *On the same subject.*

The eddy that has the lip of its mouth less oblique <note>That is, an eddy opening that faces the oncoming current more directly rather than at a sharp angle.</note> will be more penetrable. This happens because of the following [proposition], which says:

### CHAPTER XLIV.
#### *On the same subject.*

That eddy which is straighter will be of greater power, and conversely, that eddy which is more oblique will be weaker; and this arises because it is bent and overcome by the current, etc.

<summary>Leonardo explains the physics of "hollow" whirlpools, noting that the water's momentum (its "line of motion") allows it to resist gravity and form walls around a core of air. He also observes that back-flows penetrate the bottom of a river more easily than the surface because the surface current is faster and more forceful.</summary>

### CHAPTER XLV.

->*Of the same.*<-

That eddy is more powerful which is found to be more enclosed. This is proven by the preceding [propositions]. Because the more it is enclosed, the more it remains upright, and the less it is conquered and overcome by the main current.

### CHAPTER XLVI.

->*Of the same.*<-

The revolutions of transverse <term>retrosi: eddies or back-flows</term> at every degree of their length acquire width and slowness; this happens because at every degree of length the reflected motion is consumed; and consequently, it loses power, becomes less tight, and widens as it merges with the current.

### CHAPTER XLVII.

->*Of the same, and its destruction.*<-

The surgings <note>original: "bollori," literally "boilings." Leonardo uses this to describe the turbulent, bubbling upwelling of water caused by obstacles or bottom friction.</note> of the reflected motions of the water from the bottom of rivers destroy the circular movements of both transverse and longitudinal eddies.

### CHAPTER XLVIII.

->*Of the same.*<-

The law of water does not fail in its eddies, because the water that becomes slow turns back and creates eddies contrary to its [original] motion, just as it does with the eddies of faster water. Therefore, such eddies—both of the slow and of the fast water—mix together and double their power, but not entirely; for the slow eddy, in mixing with the fast one, becomes faster than before, and the fast eddy, in catching and joining with the slower one, acquires slowness.

### CHAPTER XLIX.

->*Of the concavity of the eddy.*<-

That eddy will have a deeper concavity <note>original: "concavità." This refers to the hollow, funnel-like center of a whirlpool.</note> which is generated in water of faster motion. And that eddy will be of lesser concavity, which...

### CHAPTER L.

*Of the same.*

In water of equal velocity, that <term>retroso: eddy or whirlpool</term> will better maintain its vertiginous concavity which has a greater mass of water revolving together with its motion. This is said because many times eddies are generated in a narrow current within a great breadth of water; because this [current] is partly supported by the eddy—which is full of its usual revolutions turning between the water and the air of the concavity—this lateral water, being of great weight, presses against the sides of the eddy where it rests. Finding it weak, the water comes to close it in, and thus it poorly maintains its concavity <note>The "concavity" refers to the hollow, air-filled center of a vortex or whirlpool.</note>.

### CHAPTER LI.

*Of the submersion of eddies.*

The submersion of eddies in fast waters will be against the approach of the water <note>i.e., moving upstream against the current</note>, and in slow waters, it will be toward the retreat of such waters <note>i.e., moving downstream with the flow</note>.

### CHAPTER LII.

*Of the motion of eddies and their velocity.*

The helical or revolving motion of every liquid is so much faster the closer it is to the center of its revolution. This which we propose is a case worthy of admiration <note>Leonardo often uses "admiration" in the sense of scientific wonder or a paradox that challenges common sense.</note>. For the circular motion of a wheel is so much slower the closer it is to the center of the <term>circonvolubile: the revolving mechanism or axis</term>. But we have this specific case in the behavior of water. It is the same motion in terms of speed and width in each entire revolution of the water, whether it be in the circumference of the larger circle or the smaller one. But the smaller circle is as much less oblique than the larger one as the larger circle is more oblique than the smaller one. And so, such water is of equal motion throughout its circular path; if it were not so, the concavity would immediately collapse. But because the lateral weight of such a revolving circulation is double, such a concavity does not have permanent motion. And of this doubling of weights, the first arises in the revolving motion of the water; the second is generated in the sides of that concavity, which lean upon and finally collapse over the air that the aforementioned concavity had filled.

<summary>Leonardo examines the structural stability of whirlpools, noting how lateral water pressure can collapse a vortex's hollow core and observing the counterintuitive increase in speed near the center of a liquid vortex compared to a solid wheel.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, whirlpools, vortex, rotational velocity, water pressure, concavity, helical motion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s investigation into the fluid dynamics of "retrosi" (eddies or whirlpools). Having previously established the relationship between velocity and the center of rotation, he now examines the vertical structure of these vortices—how they behave at the bottom versus the surface and how water pressure influences their power and shape.</meta>

LEONARDO DA VINCI

### CHAPTER LIII.

->*Of the motion of eddies.*<-

Water at the bottom creates its own <term>retrosi: eddies or whirlpools</term>, which revolve in a motion contrary to that on the surface. The reason for this is that the circular currents, which are wide at the top, narrow down to a single point and submerge there. Continuing their motion along the path they began, they arrive at the bottom and create a motion opposite to the one above when they disperse from their center. This is shown in (Fig. 88, Plate 16), in the first of which you see that all the revolutions end by plunging into a single point; in the second, the surface circulation at point A of the dispersion turns in the opposite direction. This is also proven by the thirtieth subject of the second <note>original: "trentesima materia del secondo"; likely referring to the second book or section of Leonardo’s lost treatise on water</note>, which says that the current flows faster at the surface than at the bottom; consequently, if the eddy is turned in one direction by the speed at the surface, it reunites at a point in the slower water below and finds its circular motion with a contrary movement.

---

### CHAPTER LIV.

->*Of the power of the eddy.*<-

The eddy sometimes increases in power and decreases in diameter, and sometimes it decreases in power and increases in diameter. This was discussed in the forty-fifth [chapter]. The first case occurs when the water pours out through its bottom. This is because the water composing the eddy is faster the deeper it is, as it has a greater weight of water above it <note>Leonardo is observing the effects of hydrostatic pressure—the deeper the water, the more force it exerts</note>, and thus it becomes faster. And because the water pushes more at the bottom than at the top, it further narrows the <term>vacuita: hollow core</term> of the eddy and bends it, because it directs itself toward the exit of the water from its <term>pelago: basin or main body of water</term> (Fig. 89, Plate 16).

---

### CHAPTER LV.

->*Experiment to see if eddies are wider at the bottom than at the top.*<-

Take a rod (Fig. 90, Plate 16) and attach small wooden fins to it. Give it enough weight at the foot so that the bottom part sinks to the floor, and tie it with a thread suspended from a staff. Dip a portion of it under the water and observe whether the top part, in its rotation, bends or not, and by how much. <note>This experimental setup allows the observer to measure the difference in rotational force between the surface and the deep water by seeing how the rod twists or tilts.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains the vertical mechanics of whirlpools, noting that rotation directions can reverse between the surface and the bottom, and proposes a mechanical experiment using a weighted rod with fins to measure these forces.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, eddies, whirlpools, water pressure, hydraulics, experimental method, Leonardo's notebooks</keywords>

LEONARDO DA VINCI

# BOOK FIVE
## OF FALLING WATER

---

### CHAPTER I.
->*Of water that falls through the air.*<-

**Water**, as it falls through the air, separates with difficulty from its own mass. The evidence for this is seen in the curvature it generates and the twisting of one part around the other, between which the <term>panniculata: filmy or membrane-covered</term> water is interposed <note>Leonardo is observing surface tension, describing the water as having a "panniculazione" or a skin-like quality that holds the stream together</note> ( **fig. 91. tav. 17** ).

### CHAPTER II.
->*On the same.*<-

Every part of the mass of water falling from a river through the air follows the line along which the <term>impeto: impetus or momentum</term> that brought it to such a fall first began, as demonstrated in ( **fig. 92. tav. 17** ).

### CHAPTER III.
->*On the same.*<-

The motion of water through the air follows, for a certain distance, the line of the sides of the <term>spiracoli: openings or vents</term> from which it descends. This does not happen to a discontinuous quantity <note>meaning a solid, separate object</note>, such as is demonstrated by a stone thrown by the <term>moto circonvolubile: revolving or circular motion</term> of a person, which follows a straight path <note>Leonardo refers to how a stone leaves a sling in a straight line, whereas water tends to maintain the shape and "memory" of its container for a short distance</note>. Water does not do this because of its <term>panniculazione: the formation of a skin or membrane</term>, which over a long distance of movement binds all its parts together ( **fig. 93. tav. 17** ).

<summary>This page introduces Leonardo's study of falling water, focusing on why a stream of water remains a cohesive unit rather than instantly dispersing. He attributes this to "panniculazione" (surface tension) and the initial momentum (impeto) provided by the opening from which it falls.</summary>
<keywords>falling water, surface tension, impetus, fluid dynamics, cohesion, momentum, spiracoli</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of falling water begun in Book Five. Leonardo applies principles of fluid dynamics established in earlier books (specifically Book 2 and Book 8) to explain why water accelerates as it falls and how the trajectory of a falling stream is determined by the speed of its surface versus its depth.</meta>

LEONARDO DA VINCI

### CHAPTER IV.
### Of the velocity of falling water.

The upper water will have a faster descent than the lower water: this is evident from the thirty-third [proposition] of the second [book], which says the current is faster at the surface than at the bottom; and also because the further water descends from a height, the more weight and velocity it acquires, with the upper part having a greater distance to descend than the lower part (**fig. 94. tav. 17**). <note>Leonardo notes that because surface water starts with a higher initial velocity and has the entire height of the fall to accelerate, it always outpaces the water from the bottom of the stream.</note>

### CHAPTER V.
### Of the course of falling water.

In every course of water that approaches its fall, the bending of the <term>declinazione: downward slope or curvature</term> will begin sooner on the surface than at the bottom. This is proven by the preceding [chapter], because if the surface water AB (**fig. 95. tav. 17**) is faster than the lower water CD, the water EA will be pulled with greater velocity by the water AB than the water FC is pulled by the water CD; and consequently, the bending of the slope will begin first on the surface at point G rather than at the bottom, where it begins at point H. <note>Leonardo is describing the "lip" of a waterfall, observing that the top of the stream begins to curve downward earlier than the bottom because of its greater momentum.</note>

### CHAPTER VI.
### Of the impetus of water falling through the air.

Falling water of equal thickness will be more powerful in its motion the lower it pours into the vessel where it was enclosed. This is proven in the eighth [book] by the seventeenth [proposition]. <note>This refers to the "head" or pressure of water; the lower the exit point on a tank, the greater the pressure and resulting force of the stream.</note>

### CHAPTER VII.
### Of the percussion of water with water through the air.

Of waters that strike one another through the air, the more powerful penetrates the less powerful, twisting and carrying with it all that water which falls upon it, while the remainder follows its natural course. This is proven by what was said in the second [book], that the line of greater motion of the water breaks that of the lesser motion. <note>Leonardo is applying a law of momentum: a stronger, faster jet of water will physically "cut" through and redirect a weaker one upon impact.</note>

<vocab>falling water, velocity, descent, curvature, impetus, impact, through the air</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the acceleration and trajectory of falling water, concluding that surface water moves faster and begins to curve downward sooner than the water beneath it. He also describes the physics of colliding water streams, where the more powerful current dictates the path of the weaker one.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, acceleration, momentum, waterfall mechanics, water pressure, stream collision, hydraulics</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo's analysis of the physics of falling water from Book Five. Having previously established that falling water accelerates and that the surface moves faster than the interior, he now investigates how different streams of water interact when they collide mid-air.</meta>

LEONARDO DA VINCI

### CHAPTER VIII.
#### Of the same subject.

It is possible that when water falling through the air strikes other water pouring from a vessel through the air, the more powerful stream may entirely block the straight descent of the less powerful one, carrying it along through its entire course in the air. For example, if the water pouring from vessel AB (**fig. 96. tav. 17**) and falling through the air with motion BO is struck by the water pouring from vessel DE with motion EF. I maintain that the motion EF will bend upon the <term>percussione: impact or collision</term> made by the water against the water BO, and will follow the remainder of its course along the line FM, together with the water BO; none of that water will fall along the path it originally began at EF. <note>Leonardo is observing the transfer of momentum, where a stronger stream absorbs the trajectory of a weaker one upon impact.</note>

### CHAPTER IX.
#### Of the same subject.

It is possible that water falling through the air onto other water falling through the air in the same direction—where one is more <term>obliquo: slanted or angled</term>—may carry the less slanted stream entirely with it. For example, if the water falling from vessel GH, which flows with a less slanted motion HI, is struck by water falling with a more slanted motion from vessel KL. I maintain that the more slanted water LN will entirely carry with it the less slanted water HI along that same path LN through which it flows. This occurs because the more slanted stream, due to the weight of the water, can be more powerful than the less slanted one (**fig. 97. tav. 17**). <note>Here, "oblique" refers to the angle of the stream's descent; Leonardo suggests that the stream with the steeper angle or greater mass dominates the final path of the combined water.</note>

### CHAPTER X.
#### Of the same subject.

It is possible that of two waters falling through the air toward the same direction, the less slanted one may partially block the straight descent of the more slanted one, and by partially piercing through it, carry the struck portion along with it. For example, if the water OQ (**fig. 98, tav. 17**) from vessel OP, pouring with a more slanted motion OQ toward the center of the world <note>original: "centro del mondo"—a common Renaissance term for the center of the Earth, which defines the direction of gravity</note>, is forced at point R by the less slanted water BK, which pours from the same vessel OP with motion BK; I maintain that the portion of the water OQ that is struck by water BK at point R is carried by that same water BK along the path BK.

<note>This final observation describes a partial collision where only a fraction of one stream is diverted by the other, depending on the point of contact and the relative force of the streams.</note>

<summary>Leonardo examines the dynamics of colliding water streams in mid-air, explaining how a more powerful or more steeply angled stream can divert or absorb the path of a weaker one.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, momentum, trajectory, gravity, water collision, physics of fluids</keywords>

### CHAPTER XI.
->*Of the same subject.*<-

It is possible that of two streams of water falling through the air toward the same direction, the one that is more slanted may partially block the less slanted one in its straight descent; by striking a part of it, it carries that struck portion along with it. As if the water XY (**fig. 99. tav. 17**) from vessel VX, falling with a more slanted motion onto the water AO which pours from vessel EO, should strike it; I say that the water XY carries with it that portion of the water OA which it strikes. <note>Leonardo is describing how a diagonal stream "clips" a vertical one, pulling a portion of the vertical stream into its own diagonal trajectory.</note>

### CHAPTER XII.
->*Of the same subject.*<-

Of falling streams of water that strike each other in the air and are of equal thickness, the one that descends from a higher position in its <term>bottino: a reservoir or water source</term> will join with the path of the lower one, and (**fig. 100. tav. 18**) will finish its course with it. This is proven by the sixth rule of this work, which says that falling water of equal thickness will have a motion that is more powerful the lower it pours into the vessel; and by the seventh rule, where it is said of waters striking in the air that the more powerful penetrates the less powerful, twisting and carrying the other struck stream along with it. <note>Leonardo uses "power" to describe what we would now call kinetic energy or momentum. Water falling from a greater height gains more speed and thus dominates the collision.</note>

### CHAPTER XIII.
->*Of the same subject.*<-

The falls of water that intersect in the air fill with air during their reflected motions. This occurs because, at the collision of these falls, the water bounces back and the air enters; as new water arrives, that air is submerged there and re-submerged (**fig. 101. tav. 18**). <note>Here Leonardo explains the physical process of aeration—how air bubbles are forced into water through the turbulence of a collision.</note>

### CHAPTER XIV.
->*Of water that becomes sheet-like in the air.*<-

Water that pours through a concave angle into the air will become <term>panniculata: sheet-like, forming a thin membrane or film</term>. This sheet will continue for a longer distance on that side of the angle where the water has the greatest contact with the surface. And from the opposite side the sheet

Leonardo moves from observing how streams interfere with one another to analyzing the internal forces of water—specifically acceleration, viscosity, and the formation of droplets.</meta>

LEONARDO DA VINCI 363

### CHAPTER XXI.

->*Of the same.*<-

That water is faster which descends along a more oblique line. This is proven by the twenty-seventh proposition of the second book, which says: water becomes faster the greater its declination. <note>Leonardo often cited his own "propositions"—axioms he had developed in earlier notebooks—to provide a geometric foundation for his observations on nature.</note>

### CHAPTER XXII.

->*Of the same.*<-

Where the thread of falling water is thinner, it is by its nature faster than where it is thick. The reason is that every liquid possesses a degree of <term>viscosity: the internal "stickiness" or resistance to flow</term>, and the thicker part will be more viscous. Consequently, it is with less ease that one part separates from another; once those parts that were stretched have separated, they will pull back and reform into a spherical shape at their ends. This shape will rise until it is overcome by its own weight and falls back, etc. <note>Leonardo is describing surface tension. He notices that as a stream falls and accelerates, it stretches and eventually breaks into droplets that "snap" into spheres because of the water's internal cohesion.</note>

### CHAPTER XXIII.

->*Of the same.*<-

Water that descends acquires a degree of speed and weight with every step of its descent. This is a certainty in <note>original: "filosofia"</note> philosophy <note>In the Renaissance, "natural philosophy" was the term used for what we now call science or physics.</note> and is proven by experience. Place twenty-five balls of equal weight in a tube [cannone] in such a way that they stand one above the other perpendicularly; set them in a high place and release them with a string. Stand at the base, but (**fig. 106. tav. 19**) the motion will not allow you to distinguish the pure intervals of space. And so, if the segment AB has covered a degree of descent in one unit of time, the segment BC—being faster—will have covered a greater degree of motion; likewise CD, being even faster, and so on. But know finally that if in every unit of time you have the motion of one of these balls, the motion of the very last ball also finishes in every unit of time. <note>Leonardo uses a sequence of falling balls to visualize how a continuous stream of water accelerates. As the "balls" (or water particles) fall, they move further apart because the leading ones are always moving faster than those just behind them.</note>

<vocab>water, speed, declination, viscosity, spherical shape, descent, balls, motion</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo examines the physics of falling water, explaining how a stream thins and breaks into droplets due to viscosity and how it continuously accelerates as it descends.</summary>
<keywords>acceleration, viscosity, surface tension, fluid dynamics, gravity, natural philosophy, ballistics experiment</keywords>

## CHAPTER XXIV.

*Experiment on the proportion of the intervals of the descent of water, of equal and uniform weights.*

To test the proportion of the intervals in the descent of water of equal and uniform weights, let the board MN be placed upright in a perpendicular line (fig. 107, plate 19). Let it be well-coated with earth mixed with <term>lint: original "cimatura," the fiber scraps from cloth-making used to make the clay more adhesive</term>. To this board, another board OP is joined like the cover of a book, which can be snapped shut instantly with two cords, as you see. At the top of this coated board, place the mouth of a <term>tube: original "cerbottana," a blowgun or pipe</term> plugged at the bottom and filled with balls of equal weight and shape. Once the tube and the coated board are firmly fixed, suddenly release the counterweight; the two boards will snap together, and the falling balls will all be caught in the clay. You will then be able to measure the proportion of the variety of their intervals <note>By "freezing" the balls against the board, Leonardo could measure the increasing distance between them, proving they accelerate as they fall.</note>. And if you wish to see the descent of water, do the same with millet falling from a <term>bushel: original "moggio," a dry measure</term>, and then weigh it <term>cubit: original "braccio," a unit of length roughly 23 inches</term> by cubit, and you will see which cubit-section contained more grain.

## CHAPTER XXV.

*Of the velocity of falling water.*

Where water moves faster, it becomes thinner. To define this truth, using the same example of the falling balls, I say according to the twenty-third proposition that if the descent of each ball is divided into degrees of height, it acquires a degree of velocity in every degree of its motion. Therefore, such a proportion of degrees of velocity will be a continuous arithmetic proportion, because the excesses, or differences in speed, are proportional to one another. From this, I conclude that those spaces [between the balls] were equal <note>Leonardo is likely referring to the time intervals or the increments of force, as he notes they exceed one another by "equal growth."</note>, because they always exceed and surpass one another with equal increase. For this reason, water pouring from a similar height also does the same, acquiring a degree of velocity in every degree of motion; thus, by arithmetic proportion, it continues to exceed itself degree by degree in its descent. Because of this, it is necessary that where the water moves faster, it becomes thinner.

The adversary says: if the water becomes thinner as it descends, then it does not become faster, because the thinner it becomes, the lighter it is, and thus it weighs less. <note>Leonardo often uses a rhetorical "adversary" to voice common misconceptions of Aristotelian physics which he intends to debunk.</note>

I will reply that the water pressing down from above is what pushes it. And you will tell me that because the water above is slower, it cannot [push it]...

<vocab>proportion, descent, tube, velocity, falling water, arithmetic</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo describes an ingenious experiment using a "trap" of clay-covered boards to capture falling pellets, proving that falling objects accelerate. He uses this data to explain the "thinning" of a water stream as a result of this constant acceleration.</summary>

### CHAPTER XVII.
*Figure of water falling from a large reservoir through a narrow opening.*

Water falling downward from a narrow opening, having exited from a <term>large reservoir: original "gran pelago," literally a great sea, used by Leonardo to describe a large tank or body of water</term>, will create various <term>intersections: original "intersegazioni"</term> in the air; these will be higher or lower depending on whether the cause of one is nearer to the opening than the other. The reason is that since intersection M (fig. 104, plate 18), caused by ABCD, is nearer to the exit of the reservoir than intersection H, caused by EKGF, it is generated higher up, as appears in the first figure. You shall perform the experiment of the intersection with tinted water placed in the right side of the reservoir, and it will strike the water from the left side at the bottom, etc. <note>By coloring the water on one side of the tank, Leonardo could track how the different parts of the stream crossed over each other after exiting the hole.</note>

### CHAPTER XVIII.
*That the intersections and collisions within the air are infinite.*

Try making the water exit from various kinds of <term>apertures: original "spiracoli," literally vents or breathing holes</term>—twisted and straight, long and short, <term>beveled: original "smussi"</term> outside and inside, round and square, thin and thick—and have it strike in various oppositions; in this way, you will have infinite experiments to record and from which to establish a rule.

LEONARDO DA VINCI 362

### CHAPTER XIX.

*Why falling water does not bend while falling through the air.*

If you have a <term>tube: original "canna," literally a reed or pipe</term> of water which is open at the top and bottom, and you press the bottom end so firmly against the ground that it remains without any leak or air-hole, and you fill it with water; then, with sudden speed, you lift said tube upward, you will see the water remain for a moment in the shape it held in the <term>void: original "vacuo"</term> of the tube, and then, with almost invisible speed, it forms a circle and flattens out into a round shape. If the surface is perfectly flat, the roundness of this circle will be greater in proportion to how much higher the water was in the tube than it is after it has flattened out.

If the water within the tube AB (fig. 105, plate 19) stays straight, it is no surprise, because it is supported and encased by a body harder than itself. But why does that water which is free outside the tube AB and touches the ground not bend in some direction, since it is not supported from above? You might say it is because the water inside the tube leans on and is supported by the water outside, rather than the outside water being supported by it. 

Nor does the water falling freely from the tube twist, because the <term>impact: original "percussione"</term> it makes upon the ground breaks the <term>cohesion: original "unizione," the joining or unity of the stream</term> of the oncoming water so quickly that, as it falls, it always finds itself without a foundation. Since the water finds no place to lean, it cannot bend; for a <term>physical line: original "corporea linea," referring here to the column of water</term> cannot bend if it is not held between two <term>resisting points: original "resistenti," or opposing forces</term>. <note>Leonardo is observing that for a column to buckle or bend, it needs structural resistance at both ends; because the falling water is "losing its foundation" as it hits the floor and spreads, it remains a straight vertical line.</note>

---

### CHAPTER XX.

*Of the velocity of falling water and its motion.*

Water that descends through the air in great quantity will not have an equal motion in all parts of its <term>thickness: original "grossezza," referring to the diameter of the stream</term>. Rather, that part will have a slower <term>descent: original "descenso"</term> which is furthest from the central line of its thickness. This occurs because the part furthest from the center is more mixed with the air than the part near the middle; because of this, it becomes lighter, and the lighter it is, the slower it becomes. <note>Leonardo is intuitively describing fluid friction or "drag." He perceives the outer layer of a water column as being slowed down by its interaction with the surrounding air.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains why a falling column of water maintains a straight vertical path, attributing it to a lack of structural resistance, and notes that the outer edges of a water stream fall more slowly due to air resistance.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, air resistance, cohesion, velocity, falling water, physical line, drag, Leonardo's experiments</keywords>

<term>cubits: original "braccia," a Renaissance unit of measurement approximately 23 inches</term> of descent, when the second unit has completed nine, and the third eight, and the fourth seven, and so on for all of them, so that when the first strikes, the last has not yet moved to begin its descent. But if a hard body falls, the motion of the part that strikes will be the same as the motion of the opposite part.

### CHAPTER XXXI.

->*On the impact of falling water into water.*<-

Water or any other thing that falls upon water causes the receiving water to spread out beneath the blow. Then, having surrounded and overtaken the cause of the impact, it rises above it in a <term>pyramidal shape: Leonardo is describing the "Worthington jet" or the central column of a splash</term>, and later falls back to the common level. The reason for this is that when a drop of water falls from a roof onto water, that part of the water receiving the blow cannot find space nor escape into the rest of the water with the same speed at which it is attacked; this is because it would have to lift too great a weight from itself to enter beneath such a large quantity of water. 

Therefore, needing to obey by fleeing from the displacement of the falling water—which drives it from its site—and finding the neighboring water (which does not receive the blow) is not prepared for such a flight, it cannot penetrate it so quickly. Consequently, it seeks the shortest path and rushes into the thing that offers the least resistance: the air. And that first circle surrounding the struck area, closing back in with fury because it was raised above the general surface of the water, forces the water that was fleeing upward into a pyramidal shape. If you do not believe that it is the receiving water <note>Leonardo clarifies that the splash is composed of the water already in the basin, not just the drop that fell</note> that bounces, drop a small stone onto the water; you will see that the water bounces in the same way, and not the stone.

### CHAPTER XXXII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Water falling into a channel of a width equal to the width of the falling water will create a deep <term>concavity: a hollow or depression</term> within the water's surface, because the water cannot be reflected from the banks back to the site of the <term>impact: original "percussione"</term> (fig. 111, plate 19).

He also observes how the width of a channel affects the depth of the impact crater.</summary>

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

### CHAPTER XXXIII.
*On the same subject.*

Water falling into a channel that is wider than the width of the falling water will not create too much of a hollow within the receiving water. This is due to the <term>eddies: original "retrosi," circular back-currents</term> which reflect the water back toward the hollow created by that fall. <note>Leonardo is observing how surrounding water rushes back in to fill the "hole" made by a waterfall, preventing a deep cavity from forming if there is enough space.</note>

### CHAPTER XXXIV.
*On the same subject.*

Water that is narrower than the water falling upon it will rise up and leap. The reason is that the water struck by the blow, being confined in a narrow space, cannot dissipate its escape from circle to circle, as it would in a great <term>open sea: original "pelago," meaning a vast or deep body of water</term>. Because the struck water is hemmed in by the nearby banks of the bucket or the narrow channel—which are harder and more resistant than water—it cannot spread out into many waves. Consequently, the entire force of the escape is directed upward, etc.

### CHAPTER XXXV.
*On water after its descent.*

After the descent of the water, that which was on top remains below, and the lower part is converted into the upper. This occurs because of the fourth <note>referring to a previous rule or proposition in his notes</note> which states that the upper water will have a faster descent than the lower; since the faster part in the fall penetrates the slower part and strikes the bottom first, the slower part, while descending toward the bottom, meets the faster water as it reflects upward. Being pushed by that reflection, they together mark the common course of the water, etc. <note>Leonardo is describing the "inversion" of a water stream: the top layer of a falling sheet of water often ends up at the bottom of the pool or stream bed after impact.</note>

### CHAPTER XXXVI.
*On the movement and speed of water after its descent.*

In water following its greatest descent, the lower part moves with a faster motion than the upper. This arises from the previous point: because the water retains its <term>impetus: original "impeto," the force or momentum of a moving body</term> after the descent, necessity dictates that if the velocity of the upper part is converted into the lower, the velocity of the lower part is converted into the upper.

<summary>Leonardo explains how water reacts when falling into confined spaces versus open ones, and details the "inversion" of water layers where the faster-moving top layer of a fall becomes the bottom layer upon impact.</summary>
<keywords>hydrodynamics, eddies, water impact, momentum, impetus, fluid mechanics, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI 365</header>

...to push forward. I conclude that in a certain part of the descent, the water thins out and gains such speed that the air divides it—as stated in the twenty-second proposition—and turns a <term>continuous quantity: a solid, unbroken stream</term> into a <term>discrete: broken into separate parts or droplets</term> one. However, the eye cannot discern this, and thus the descent appears continuous even though it is discrete. From this, I conclude that where water descends only a short distance, it shows a pyramidal shape; and although it weighs less as it thins out, the weight that strikes the bottom is much heavier than if that thin shape had arrived at the point of impact from its original thickness. <note>Leonardo is observing that while a thinning stream has less mass per inch, its increased velocity makes the force of the blow—the "percussion"—much stronger.</note>

Or, let us truly say: if in that part of its descent the water did not thin out to half its size at its origin, and beyond this did not become twice as fast, it would follow that it would take twice as long to fill a vessel at that thinned point than at its origin. This would be impossible, because the water poured from above in one hour could not arrive at a spot where it thinned by half in the space of two hours. Otherwise, it would be necessary for that water to turn into smoke, or truly to multiply continuously into various twists and turns; and this is not seen in experience.

And if you wanted to argue that the falling water remained of uniform thickness, the response would be found in the twenty-third proposition: that since the water is faster at the end than at the beginning—<note>original: "verbigrazia," Latin for 'for example'</note> let us say double—twice as much water would arrive at the end of the descent than was poured from the top. This cannot exist in nature. And if you wanted to say that it was of uniform size and equal speed, you would be denying the aforementioned twenty-third proposition, which is already accepted as true in philosophy <note>In Leonardo's time, "philosophy" referred to natural science and the study of physical laws.</note>.

### CHAPTER XXVI.

#### *On the shape of falling water.*

It is a necessary thing that water falling with a continuous descent through the air should also be of a pyramidal shape, even if it always exits from a (fig. 108. plate 19) pipe of the same thickness. The reason is that the quality of the descent is not of equal speed, as has been said; for that which has fallen further becomes thinner, and that which falls less does the opposite. Therefore, if you were to drop lead balls at equal intervals of time, they would not maintain equal spaces between one another; rather, the distance between them would increase as they fell, with a continuous increase in <term>geometric proportion: a mathematical relationship where values increase by a constant multiplier</term>. And the same would happen to segments of water: even if they were measured as equal at their beginning, you would find them stretched out in length at the bottom, and thickened at the top, in a continuous geometric proportion.

<summary>Leonardo explains why a stream of falling water tapers into a "pyramidal" or conical shape, arguing that gravity accelerates the water, causing it to stretch and eventually break into droplets.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, acceleration, gravity, pyramidal shape, continuity, percussion, geometric proportion</keywords>

## CHAPTER XXVII.

*On diminishing the fall of water.*

If the water NAPO (fig. 109, plate 19) has a fall of two <term>cubits: original "braccia," a traditional measurement of approximately 23 inches</term>, and one adds the water NMPQ above it, it will not have as great a fall; because by raising OP to the height of Q, it becomes nearly the same height as the bottom of the fall RA.

## CHAPTER XXVIII.

*Velocity of falling water.*

Water that falls along a line closer to the perpendicular descends faster, and delivers a greater blow and weight of itself to the place it strikes. This is proven by the twenty-first proposition of this work, which states that water is faster when it descends along a more <term>oblique: here meaning a steeper or more direct vertical path</term> line (fig. 110, plate 19).

## CHAPTER XXIX.

*How water strikes in a greater descent.*

Water strikes with greater <term>percussion: the force of impact or the "blow" delivered by a moving body</term> in a greater descent. The reason is solely that the mass has greater weight altogether in the air; it is supported only from below and is not attached at the top—rather, it is pushed downward, etc.

## CHAPTER XXX.

*Difference between the percussion of water and that of a hard body.*

The percussion made by water in continuous descent upon the place it strikes will not be of such power as that of a hard body made of a material weighing the same as that quantity of water. This is because the weight of the first stage of the water that strikes has descended the entire height of its fall, and will have made ten <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing the mathematical comparison of water's fluid impact versus a solid weight's impact on the following page.</note>

<summary>Leonardo examines the conservation of flow in lakes and the physics of falling water, noting that height and verticality increase the force of impact, though water's impact is less powerful than that of a solid object of equal weight.</summary>
<keywords>hydrodynamics, percussion, water descent, cubits, fluid velocity, channel flow</keywords>

### CHAPTER XXXVII.
->*On the same subject.*<-

<margin><unclear>R n z r :</unclear></margin>
If the fall is of the same width as the river into which it drops, the water that strikes the bottom will rebound (fig. 112. plate 20) and then fall back along every line moving away from the center of the <term>surge: original "sorgimento," the point where water splashes upward after hitting the surface</term>; and the further it descends from this surge, the more it spreads out, moving partly with the river’s course, partly toward the bottom, and partly toward the bank, and so this surge falls through a <term>triple descent: Leonardo is describing how the water disperses in three directions—downstream, toward the depths, and toward the sides</term>.

### CHAPTER XXXVIII.
->*On the same subject.*<-

<margin><unclear>r s da : 12</unclear></margin>
If the riverbed is as narrow as the falling water, the line CB (fig. 113. plate 20) will flow with as much force as AC. And the reason is that the line CB has a free escape at B; therefore, since the water that AC places into CB is fast, it must quickly clear CB to make room for the oncoming water; and by clearing it quickly, because there is no obstruction at B, the water will be more <term>swift: original "corriva," meaning fast-flowing or ready to run</term>, flowing through the line ACB than through AB; and the water M in A will be pulled as fast as AC pushes CB. And the flow MACB will be faster than MAB, which is shorter, and the cause of this is the motion and <term>impact: original "colpo," the force or "blow" delivered by the falling water</term> of AC.

### CHAPTER XXXIX.
->*On the same subject.*<-

<margin><unclear>L: n 15</unclear></margin>
Water that falls through a narrow channel into a lake will move faster the more it falls from a height greater than its own thickness. The reason for this is that the <term>impetus: the force or energy with which a body moves</term> of the water follows its course more quickly where it is less impeded, and thus the wide channel does not obstruct the water descending from the narrow channel as much; and the wider it is, the less it obstructs. Therefore, according to the twenty-third proposition—which states that water acquires a degree of velocity with every degree of descent—water that falls from a narrow channel into a wide one will have a much faster motion.

<sig>47</sig>

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, surge, triple descent, line ACB, narrow channel, twenty-third proposition, motion of water</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the flow of water falling into rivers and lakes, explaining how the absence of obstructions and the height of the fall increase the velocity of the current. He specifically details how water rebounds upon impact and disperses in multiple directions.</summary>

### CHAPTER XL.

->*On the same subject.*<-

That water will jump higher above its natural level which is closer to the point of submersion of the water falling upon the other water. And this is because the water that has the greatest <term>impetus: original "impeto," referring to the momentum or kinetic energy</term> jumps the most, and that water has the greatest impetus which is closest to its cause (fig. 114. plate 20).

### CHAPTER XLI.

->*On the barriers created by falling water.*<-

From the water that falls over the <term>weirs: original "chiuse," referring to dams, sluices, or man-made barriers in a river</term> of rivers, the straight course will be blocked in those parts where the fall is most powerful. This happens because water with a powerful fall hollows out the ground at the spot it strikes, and discharges it where its course becomes weaker, which is beneath the <term>rebound: original "moto riflesso," the upward and backward motion of water after it hits a surface</term> of the water. This rebound, being moved toward the sky, grows weaker at every stage of its motion because of the <note>The OCR shows gaps here, likely due to missing or illegible text in the original manuscript</note> . . . . of the . . . . until finally it loses all its power; and lacking power in this rebound, it allows all the heavy materials taken from the place of impact to fall beneath it. After such a flood, the water level drops and finds itself trapped between the material it previously carried and the embankment from which it descends.

### CHAPTER XLII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Of the water that descends over river weirs during floods, only that part which had a weaker and slower fall will maintain its straight course after the flood. This occurs because water that moves slowly makes a weak impact; from this it follows that it lifts little from the bottom it strikes, and consequently deposits little beneath the rebound of the water. Because of this, after such a flood, the bank of sediment remains low here, and all the falling water follows its course where the bank is lowest. Thus, the straight course of the entire river will be found here, along with the water of the weak fall.

<vocab>water, weirs, rivers, impetus, rebound, floods, impact, embankment</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains how powerful waterfalls create sediment barriers by hollowing out the riverbed and depositing debris where the water’s upward rebound loses energy. He concludes that a river's main course naturally shifts toward areas where the water's fall is weakest, as less sediment accumulates there.</summary>
<keywords>hydrodynamics, erosion, sedimentation, river weirs, fluid mechanics, impetus, riverbed formation</keywords>

### CHAPTER XLIII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Water does not follow its course after its fall in the same straight line of its descent; rather, it creates a bank from the material excavated from the place where it has struck. This happens more significantly the more direct the fall is, as is evident from the two previous <note>original: "passate," likely referring to the previous two chapters or diagrams</note> etc.

### CHAPTER XLIV.

->*What the foam of water is.*<-

Water falling from a height into other water encloses within itself a certain quantity of air, which, by means of the <term>impact: original "colpo," the physical strike of the water</term>, is submerged with it and rises quickly back up, reaching the surface it had left clothed in a thin moisture in a spherical body <note>Leonardo is describing the physical formation of a bubble</note>, spreading out in circles from the initial point of impact.

Alternatively, the water falling upon the other water departs from its place, and with various and diverse bifurcated and concave branchings, it goes about entangling and weaving itself; these branches, having struck the surface of the water through the power of their weight and the blow given by the said water, do not give the air time to escape into its own element due to the extreme speed; rather, it becomes submerged in the manner described above.

### CHAPTER XLV.

->*On the leap after the fall of the water.*<-

Water falling upon running water that is moving equally fast cannot generate a <term>rebound: original "balzo," referring here to a vertical leap or surge</term>. And this occurs because a thing that flees cannot receive a blow <note>Leonardo uses a mechanical analogy here: if the target moves at the same speed as the projectile, no impact occurs</note>.

### CHAPTER XLVI.

->*On the same subject.*<-

The <term>rebound: "balzo"</term> of the water is the leaping up of the water into the air caused by the impact; that is, the strike of the water against other water, or against the bank, or the bottom, or against objects. And this leap will be of a greater height the greater the fall of the water that caused it was. This is proven by the

->**On the same subject.**<-

The leaps of the water are of two natures—that is, composed of two causes. One is due to the <term>roundness: original "globosità," referring to the convex or irregular shapes</term> of the bottom over which the water passes; the other occurs when the parts of the water that strike the roundness of the bank rebound from the opposite shore. These quantities of water that strike then leap back to the opposite bank, and in the first wave they encounter, they press and push one another, and swelling up, they leap toward the sky. Each part flees equally from the place of impact until another wave drives it back, and then the other forward; in this way, they successively fill the surface of rivers with a lattice-like figure, always rising in the places of said impacts (fig. 115. plate 20). <note>Leonardo is describing the standing wave patterns or "diamond" ripples often seen in fast-flowing streams where currents intersect.</note>

->### CHAPTER XLVIII.<-

->**On the same subject.**<-

The leaps of the water, which rise because of the impact of water falling upon other water, will not be carried along the equal angles of its impact; rather, they will leap to the surface by the shortest path. The cause of this is the air that was submerged together with the water, which seeks to return by the shortest line to its own element <note>The "element" here is the open air above the water</note>, as is proven in the twenty-fourth [proposition] of the first [book].

->### CHAPTER XLIX.<-

That leap which is found furthest from the first leap, both in time and in distance, will also be further from its original power <note>meaning the leap will be weaker</note>. It will not behave like balls thrown upon the ground, where the smaller the leap, the smaller the base of its leap. <note>In solid mechanics, a smaller bounce typically covers a smaller horizontal distance.</note> On the contrary, water in this case does the opposite. ### CHAPTER L.

->*Experiment on leaps within a uniform channel.*<-

Let one side of a channel be made of glass and the rest of wood, and let the water that strikes it be mixed with millet or paper pulp, so that the course of the waters may be better seen through their movements. <note>Leonardo was a pioneer of flow visualization; adding small, light objects like millet allowed him to track the internal currents of the water.</note> And once you have performed the experiment of such leaps, fill the bottom with <term>sand: original "rena"</term> mixed with fine <term>gravel: original "ghiara"</term>; then have that bottom leveled, and let the water leap upon it, and observe where it removes or deposits [material]. Then observe the bank toward the wood, and look through the glass at its effects; and do this in continuous water (fig. 117, plate 20). Whatever you see the water doing in its up-and-down motion, it will also do from side to side as it strikes the banks, except for the folding back of the water that leaps, etc.

### CHAPTER LI.

->*How at times the leap is higher than the fall of the water.*<-

Water falling from a height of one <term>cubit: original "braccio," a Florentine unit of measurement approximately 58 centimeters</term> will never return to the same height, except in small droplets, which will leap quite high, because the motion of the <term>rebound: original "risaltazione"</term> will be much faster than that of the descent. For when water falls, it submerges a large quantity of air along with itself; and once the water is struck, it leaps back toward its surface with impetus, and moves almost as fast as it did during its descent. But it will not be [quite as fast] because of what has been said regarding the nature of the blow—that is, the motion of the leap will never be as fast as the descent of the object that leaped; and therefore, the subsequent leap will never be equal to the preceding one. Thus, because of this, the leap made by the water leaves the bottom where it was generated with almost the same velocity as the descent that gave birth to it; and in addition to this, a second velocity is added which increases such motion—and this is that air which was submerged along with the falling water, which, being <gloss>enveloped: original "vestita," literally "clothed"</gloss> in water...

### CHAPTER LII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Water will leap much higher than its original fall due to a violent motion. This is caused by the air trapped within the water's bubbles <note>original: "vessiche," literally bladders or blisters</note> that have risen; these <term>bubbles: original "sonagli," literally "little bells"</term> float upon the water, and when they return to the point of impact, they are submerged again by that impact. Thus, finding itself trapped between the water that pushes it and the water that strikes it, this air is compressed by such fury and impact. It immediately breaks through the water that served as its cover, and like a bolt <note>original: "saetta," an arrow or lightning bolt</note> emerging from the clouds, such air exits the water, carrying with it part of the water that previously covered it (fig. 118, plate 21).

### CHAPTER LIII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

The motion of the water's rebounding will be as much faster than the motion of the impact as the striking water is mixed with air. This is made evident by the two previous chapters.

### CHAPTER LIV.

->*Difference between the water bubbles and the leaps.*<-

Water that, with little motion, traps air just beneath its surface—which submerges with little force—returns to the surface carrying a "garment" of water. Since this is of equal weight to the air, it stays above it in the form of a hemispherical shape. But if such air is submerged with great force, it returns out of the water with force, and because of the length of the motion made underwater, it is squeezed by the weight of the water above it. It then leaps out of the water, forcefully breaks the surface, and generates a leap <note>a splash or jet</note>; thus, it does not remain floating like the first kind of bubble, which is clothed by the water's surface.

<summary>Leonardo explains how air trapped in falling water acts as a forceful agent, causing water to "leap" or splash higher than its original fall height. He distinguishes between calm surface bubbles (sonagli) and violent splashes (balzi) caused by deep-air compression.</summary>

### CHAPTER LV.

->*On the surges made by falling water.*<-

Of the <term>surges: original "bollori," referring to the churning, boiling appearance of water where a falls hits a pool</term> created by falling water reflected from the bottom of the <term>pool: original "pelago," usually implying a deep body of water or abyss</term>, part falls back onto the surface of the water, and from there creates further incident and reflected motions; and part of it turns back toward the initial fall, and there it is submerged with it, returning upwards with lateral <term>eddies: original "retrosi," back-currents or whirlpools</term>; and part falls back into the middle of the surges, spreading around the center of its fall (fig. 119. plate 21).

### CHAPTER LVI.

->*On the same subject.*<-

One asks why the surges are not continuous, given that the falls are continuous. The cause is that the water that falls and flows above is faster than that which flows below, as stated in the fourth [proposition] of this [treatise]; and when the water below crashes into some <note>original: "tomba," literally a tomb, here referring to a cavity or deep hollow in the riverbed</note>, it rises toward the surface with almost the same impetus, and sometimes it prevails and passes the water flowing above, and sometimes it is defeated by it; thus, being held in a balance of the power of motion, sometimes one prevails, and sometimes the other.

### CHAPTER LVII.

->*On a drop falling into water.*<-

The drop that rebounds is similar to the drop that strikes the "skin" of the water; it rises higher the further the <term>incident motion: the downward path of the falling object before impact</term> begins from above; because where there is a greater <term>descent: original "descenso"</term>, there is a greater <term>percussion: the force of the impact</term>, and consequently a greater leap.

### CHAPTER LVIII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Water that rebounds in the form of two drops close to one another is due to a combined cause. The reason is that when the water is struck, it leaps up, and the "opened" water closes back in, and the wave restricts itself toward the place where the drop was divided, and having all struck into the water that

# BOOK SIX
## ON THE BREACHES MADE BY WATER.

---

### CHAPTER I.

->*For what reason water breaches the bank and the bed.*<-

Water does not break anything except where it finds resistance, and where it strikes by means of an impact. This condition is evident through the experience of all things that break, which do not break except through resistance and impact. <note>Leonardo applies a general law of physics—that force requires resistance to have an effect—specifically to the way rivers erode their surroundings.</note>

### CHAPTER II.

->*On the same subject.*<-

If water does not wear away where it does not strike, and does not strike where it finds no resistance, then that object which is the primary cause of breaking the straightness of the water’s established course will be the most worn away and removed by that water.

### CHAPTER III.

->*On the same subject.*<-

That part of the <term>bed: the bottom of a river or stream</term> and the bank which opposes the straight course of the waters with more <term>right angles: perpendicular or 90-degree angles</term> will be most damaged by the running waters. This happens because that impact will be more powerful when it occurs between more equal angles. <note>Leonardo is noting that a head-on collision between the water and the bank causes more erosion than a glancing or oblique flow.</note>

### CHAPTER IV.

->*Where water creates lesser or greater concavity, or breach.*<-

The more the angle of the water’s incidence is formed between more <term>unequal angles: angles that are not 90 degrees, such as acute or obtuse angles</term>, the concavity beneath the angle of incidence will be of less 

<vocab>breach, bank, bed, resistance, impact, strike, straightness, incidence, concavity</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo opens Book Six by establishing the physical principles of hydraulic erosion, arguing that water only damages banks and riverbeds where it meets resistance, particularly when striking surfaces at a perpendicular angle.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, erosion, fluid mechanics, river engineering, resistance, angles of incidence, physics of water</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 377 regarding the relationship between the speed of water, the angle of its impact, and the resulting erosion of riverbanks and beds. It completes Chapter IV and proceeds through Chapters V to VIII of the sixth book.</meta>

<page-num>378</page-num>
<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

concavity. For example, if the water’s impact occurs along line DE against bank AC, the angles ABD and DBC are very unequal; therefore, the impact is weak at point B, the angle of incidence. Consequently, it does not bounce upward, does not strike forcefully, and does not hollow out the bed as much (fig. 120, plate 21). <note>Leonardo is observing that when water hits a bank at a shallow, glancing angle, it lacks the force to "dig" into the riverbed compared to a direct, perpendicular hit.</note>

### CHAPTER V.

->On the same subject.<-

Where the water has less motion, it does not wear away its <term>bed: the bottom of the river</term>. This happens because it creates less impact and gathering <note>original: "congregazione." Leonardo uses this to describe the way water piles up or concentrates its force against a surface.</note> against its bed. On the contrary, where there is less motion, it raises its bed, as will be explained in the seventh [chapter]. <note>Leonardo notes that slow-moving water allows silt and sand to settle, actually making the river shallower.</note>

### CHAPTER VI.

->On the same subject.<-

Where the water is faster, it wears away more of the bed that confines it. This happens because the bed offers resistance to the water it supports, and where the water moves faster, it creates a greater impact against that resistance.

### CHAPTER VII.

->On the same subject.<-

Where the water is most constricted, it wears away its bed the most. This is proven by the . . . <note>The text here has a small gap, likely referring to a specific axiom or "proposition" from an earlier book.</note> of the second [book], which states: where water is most constricted, it becomes faster; and according to the previous [rule], faster water wears away its bed more. <note>This is a foundational principle of fluid dynamics: as a channel narrows, the water must speed up to move the same volume through, increasing its erosive power.</note>

### CHAPTER VIII.

->On the same subject.<-

The deepest point across the width of the river is always found between the <term>incident motion: the incoming flow of water striking a surface</term> and the <term>reflected motion: the water bouncing back after the strike</term>. This happens because when the reflected motion meets the incident motion, an impact of water is generated. As the water rises, it falls back onto the bed, and there it continually hollows it out.

<vocab>water, incident motion, reflected motion, impact, bed, bank, river, incidence</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains how the velocity and constriction of a river dictate where the bed is hollowed out by erosion or raised by sedimentation. He specifically identifies that the meeting point of incoming and bouncing water currents creates the deepest parts of a river.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, erosion, riverbeds, velocity, fluid dynamics, angles of incidence, sedimentation, water currents</keywords>

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

### CHAPTER IX.

->*On the same subject.*<-

The river always rises beneath the incident motion <note>original: "moto incidente." This refers to the downward or incoming strike of water against a surface.</note>, and beneath the reflected motion <note>original: "moto riflesso." This refers to the water bouncing back or rebounding after an impact.</note> the riverbed restores its height. This occurs because the bed is worn away more under the incident motion because the water is faster there, as stated in the sixth [proposition] of this [book]. And under the reflected motion, the water drops what it is carrying because it slows down, according to the second [proposition] of the seventh [book]. <note>Leonardo is explaining a cycle: fast water striking the bed digs a hole, but as that water bounces back and slows down, it loses the energy needed to carry silt, which then settles and rebuilds the bed elsewhere.</note>

### CHAPTER X.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Water strikes its bed more forcefully where it falls from a larger and higher wave. This is proven by the twenty-ninth [proposition] of the previous [book], which says: water in a greater descent produces a greater impact, and consequently, it will cause greater erosion <note>original: "rottura," literally a breaking or rupture of the soil.</note>.

### CHAPTER XI.

->*On the same subject.*<-

The maximum height of a wave will not wear away the bed beneath it; for it barely touches it, since everything exerts weight only along the line of its motion; hence we say that such a wave moves toward the air, which flees from its impact, and its weight is directed toward the air; and if there is indeed any gathering <note>original: "congregazione." Leonardo uses this term to describe the concentration or "piling up" of water force.</note> of water there, it will have little force and will wear away that bed very little. <note>Leonardo observes that at the very peak of a wave, the energy and "weight" of the water are moving upward into the air rather than pressing down into the riverbed.</note>

### CHAPTER XII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Where the river channel is more slanted <note>original: "obliquo." In this context, it refers to the steepness of the incline.</note>, it deepens the bed of its river, causing the same quantity of water to occupy less width. This is proven by the twenty-first [proposition] of the second [book], which says: where the river channel has a greater slope <note>original: "declinazione."</note>, the water becomes faster; and by the sixth [proposition] of this [book]: where it is faster, it wears away the bed more.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes how the "incident" and "reflected" motions of water affect the riverbed, noting that faster water and steeper slopes increase erosion, while slower, rebounding water allows for sediment to settle.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, riverbeds, erosion, wave mechanics, incident motion, reflected motion, water velocity, sedimentation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s systematic study of hydraulic forces, specifically focusing on how the composition of the riverbed and the clarity of the water influence erosion and the deepening of river channels.</meta>

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

### CHAPTER XIII.
->*On the same subject.*<-

A body with a denser and harder surface will cause the object striking it to rebound with greater power and speed; for this reason, water falling upon gravel <note>original: "ghiara," referring to a bed of small stones or shingle.</note> mixed with coarse sand <note>original: "sabbione."</note> will create a greater and more rapid depth there than if it fell upon soft, simple silt <note>original: "litta," a fine, soft mud or clay.</note>. This is because when water falls upon gravel, it regains a swift and powerful leap, and it performs a greater scraping <note>original: "radimento," literally a shaving or raking away of material.</note> and lifting of material at the first resistance to its leap.

### CHAPTER XIV.
->*Which water most breaks the bank and the bed.*<-

Turbid water <note>Water made cloudy or thick by suspended silt and mud.</note> does more damage to the banks than clear water, and more at the bottom than at the top; because it is heavier, according to the twenty-second [proposition] of the first [book], and consequently faster, according to the twenty-eighth [proposition] of the second [book], and it creates a greater impact <note>original: "percussione."</note> against what opposes it, wearing it away according to the sixth [proposition] of this [book].

### CHAPTER XV.
->*On the same subject.*<-

The shorter the river, the greater the depth it will carve into its bed; and it will perform less excavation <note>original: "cavamento."</note> of its bed the longer its course becomes. This is proven by the sixty-fourth [proposition] of the second [book], which says: the shorter the course of the river, the greater its velocity will be, and the longer it is, the slower it will be. <note>Leonardo identifies a direct correlation between the steepness/shortness of a mountain stream and its power to erode compared to a long, slow-moving river in a plain.</note>

### CHAPTER XVI.
->*On the same subject.*<-

All rivers continuously lower their beds, except where they are dammed up <note>original: "ringorgati," referring to places where the flow is obstructed, causing the water to swell or back up.</note>, because there they do the opposite; and the reason is that the friction <note>original: "confregazione," the rubbing or grinding action of the water against the earth.</note> of the current against its bed is always consuming and scraping away said bed.

<summary>Leonardo explains how the hardness of a riverbed increases the "rebound" force of water, leading to faster erosion, and posits that shorter, faster rivers and heavier, turbid water are the most destructive forces acting upon a landscape.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, erosion, riverbed mechanics, turbidity, water velocity, sediment, friction</keywords>

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

### CHAPTER XVII.

*Why fast-moving water, upon meeting slow-moving water, breaks the bank, and its remedy.*

Where water moves with fury and strikes against water of little movement, it encounters resistance within it and does not continue its straight path; instead, it turns toward the sides and strikes or makes an impact on the bank, widening and wearing it away. Therefore, level the course of the water by lowering it where it was too high, and fill the [eroded] area.

---

### CHAPTER XVIII.

*Why rivers change their beds, and often remove and deposit material in different places.*

The movement of water always seeks to wear away its support, and the part that is softer offers less resistance, leaving its place and creating various hollows. There, the water swirls with various eddies <note>original: "retrosi," referring to backcurrents or vortices that swirl against the main flow.</note>, wearing away and excavating while widening said hollow; striking against the new banks, it rebounds, striking the shores, wearing and gnawing away until the opposition collapses <note>original: "ruina."</note>. It changes its course by means of these same collapses, carrying the lighter earth along with these currents, which it then discharges in calmer places. By raising the riverbed, it diminishes the volume and force of the water, directing its fury toward the opposite side; and if it finds a bank, it gnaws at it and undermines <note>original: "scalza," literally "to unshoe," a technical term for eroding the base or foundation of a structure or bank.</note> its foundations until, with great collapses, it reveals new lands. If it finds a plain, it occupies it, and by removing and excavating material, it creates a new bed. And if it finds buried stones, it uncovers them and then undermines them; because of their great size, these often offer resistance to the impetuous current, which, struck back by the opposing stones, rebounds to the opposite side, breaking and damaging the facing banks.

---

### CHAPTER XIX.

->**On the same subject.**<-

The course of the smaller volume of water obeys the greater volume of the great floods <note>original: "diluvii."</note>; it changes its course, joining with the larger flow, and ceases its excavation beneath the banks. This experience is seen in the Po <note>The longest river in Italy, which Leonardo studied extensively for his canal and irrigation projects.</note>, which, when its water is low, often flows through transverse channels, drawn by the lower places, and directing itself toward them, it takes its course, and...
