...strikes the bank at its foundations; it hollows them out and causes great collapses <note>original: "ruine."</note>. And when the river flows in full flood, the smaller volume of water, which previously moved crosswise and struck and hollowed out the area under the bank, leaves its course, because it is pulled along by the company of the larger volume; and as it follows the direction of its bed, it does not damage the banks.

### CHAPTER XX.
->*Concerning winding rivers and their collapses.*<-

The winding courses of water <note>original: "serpeggianti," literally snake-like or meandering.</note>, which are caused by the rebounding impacts of the water against the banks, will hollow out the riverbed beneath them more than in any other part. At these points of impact, the river will reach a very great depth, and the water that swirls within that depth is the cause of the excavations and the collapse of the battered banks. It is proven, clearly seen, and known that the waters striking the riverbank act similarly to balls struck against walls: they bounce off at angles similar to the angle of impact and go on to hit the opposite wall. Thus, these waters, having made their first impacts on the bank, rebound to the opposite shores, and there they cause a great impact and hollowing; this happens because there is a greater gathering of waters in that place.

The reason is that water rebounding from one bank to the other hollows out the part of the riverbed beneath it. The other water in the river, which cannot be contained in this new hollow, remains pushed and somewhat repelled along the straight path of the river; and because its means of escape is restricted, it returns to its natural course. That is to say, as the riverbed is deeper beneath the winding paths created by the aforementioned impacts of the water, this second stream of water—which had taken an accidental diversion—resumes its natural course and falls into the low places of the river. It then strikes the bank at the same spot where the impacts from the rebounds occur. Since that bank is battered by two different impacts, a larger hollow is carved out there: one impact strikes the upper part of the bank, while the other, having a greater downward slope, gnaws at and undermines <note>original: "discalza," literally "to unshoe," a technical term for eroding the base or foundation of a bank.</note> the bank at its base. This is the cause of the aforementioned collapses.

### CHAPTER XXI.
->*On the same subject.*<-

The entry of rivers into other rivers generates the first twists and turns <note>original: "tortuosità," referring to the winding path of the river.</note> of the rivers (fig. 121. plate 21). This arises because, in the collision of rivers, the more powerful water strikes the less powerful one, and with its eddies <note>original: "retrosi," vortices or back-currents.</note> it enters...

### CHAPTER XXII.
->*Of the same.*<-

The winding courses of major rivers are greater the closer they are to the confluence <note>original: "introito," literally the "entrance" where a tributary meets a larger river.</note> of a smaller river into the larger one. The reason is that the impacts of the river against the bank caused by such a confluence are more powerful at the beginning than at the end; and consequently, they excavate the bank more near the said confluence than elsewhere, and from this arises greater winding.

### CHAPTER XXIII.
->*Of the same.*<-

If a meandering river is entirely removed from its original bed and placed into a straight channel, it is necessary that the tributary rivers flowing into it from both sides change: on one side they will lengthen by as much as they are shortened on the opposite side. That which gains in length loses in velocity <note>original: "velocità." Leonardo observes that a longer path reduces the gradient and thus the speed of the water.</note>, and this velocity is transferred to the shortened river. Thus, one gives greater velocity to the larger river and less to the smaller rivers that flow into it, and you will achieve the same effect as the same proposition.

### CHAPTER XXIV.
->*Of the same.*<-

Ensure that the smaller rivers enter into the larger ones at acute angles. The utility of this, as with the previous point, is that the current of the larger river bends the entrance of the smaller river and does not allow it to strike the opposite bank.

### CHAPTER XXVI.

->*Of the same.*<-

The elbows <note>original: "gombiti," referring to the sharp bends or meanders in a river's path.</note> formed by the riverbanks are wiped away by great river floods, because the maximum current pushes the water in a straight path; but as the water recedes, it resumes its winding course, which reflects from one bank to the other, and it is this decrease in water that carves away the riverbank. But in this diminished depth, the water does not move with a uniform flow; for the main current leaps from one bank to the opposite bank, and the edges of the water bordering the bank move with a very short <note>meaning slow or sluggish</note> course (**fig. 122, table 21**).

### CHAPTER XXVII.

->*Of the same.*<-

The turbulences <note>original: "globulenze," likely referring to the bubbling or churning of the water.</note> found in the small eddies <note>original: "gioretti," small circular currents.</note> created by the bends in the banks are derived from the back-eddies <note>original: "retrosi," or vortices.</note> of rivers, which extend with their revolutions between the opposing concavity and convexity of the riverbanks. From this, small branches <note>small secondary channels</note> are born (**fig. 123, table 22**) interspersed between the shoals <note>original: "secche," shallow sandbars.</note> and the banks, situated opposite the concave parts of the riverbank.

### CHAPTER XXVIII.

The fine gravel, then the sand, then the silt <note>original: "litta," fine sediment or mud.</note>, and finally the wood, roots, and leaves will remain in the place that will be described in the seventh section.

...so that it drives it out; therefore, it falls disunited and thin, almost in a perpendicular line. The air, which is found within this disunity and possesses almost equal weight, cannot escape quickly enough, so that the weight placed above it, together with the impact, submerges it. But because air cannot be separated from its own element without violence, once it has submitted to the fury of the impact and the weight, it rises immediately and returns in round bubbles <note>original: "sonagli," literally "little bells," describing the hemispherical bubbles formed on a water surface.</note> to the surface of the water near the place of impact. Since it does not move far from the first impact, it does not damage the banks of its hollow <note>original: "fossa," referring to the pit or basin formed by the water's fall.</note>. 

But when the impetuous river, swollen <note>original: "insuperbito," literally "made proud," a poetic way to describe a river emboldened by floodwaters.</note> by new rains, flows between its banks, it falls with fury into the deep basins <note>original: "pelaghi," referring to deep pools or stretches of water.</note> below. It no longer falls calmly as it did before when it rained over the other water mixed with air; instead, being united and powerful, it strikes and opens the struck water all the way to the hard bottom. By undermining <note>original: "scalzando," literally "unshoeing" or stripping away the foundation.</note> and removing the covered stones, it creates a new barrier against itself with the banks of the hollow already made. Striking and moving against this, the water is overcome and divides above the said impact into two contrary courses which depart in two semicircles; by gnawing and consuming every opposition, it widens the hollow into a round shape. 

To put it better, when the rivers are full, the waterfalls are less high; this is because, as the mass of water strikes the lower places, the water following the impact does not escape with the same fury as the falling water. This being the case, it creates resistance, and through this resistance the water level rises, making the fall shorter. Thus, it does not trap as much air, because the lower parts of that fall are hardly separated from the rest of the water. Since this is so, very little air will enter; for this reason, the impact and weight of the water has nothing to turn it upward, and its blow goes united all the way to the bottom, removing the earth that surrounds and covers the stones, and increasing the depth (**fig. 125, plate 22**).

### CHAPTER XXX.

->*Where two running waters unite and hollow out the bottom.*<-

When there are two currents of water in the courses of rivers, or two equal branches of water that begin far from one another and converge at a place where they strike together, they will rise upward after such an impact. The bottom beneath them will be little worn away, because as the waters rise, they move away from the bed. Then, having acquired weight by rising, they fall back down as they disperse or separate; in falling back, they scrape the bottom. Therefore, because of this impact, which beats and scrapes the bottom with its motion, a great depth will be created there, and this occurs in the large currents of rivers. **A** (**fig. 126, plate 22**) is the place of the aforementioned impact, where you can also see that the elevation of the bottom, which is little worn, is that which always...

### CHAPTER XXXI.

->*Of the river that turns at a right angle, and its erosion during floods.*<-

This water, in its general descent, turns at a right angle; but during floods, it flows straight, and the impact is so powerful that, by scouring, it carries stones with its course, rolling them up over the bank of other stones. Thus, as the water follows its leap beyond its surface, it leaves the pushed stones at the extremity of the mounded opposing hill. But once the depth or the floods subside, the water can no longer pass over the newly formed hill of gravel <term>ghiara: coarse gravel or shingle typical of riverbeds</term>; therefore, it turns back into its original course by the fall of the other water which overflows from above, and creates such a hollow in the place where it falls. AC is the water that flows, B is the mounded rock, C is the right angle, CD is the fall (fig. 127. plate 23.).

### CHAPTER XXXII.

->*The erosion of water floods in narrow valleys.*<-

When the general course of rivers is restricted by the narrowing of valleys and by the cuttings of mountains, then the water will gorge <note>original: "s’ingorgherà," describing water backing up or swelling due to an obstruction.</note> in the wide part and will create great movement through the said narrowness of the mountains; and having passed the middle of said narrowness, it will create a great cavity <note>original: "concavità," referring here to a deep pool scoured out by the increased velocity of the water.</note>. Upon returning to the wide part, the depth will decrease in proportion to the increase in width. And the aforementioned depth will diminish after the leap of the water, because it will fill with gravel beneath the greatest elevation of the jump of the aforementioned waters.

### CHAPTER XXXIII.

->*The erosion caused by falling water.*<-

If the fall of the water is of the same width as the river, the water that strikes the bottom will rebound, and then fall back along every line, starting from the center of the surging; and the further it descends from such a submergence, the more it spreads out, and part of it moves along the course of the river. Wherefore it must necessarily create two motions, both of which cause great wearing away of the foot of the bank <term>argine: a levee or riverbank, often reinforced</term>; because that which descends from the height of the surging throws itself toward the bottom, and because such a descent is oblique...

<meta>This page continues Leonardo's analysis of how water erodes the banks and beds of rivers. <page-num>387</page-num>
<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI 387</header>

...it acquires a motion toward the base of the bank; and because this descent partly follows the general flow of the river, this plunging action <term>sommergimento: the action of water diving beneath the surface to strike the bottom</term> descends with a triple motion: one downward, another toward the bank, and the third toward the current of the river. All three consume the foot of the bank with the greatest scouring action that can be produced by such force. For if the river were to run straight alongside such a bank, it might encounter some stone that would protect a section of the bank behind it. But this specific motion moves downward toward the bottom and against the bank, and down along the course of the river, so that every stone is struck from three different motions and sides; therefore, it is inevitable, if the bank is erodible, that it will collapse (fig. 128. plate 23).

### CHAPTER XXXIV.

->*On the same subject.*<-

The fall of water, or the descending motion of a fall transverse <note>meaning a fall that runs across the width of the river rather than parallel to the bank</note> to the river, is what breaks the bank, as shown at AB (fig. 129. plate 23); and the said motion breaks at F, and the removed earth is deposited at S, the smaller particles at H, and the coarse earth at G, as well as the sand.

### CHAPTER XXXV.

->*On the same subject.*<-

The water that falls from the bank AB (fig. 130. plate 23) into the running water MN will deepen the bed where it falls, all the way to the lowest point of the place where it falls—namely, from AB to CD.

### CHAPTER XXXVI.

->*On the same subject.*<-

The falls that waters make from their banks always consume the base of the bank and cause them to collapse from their foundations. This is proven: let the height of the bank be AC (fig. 131. plate 23) from which the water AN falls, striking and consuming the point of impact <term>percussione: the physical point where the falling water hits the surface or the bed</term> MAC. The center of the impact is where the reflected motions <term>moti riflessi: the rebounding currents that swirl back after the initial strike</term> NMO and NCB divide; these motions consume the bank from every side, concentrated by the revolution of their swirling movements <note>original: "circonvolubili," describing the vortex-like or spiral motion of the water</note>. Thus, when the banks find their supports consumed, they collapse on the side where the supports are missing.

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

### CHAPTER XXXVII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Water that falls from a height creates a deep abyss <term>pelago: a profound pool or chasm carved into a riverbed</term>, which will continually grow, and its banks will often collapse back into it. The reason for this is that the water falling onto the water below—due to the speed of the impact and its weight—forces the surface water to give way and penetrates all the way to the bottom. Because of this impact, and because of the air that is submerged along with the water during the fall, it rises again and leaps upward through various paths. These paths create a circular churning <note>original: "ribollimento," literally a "re-boiling" or turbulent frothing</note>, and the impact of the water against the bank will also be circular; thus, the surrounding banks will be gnawed away and consumed in circles.

### CHAPTER XXXVIII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

The fall of water that then runs over planking <term>tavolati: wooden platforms or dams used in Renaissance engineering to manage water flow</term> greatly scours the bottom at the end of such planks. This happens because as the current of the water reaches the edge of these planks, it falls and lifts material from the bed; the longer it falls, the deeper it scours, because the force of the fall is more powerful in greater depths than in shallower ones (fig. 132. plate 24).

### CHAPTER XXXIX.

->*On the meeting of the water where it scours.*<-

If the water falls from OS (fig. 133. plate 24) it intersects at X, and where it opens into its widest aperture, it creates the greatest depth; it then intersects again at U, and as it opens, it deposits gravel <note>original: "ghiara," referring to the coarse stones and shingle found in riverbeds</note>, and creates depth, etc.

### CHAPTER XL.

->*On the same subject.*<-

When the currents of the water are equal, the revolutions <note>referring to vortices or eddies</note> made where the waters meet travel in a straight line, as proven by the eleventh proposition of the fourth book. But if these currents in the water are not equal, these encounters will carry the eddies of that water toward the bank of the weaker current; there, it bores <note>original: "trivellando," describing a drilling or auguring action</note> underneath with its doubled motions...

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

...that is, straight and revolving, go on hollowing out the bank at the bottom, where the upper parts eventually collapse over their missing foundations, and are once again consumed by that same churning <note>original: "revertigine," a swirling or vortex-like motion of the water</note>.

### CHAPTER XLI.

#### ->Excavation by the eddy of double power.<-

It is possible that the depth beneath the main current is less than at the front or the sides; let BCN (fig. 134, plate 24) be the current, and A is an eddy <term>retroso: a back-current or vortex that moves against the main flow</term> composed of double power according to the thirty-eighth rule of the fourth book. This is because, in addition to its rotation, it strikes against the bank, leaps into the air, and falls back down upon the other water; it penetrates that water and strikes it, hollowing out the bottom with sudden depth. This happens because, added to the impact, there is the drilling <term>trivellamento: a boring or auger-like action</term> caused by the aforementioned rotation, which tears away and carries off whatever the impact loosens. This action becomes all the more powerful the more "twisted" it is, and the more forceful its method of moving and carrying away the soil, leaving behind a great depth. The eddy B will create a long but not sudden depth.

### CHAPTER XLII.

#### ->On the same subject.<-

The eddies generated in a bank that has been pulled back to provide greater width to a canal will be the cause of the ruin of that same bank. This is proven by the same reasoning as the eighteenth rule of the fourth book: because where the water widens, its depth also becomes shallower, and a sudden current is generated. As this current throws itself against the widened bank and strikes it, it must necessarily drill into and deepen the foot <note>the "foot" refers to the underwater base or foundation of the embankment</note> of the bank, causing it to collapse.

### CHAPTER XLIII.

#### ->On the same subject.<-

Water that moves through a narrow channel and enters a wider part of it immediately becomes thinner and faster, because it finds a greater slope <note>original: "declinazione," referring to the gradient or the "fall" of the water surface</note>. Thus it moves with force, and the course it has begun directs itself toward the foot of its bank and strikes it. Following this impact, it turns upward, and with a revolving motion <term>revertiginoso: a turbulent, swirling movement</term>, it goes on rasping away the foundation of the bank.

<summary>Leonardo explains how eddies act like mechanical drills (trivellamento) to undermine riverbanks, specifically noting that widening a canal can paradoxically increase erosion by creating faster, shallower currents that strike the foundations.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, eddies, erosion, embankments, fluid mechanics, Leonardo da Vinci, vortex, river engineering</keywords>

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

...until it returns to the top, and such scraping <note>original: "raspamento," referring to the abrasive action of water against the soil</note> will be in the shape of a ship, starting and ending narrow, and in the middle deep and wide (fig. 135. plate 24).

### CHAPTER XLIV.

#### ->Of the same subject.<-

Of two eddies <term>retroso: a back-current or vortex that moves against the main flow</term> formed after the bank is widened, the first will cause greater drilling <term>trivellamento: a boring or auger-like action caused by a vortex</term> and depth than the second. This is proven by the nineteenth proposition of the fourth book, which says: of two eddies formed after the bank is widened, the first is more powerful (fig. 136. plate 24).

### CHAPTER XLV.

#### ->Of the same subject.<-

Where the eddies generated in one of the widened banks are struck by the columnar wave <term>onda colonnale: the main, powerful jet or "column" of the current</term> of the opposite bank, they will hollow out and deepen that bank even more. The reason is because the eddies remain straighter and acquire double power (fig. 137. plate 25).

### CHAPTER XLVI.

#### ->Of the same subject.<-

If the course of the river is narrowed on one of its sides, the eddies generated between the bank and the columnar wave will be the cause of the undermining <term>scalzamento: the erosion of the foundation or base of a structure</term> and ruin <term>ruina: the structural collapse of the embankment</term> of that same bank. And the reason is that when the water is narrowed, it acquires weight and speed; and where the water is faster, its eddies are of greater power (fig. 138. plate 24).

### CHAPTER XLVII.

#### ->Of the same subject.<-

If the banks are equally narrowed on each side of the current, and opposite one another, then the columnar waves will intersect <term>intersegazione: the crossing or intersection of two currents</term>, and after such intersection they will descend to the ruin of their bank (fig. 139. plate 24).

<summary>Leonardo explains how riverbanks are eroded by eddies and "columnar waves," noting that narrowing a channel increases water speed and the destructive power of its vortices. He identifies specific patterns of erosion, such as ship-shaped hollows and the intersection of currents that lead to the collapse of embankments.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, eddies, erosion, riverbanks, columnar wave, undermining, water velocity, Leonardo da Vinci, fluid dynamics</keywords>

### CHAPTER XLVIII.
->*Of the same subject.*<-

All the eddies <term>retroso: a back-current or vortex that moves against the main flow</term> caused by the impacts <term>percussione: the striking force of the water against a surface</term> of the water against obstacles in the bank, where they are created and whirl around, will create a greater or lesser depth in those eddies. And the reason is that eddies of greater power also create a greater impact in their opposition, and a greater drilling <term>trivellamento: a boring or auger-like action caused by a vortex</term> even in the bottom.

---

### CHAPTER XLIX.
->*Of the same subject.*<-

Where the eddies—formed by the impact of the water against obstacles in the bed that rise above the water—gather, or are carried either to the middle of the current or to the sides, they will perform their drilling or hollowing <term>cavamento: the process of the water digging out the earth</term> according to the proportion of their power, as was proven in the previous section.

---

### CHAPTER L.
->*Of the same subject.*<-

If the widenings or narrowings of the channel, or the obstacles of the bank or in the middle, are not made or placed opposite one another, then the depth of such a river will be uneven <note>original: "fia zoppa," literally "will be lame," indicating an asymmetrical or unbalanced flow</note> (fig. 140 plate 24).

---

### CHAPTER LI.
#### *Hollowing out of the water after obstacles surrounded by water.*

If a rock <term>scoglio: a large stone or submerged reef</term> that divides the course of the water is covered by the flowing waters only on its lower parts, the water that passes over the top will rise upward and, acquiring weight, will fall at the rock’s feet; the place struck by its fall will take on the nature of a well <term>pozzo: a deep, cylindrical cavity carved into the riverbed by the water's force</term>, and will cause the water to turn within said well, and again after this, by hollowing it out, it causes it to fall back (fig. 141. plate 25).

<summary>Leonardo explains how vortices (eddies) act as mechanical drills that deepen the riverbed, especially when water strikes obstacles or falls over submerged rocks to create deep "wells." He emphasizes that asymmetrical river features result in uneven depths.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, eddies, erosion, riverbeds, vortices, obstacles, fluid dynamics, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

## CHAPTER LII.
### *On the inequality of riverbeds.*

The inequality <note>or unevenness</note> of the riverbed arises from the bends in the bank, or from material that has fallen from said bank at its base. Earth falls from the bank and stops at point A (fig. 142 plate 25); the water B strikes A and divides; part of it turns back, forming the back-current <term>retroso: a vortex or eddy that moves against the main flow</term> N, and part of it moves toward C.
The water BC has an equal velocity in its current on side C, and the two back-currents—one moving from top to bottom, and one of the water at N—come to a halt; thus, beneath the current, the water becomes slow from the middle forward at the start because the depth is shallow. The current is as fast below as it is above, and because of this, it uncovers the coarse gravel <term>ghiara: large river stones or heavy gravel</term>, then the sand, then the mud, and finally the leaves <note>Leonardo is describing the process of sediment sorting, where faster water carries away lighter materials like leaves and silt, leaving only the heavy stones behind</note>.

---

## CHAPTER LIII.
### *How a stone placed in a channel can be the cause of the destruction of its bed.*

A stone placed on the level and flat beds of flowing rivers will be the cause of its unevenness and destruction. It is proven that when a thing falling from high to low strikes an object harder than itself, a leap <note>original: "balzo," here referring to a hydraulic jump or a sudden surge in the water's path</note> is immediately generated, which will be of greater height the greater its descent was. Therefore, the larger a stone placed beneath the surface of flowing rivers, the greater the impact made by the water falling from its height onto the riverbeds; because of this, a larger cavity is generated in the place struck by that water, and after the first impact, many rebounds <term>risaltamenti: secondary surges or ripples caused by the initial disturbance</term> are created. The further these are from the first, the larger they will be in size, but the lesser in power, as appears in ABC (fig. 143 plate 25).

---

## CHAPTER LIV.
### *How the root of a plant can be the cause of the ruin of the opposite bank.*

That bank which thrusts out the bulk of its growing plant against the waves of rapid rivers shall be the cause of the destruction of the opposite bank... <note>The text ends here mid-sentence, suggesting the explanation continues on the following page. Leonardo likely intends to explain how the plant acts as a deflector, shunting the water's force toward the other side of the river.</note>

**Vocabulary:**
hydraulics, rivers, bank, current, bed, percussion <note>impact</note>, back-current, gravel
</margin>

<summary>Leonardo explains how obstacles like fallen earth or stones create turbulence and "leaps" in water, which sort riverbed sediment by weight and erode deep cavities. He also notes how vegetation on one bank can deflect the current's power, leading to the destruction of the opposite bank.</summary>

<sig>50</sig>

...opposite bank. The reason for this effect is that the water that runs through rivers is always bouncing from bank to bank, as is proven in the second <note>Leonardo frequently refers to the "books" or sections of his treatise on water; here he likely refers to his second book on water's motion</note>, and if anything grows beyond the ordinary on its bank, many flow lines <term>lineamenti d'acqua: the paths or "filaments" of moving water particles</term> gather there, and united they leap in bulk to the opposite bank, drawing with them the other lines they find along the way. Having reached the embankment, they erode and ruin it there, and in that place new lines are generated which bounce back and damage the other bank; and so, step by step, eddies <term>retrosi: vortices or back-currents that move against the main flow</term> and various depths begin to form, and from this it follows that straight rivers become winding (fig. 144. plate 25).

### CHAPTER LV.

### *Remedy for a similar defect.*

If the water strikes at the opposite point A (fig. 145. plate 25), it will bounce back to the embankment B, as seen in figure 144. If you wish to remedy this, remove the obstruction where the water strikes at MA; or, indeed, you may place an identical object opposite A, where the water can also strike, as seen in ED. Then the courses of the bouncing waters will meet and break one another in the middle of the river at point F. Thus, the impact <term>percussione: the physical force of the water hitting the bank</term> of the water, having first been weakened by its own breaking <note>Leonardo is describing "destructive interference," where two opposing currents cancel each other's energy</note>, will not be able to cause damage to the opposite bank.

### CHAPTER LVI.

### *On breaking the motion of water.*

The motion of water is most permanently countered by other motions. If the line AN breaks at G, it creates the line NM, which will strike at F and break that course. But do not make it so powerful that the surge <note>original: "balzo," meaning a leap or hydraulic jump</note> causes harm <term>nocumento: physical damage or injury to the structure</term>; or rather, you should make other slanted barriers <term>obliquità: structures built at an angle to the current</term> like those in HR that restore the flow, as seen in PO and ST (fig. 146. plate 25).

### CHAPTER LVII.

### *On dividing the power of a river.*

If the excessive size of rivers spoils and breaks the banks <term>lidi: the shores or edges of the water</term>, such rivers—since they cannot be diverted to other places—must be broken up into frequent rivulets <term>rivi: small streams or secondary channels</term>.

<summary>Leonardo explains how the "bouncing" motion of water between banks creates meanders in straight rivers and proposes engineering solutions, such as using opposing currents or slanted barriers to dissipate the water's erosive energy.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, erosion, meanders, river engineering, bank protection, eddies, fluid dynamics</keywords>

<page-num>394</page-num>
<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

### CHAPTER LVIII.

#### *On the method of straightening rivers so they do not break their banks.*

Because the straighter a river is, the faster its flow becomes, and it erodes forcefully, wearing away both the bank and the riverbed. For such rivers, it is necessary to widen them significantly, or indeed direct them through many windings <term>torture: literally "tortures," referring here to the twists, turns, or meanders of a river's course</term>, or divert them into multiple branches. And if the river, due to excessive winding, should become sluggish and swampy, then you must straighten it so the waters take a sufficient course, but not so much that it causes the ruin of the shores or the bank. And when deep hollows form near any bank, then that area should be filled with gabions <term>gabbioni: cylindrical baskets or cages filled with heavy material, used for fortification or damming</term> containing fascines <term>fascine: bundles of brushwood or sticks tied together</term> and gravel; this is so the water does not undermine the bank in such a way that, by collapsing, the river is forced to make an "elbow" <note>original: "gombito" (modern Italian *gomito*); a sharp, sudden bend in the river</note> into your property or villa as it straightens its course, etc.

### CHAPTER LIX.

#### *To ensure that water cannot undermine the struck object.*

Where water strikes an object, it rises, and it gains as much weight as it surges above its surface; when this water falls back down upon the other water, it strikes and penetrates even to the bottom, which it continually wears away. Such a concavity will be created along the sides of the struck object. To counter this, a flat platform should be constructed around such a pier; this platform must be well-founded and of such width that the falling water lands upon it (fig. 147. plate 25).

### CHAPTER LX.

#### *To ensure that a bridge does not collapse.*

If a river normally occupies the width of a single arch, ensure that the bridge has three arches; you shall do this to account for floods and inundations.

### CHAPTER LXI.

#### *To remedy the impact of a river.*

To mitigate the impact of a river against its bank, turn it with a gentle curve, and construct the bank in such a way that the fall created by the rebound of its surge lands upon the bank itself (fig. 147. plate 25).

<summary>Leonardo outlines practical hydraulic engineering techniques to control river velocity and prevent bank erosion, emphasizing the use of gabions, meanders, and protective platforms around bridge piers.</summary>
<keywords>river management, erosion control, gabions, bridge engineering, hydraulic dynamics, meanders, flood prevention</keywords>

### CHAPTER LXII.

#### *On returning soil to places exposed and eroded by the flow of water.*

One ought, by means of rain or indeed by having the convenience of other water, to direct channels or river mouths through places and lands where they may pass with a great current, in such a way that they become turbid <note>muddy or clouded with sediment</note> from the earth they pick up. These should be managed so that when they reach the places where you wish them to discharge said earth, such water channels are divided into small branches <note>original: "ramicelli"</note> in the manner of furrows; in this way, their momentum is slowed, and the effort will succeed.

### CHAPTER LXIII.

#### *How mountain soil should be led by running water into marshy valleys, making them fertile and purifying the surrounding air.*

The branching of channels that are led by natural flow along high hills are those which, through their shifting, carry the soil of those hills to the low marshes, filling them with earth and making them fertile. Let **A** (fig. 148, plate 26) be the master river, which turns into a marsh at **BFHI**; let the channel then be drawn along the height of the hills **AEN**, and from that channel let various branches be allowed to fall, moving them to different locations. Thus, their force <note>original: "ruine," referring here to the erosive power of falling water</note> will break apart the soil, and after their course, they will discharge it into the low marsh. In this way, you can shift the fall of the entire water-rich channel so frequently that you will have leveled the ground throughout the site of such marshes. <note>Leonardo suggests that by filling in stagnant marshes with fresh soil, the "bad air"—which was believed to cause disease—would be "healed" or purified.</note>

| channels | rivers | soil | marsh |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| streamlets | shifts | hills | water |

<summary>Leonardo describes a method of land reclamation using water to erode soil from hills and deposit it into marshy lowlands to create fertile farmland and improve public health.</summary>

# BOOK SEVEN
## OF THINGS CARRIED BY WATER

---

### CHAPTER I.
#### *Which water carries [materials].*

That water which does not move cannot lead anything else with it. For if **A** is found floating <note>original: "natante"</note> upon the water **MCND** (fig. 149, plate 26) or truly upon the bottom of the same water there is found **B**, an object movable <note>original: "obietto amovibile"</note> by the impact <note>original: "percussione"</note> of the water; it is a certain thing that neither **A** will be carried by the water to site **C**, nor **B** led to site **D**, if the water supporting **A** does not move toward **C**, and if the water adjacent <note>original: "contigua"</note> to **B** does not strike said **B**, and with its motion, blow, and friction <note>original: "confregazione," referring to the scouring or rubbing action of the current</note> go on rolling it toward **D**. Therefore, that water which does not move cannot lead any other thing with it.

### CHAPTER II.
#### *Where the river discharges what it carries.*

Where the flow of the water is lacking, there remains that which it was carrying. This principle arises from the previous one. Because once the momentum <note>original: "impeto"</note> and the motion of the water have ceased, the motion of the thing carried by the water also ceases.

### CHAPTER III.
#### *On the same subject.*

If water does not carry anything where it does not first move; it follows then that where the water has the greatest motion, it will carry the greatest quantity of weight with it.

<summary>Leonardo defines the fundamental laws of sediment transport, arguing that water's ability to move objects—whether floating or on the riverbed—is entirely dependent on its velocity and physical impact.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, sediment transport, momentum, friction, erosion, buoyancy, hydraulic laws</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of axioms regarding hydraulic physics. While the previous page established that motion is the requirement for transport, this page focuses on the inverse: how and where materials are deposited as motion decreases. Leonardo is building a systematic theory of sedimentation that explains how riverbeds and coastlines are formed.</meta>

<page-num>397</page-num>

# BOOK SEVEN
## OF THINGS CARRIED BY WATER

---

### CHAPTER IV.

->*On the same subject.*<-

If water carries a greater weight where it has a greater motion; then it follows that where the water has less motion, being burdened there, it discharges more of its weight.

### CHAPTER V.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Where the water has less motion, there it discharges its lightest weight; this occurs because into the water of lesser motion there come only those things carried by the smaller vortices <note>original: "rivoluzioni," referring to the circular eddies or whirlpools within a current</note>, which are not created except in water of lesser motion.

### CHAPTER VI.

->*On the same subject.*<-

One body of water striking into another remains without motion and is slowed down, and for this reason, it discharges its weight there.

### CHAPTER VII.

->*Where the bottom of the current rises.*<-

The bottom will rise beneath the current when the base of that current dies away into dead water <note>Leonardo uses the term "dead water" (acqua morta) to describe stagnant or still water that lacks a directional flow.</note>, just as if the river **AF** (fig. 150, plate 26) were to pour into the dead pool <note>original: "pelago morto," implying a deep, still body of water like a lake or sea</note> **FMNB**, the material carried by the course **AF** would be left at **FB**; and this is according to the second rule mentioned above, which says: *where the course of the water fails, there remains that which the water conducted, etc.*

### CHAPTER VIII.

->*Where the lightest things are carried.*<-

All things that are lighter than air <note>This is a curious phrasing; Leonardo may be referring to air bubbles, foam, or extremely buoyant debris that rests on the very surface, influenced more by the air than the deep current.</note> will remain toward the depression <note>original: "bassezza"</note> of the river, below the start of the wave's slope <note>original: "declinazione"</note>. And this occurs because there is the lowest point of the river's surface, and where the water between the waves is lower it has less motion; and according to the fifth rule, where there is less motion, the lightest things come to rest.

<summary>Leonardo explains the laws of sedimentation, noting that as water slows down or encounters still "dead water," it loses its ability to carry weight and begins to deposit the materials it was transporting.</summary>
<keywords>sedimentation, dead water, vortices, riverbed formation, fluid dynamics, buoyancy</keywords>

Here, Leonardo applies these abstract principles to the physical geography of rivers, explaining why meandering paths and natural obstacles like brushwood cause rivers to clog with debris.</meta>

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

### CHAPTER IX.
->*Where the silt is carried.*<-

Where the water has the least motion, the surface of its bed will be composed of the finest silt <term>litta: fine mud or silty sediment</term> or sand. This also arises from the fifth rule <note>Referring to Chapter V on the previous page, which states that slower water carries only the lightest materials.</note>, because among the lightest things carried by water are silt and sand.

---

### CHAPTER X.
->*Which river fills most with matter.*<-

That river which stretches out through long, transverse windings <note>original: "tortuosità," referring to the meanders or "S" curves of a river channel</note> is the one that most quickly fills with matter <note>i.e., sediment and debris</note>. This is proven by the fifth rule of this book, which says: where the water has less motion, there, being burdened, it discharges its weight. Therefore, the river that is more winding, by making itself longer through these windings, becomes as much slower as it is longer.

---

### CHAPTER XI.
->*Where it fills with sand and silt.*<-

Where the course of the turbid water enters among the low branches of thickets <note>original: "boschine," referring to shrubs or brushwood growing along the banks or in the shallows</note>, there, because of the many revolutions of the eddies <note>original: "retrosi," the circular back-currents or whirlpools formed when water hits an obstacle</note>, it will discharge much sand or silt. And this happens because the water is slowed down by the aforementioned revolutions of the eddies.

---

### CHAPTER XII.
->*On the same subject.*<-

The sand remains higher beneath the crest of the wave than beneath its trough. And the reason is that the wave is more sluggish at the end of its rise than in any other part.

---

### CHAPTER XIII.
->*On the same subject.*<-

If a rock <note>original: "scoglio," which can refer to a large stone, a reef, or a rocky outcrop in the stream</note> in the river exceeds and divides the course of the water, which then reunites after that rock; then the interval that... <note>The text ends mid-sentence on this page.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains how the slowing of water currents—whether caused by river meanders, brushwood, or wave mechanics—leads to the deposition of silt and sand, effectively shaping the riverbed.</summary>

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

...found between the rock and the point where the water rejoins, it will be a place where sand is deposited. The reason for this is that we have proven in the second [section] with the... <note>Leonardo frequently leaves gaps in his manuscripts for citations or proofs he intended to fill in later.</note> that water always slows down after passing objects that protrude above its surface; therefore, according to the fifth rule of this work, sand is deposited after the aforementioned rock.

### CHAPTER XIV.

->*On the same subject.*<-

The deeper the rock or object that divides the water is from the surface, the less sand it leaves behind. The reason is that, according to the fifty-first rule of the previous [section], when water covers an object, it rises there and carves into its base; and the more weight of water that descends, the greater the impact made after that object, and the more it is hollowed out, leaving less sand behind.

### CHAPTER XV.

->*On the same subject.*<-

After the water descends, that part of the sand closest to the impact of the water will be finer than the rest. And the coarse gravel <term>ghiara: coarse sand or pebbles</term> will be further from the strike. This happens because after the water falls, makes its impact, and carves out the bottom, that portion of water which flees the site of said impact pushes the gravel and the sand toward the bank; then, when the current returns toward the site of the fall, its momentum <term>impeto: the force or energy of motion</term> having diminished and consequently its motion having become slower, it does not have the strength to push back the same gravel, but it can certainly move the fine sand, which comes to rest near the impact of the water.

### CHAPTER XVI.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Sand or other light things observe and obey the twists or circulation of the water's eddies <term>retrosi: back-currents or whirlpools</term>, while large stones travel in a straight line. This happens because although—as proven by the... of this [work]—the falling water moves large stones, nevertheless the reflected water and the eddies, because they consist of circular motion, do not have enough power to turn them; but they can certainly move lighter things, churning through <term>trivellando: literally "drilling," describing a boring or spinning motion</term> them.

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

<sig>51</sig>

...interweaving with one another, it digs out the opposite ground or sand from in front of the stone; once it has undermined it <term>scalzato: literally "unshod," meaning the water has removed the supporting earth from the base of an object</term>, the stone itself turns over by its own weight. And if the water flows over it, after passing the stone, the water falls in a perpendicular line and, through the force of the impact, penetrates from the surface to the bottom of the other waters. It erodes and removes, undermining the stone from its opposite supports in such a way that it too turns over; and so it proceeds step by step until it has traveled the length of a river. And if any smaller stone stands in its way, the water hollows it out in the same manner and does the same. This is how stones are rolled along the beds of flowing rivers.

### CHAPTER XXI.
->*Where rivers carry more soil.*<-

Rivers deposit more soil where human populations are nearby than where there is no human presence. This is because in such places, people work the mountains and the hills <note>Leonardo refers here to agricultural labor such as plowing, which disturbs the soil</note>, and the rains carry away the disturbed soil with greater ease than they do hard ground covered with various vegetation.

### CHAPTER XXII.
->*On the same subject.*<-

While valleys widen over the course of time, they deepen very little; this is because the rain restores nearly as much soil to the valleys as the river carries away—though in some places it is more, and in some less.

### CHAPTER XXIII.
->*On the same subject.*<-

The beds of rivers that are naturally exposed <note>This refers to the visible riverbed during periods of low water</note> do not provide true rules regarding the nature or quantity of the materials carried by the water. This is because during high water, many places are filled with sand; then, as the water subsides through specific side channels, these sands are lifted from the gravel beds where they rested, or are undermined from below. This causes the high banks of sand to collapse one after another; because sand is light, it follows the current and is then deposited where the flow of water becomes more peaceful.

<vocab>rivers, soil, sand, gravels, banks, waters, valleys, rains</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains the mechanics of how water undermines and rolls heavy stones along a riverbed and notes that human agriculture increases river siltation by loosening the earth. He also observes that valleys widen rather than deepen because the sediment washed down by rain balances the material removed by the river.</summary>

<keywords>erosion, sediment transport, hydraulics, human impact on nature, riverbeds, geomorphology, stones, siltation</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which detailed how water erodes the ground and moves stones through "unshoeing" or undermining their base, Leonardo now transitions to a more abstract analysis of how objects of specific shapes and weights behave when suspended in or carried by flowing water.</meta>

<page-num>402</page-num>

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

### CAPITOLO XXIV.

*On a long object of equal weight and thickness carried by water.*

A long object of equal weight and thickness that passes through the middle of a uniform channel will move lengthwise along the river's path. This occurs because the said object is not naturally prone to rotating <note>original: *circonvolubile*</note>, as it does not have one part heavier than the other to cause it to turn; nor, in the middle of a uniform channel, does it receive a greater impact <term>percussione: the physical pressure or "strike" of the water's force against the object</term> on one side than on the other (fig. 151. plate 26).

### CAPITOLO XXV.

*On the same subject.*

A long object of equal weight and thickness that moves through a uniform channel between the middle and the bank <term>argine: the embankment or side of the river</term> will travel obliquely <note>that is, at a slanted angle rather than straight with the current</note>. This happens because the force of the water carrying it is unequal; that is, according to the thirty-second rule of the second book, the current is faster in the middle than at the sides (fig. 152. plate 27).

### CAPITOLO XXVI.

*On the same subject.*

A long object of equal width and weight carried through a uniform channel, which occupies more of the side than the middle, will go spinning through the water. This happens because of the various rotations of the reflections <note>Leonardo uses "reflections" here to describe the backflow, eddies, or "rebound" of water as it hits the riverbank</note> produced by the bank (fig. 153. plate 27).

### CAPITOLO XXVII.

*On a long object of equal weight and thickness carried by water.*

A pyramidal weight of uniformly varying thickness <term>uniformemente difforme: a Renaissance physics term meaning a shape whose dimensions change at a constant rate, such as a cone or a pyramid</term>, which is thrown into the water with the point forward, will immediately turn its base toward the river’s current. This happens because, just as in the air an object of uniform material and weight always lets its heaviest part lead (being faster in its descent <note>original: *descenso*</note>), so in flowing water—which only moves because it is descending—the pyramidal weight will turn its base toward the current because it is heavier, and for this reason, faster (fig. 154. plate 27).

<summary>Leonardo explains the mechanics of how long objects and tapered weights move in a river, noting that their orientation and rotation are determined by their position relative to the faster currents in the center and the eddies near the banks.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, river currents, hydraulics, pyramidal weight, water channels, physics of motion, Leonardo's rules</keywords>

<page-num>403</page-num>

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

### CHAPTER XXVIII.

->*On a wide object carried by the current between the surface and the bed.*<-

A wide object <note>original: *cosa lata*; likely a flat board or similar planar item</note> carried by a river’s current between the surface and the water’s bed <term>fondo: the bottom or floor of the river</term>—if it encounters water that is slower than the water carrying it, and is at that time positioned obliquely <note>slanted or tilted at an angle</note> toward the oncoming river—will immediately jump from the bottom toward the surface of the water. For example, if AB (fig. 155, plate 27) is a wide object carried by the river CDEF between the surface and the bed, and it encounters the slow water of the bank <term>argine: the side or embankment of the river</term> CD while tilted toward said bank, I say that AB will immediately jump from the bottom to the surface. This occurs because, with part A facing the current and part B facing the bank, part A is struck with greater impetus <term>impeto: the physical force or momentum of the water</term> than part B. This follows the thirty-second rule of the second book, which states that the water of a river is faster in the middle than at the sides. If this is the case, then part B, being slower and pushed by the faster part A, cannot move with the same speed that part A strikes the slower water. This resistance causes part B to receive a blow <note>a physical reaction or recoil from the water's resistance</note> which, while part A is pressed by the power of the current, pushes part B upward, causing it to pass from the bed to the surface.

### CHAPTER XXIX.

->*The original of this chapter is missing.*<-

### CHAPTER XXX.

->*On the same subject.*<-

A wide object carried by the river’s current between the surface and the bed—if, upon encountering slower water, its slant faces behind the flow of the water—it will suddenly cast itself toward the bottom. This happens because part B (fig. 156. plate 27), facing the current and pressed by the power of that current, is pushed downward toward the bed; as the faster part forcibly pulls the slower part along with it, part A also submerges to the bottom behind part B. However, if said slant faces toward the right or the left of the river’s width, the object will cast itself toward that right or left side of the river, and it will follow this pattern for any direction it faces.

<summary>Leonardo explains the hydrodynamic lift and descent of flat objects in a river, noting that their vertical movement is determined by their angle relative to the current's velocity gradient.</summary>
<keywords>hydrodynamics, fluid resistance, river current, lift, buoyancy, velocity gradient, Leonardo's 32nd rule, river banks</keywords>

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

### CHAPTER XXXI.
->*On things carried by water along the bottom.*<-

Of those things carried by water along its bottom <term>fondo: the bed or floor of the river</term>, the lightest ones travel the furthest in the same amount of time. This happens because they gather together less <note>original: *minore congregazione*; they do not clump or pile up as much</note> upon the bottom, and thus they are slowed down less.

### CHAPTER XXXII.
->*On things carried by water upon the surface.*<-

Of those things carried by the water’s current, that which has more of itself in the air obeys the movement of the air more than that of the water; and conversely, that which has more of itself within the water will obey the current of that water more than the movement of the air. This is self-evident, and experience teaches it to us, as can be seen in (fig. 156, plate 27).

### CHAPTER XXXIII.
->*On the same subject.*<-

If the air is slower than the motion of the water—moving in the same direction as said air—then the motion of the object <term>mobile: any body or item in motion</term> upon the water will be slower than if the motions of the air and water were equal. It will be slowed down even more as the difference between these motions of air and water increases. This is because when the impetus <term>impeto: the physical force or momentum</term> of the air is lacking, the motion of the object also fails; the less this impetus is, the slower the motion will be.

### CHAPTER XXIV.
<note>Likely a typographical error in the source text for XXXIV, following the sequence.</note>
->*On the same subject.*<-

If the motion of the air is faster than the motion of the water—moving in the same direction—then the motion of the object will become faster, and all the more so as the air is faster than the water. This is evident because as the impetus of the air increases, the motion of the object also increases proportionally.

<summary>Leonardo explores the physics of buoyancy and resistance, explaining how the relative surface area of an object exposed to wind versus water determines its speed and direction. He also notes that lighter debris moves faster along a riverbed because it is less likely to be caught in stationary clusters.</summary>

<keywords>hydrodynamics, riverbed, wind resistance, impetus, fluid dynamics, buoyancy, momentum</keywords>

<page-num>405</page-num>

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

### CHAPTER XXXV.

->*On the same subject.*<-

If the motion of the air moves at the same speed against the flow of the water as the water moves against that air, then the moving object <term>mobile: any body or item in motion</term> will follow the course of the water if it has more contact with the water than with the air. And the opposite will occur if it has more contact with the air than with the water. This is proven by the thirty-second [chapter] of this [work].

### CHAPTER XXXVI.

->*On lighter things carried by water.*<-

Things that are lighter than water do not follow the path of the surging <term>risaltazione: the leaping or rebounding of water as it hits obstacles or other currents</term> and the intersection <term>intersegazione: the point where two or more currents cross one another</term> of the water; rather, they pass through the middle of its course, or near the sides, according to where they were positioned when they entered those currents, and they are not hindered unless they are pushed with equal force. This is because if the right-hand wave of the surging current meets the left-hand one, it is necessary—if they are of equal power—that the place of impact <term>percussione: the physical collision or striking of the currents</term> results in an equal surge backward; therefore, objects moving from a position above the water, being pushed no more by one impact than the other, remain in the same line of the course. 

But if the power of one wave is greater than the other—meaning by speed of the current, for I do not mean greater power by a greater volume of water. For if one body of water were of much less thickness <note>Leonardo uses "thickness" here to refer to the depth or mass of a specific stream of water.</note> than the other, it does not matter. Let us say that one body of water has half <term>subdupla: a mathematical term for a ratio of 1 to 2</term> the thickness of another, and that it then has double the speed; because those waters, clashing together, are of equal magnitude at the point of contact, as proven by the seventy-seventh [chapter] of the second [book]; the larger being one square braccio <note>A "braccio" was a historical unit of measurement, roughly the length of an arm (approx. 23 inches).</note> and the smaller being one braccio; the smaller only strikes the larger in its half, and likewise the larger strikes the smaller with its half, whereby the contacts made by the impacts are equal in quantity, but unequal in half-power <term>subdupla: here referring to the 1:2 ratio of force</term> due to the one being twice as fast as the other. And thus the floating object will be pushed from the straight line of its course with a proportional deflection <term>torcimento: a twisting or turning away from a straight path</term> (fig. 158. plate 28.).

<vocab>risaltazione, intersegazione, subdupla, percussione, torcimento</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains that the direction of a floating object depends on whether air or water has a greater surface area of contact, and demonstrates how currents of different speeds—even if they have different volumes—deflect objects based on the force of their impact.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, current intersection, wind resistance, water velocity, momentum, deflection, hydrostatics</keywords>

### CHAPTER XXXVII.

->*On things carried within the collision of two streams.*<-

Regarding things carried by waters that intersect <term>intersegano: the crossing or overlapping of two different flows</term>, where one flows into another that is higher than the bottom of the first: that which is in the deeper water will pass beneath the higher water. To put it more clearly, the water D C has a higher bed than the water A B; and the things carried near the bottom of water A B will pass under the water D C, and will follow their straight course along with the water that carries them. And as for things carried on the surface of water D B, their course shall be changed from straight to curved (fig. 159. plate 28).

### CHAPTER XXXVIII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Things carried by water near a winding <term>tortuoso: referring to the sinuous or twisting path of a riverbed</term> bottom follow a different course than those carried by the same water on the surface. A light object is carried along the bottom following the line A B (fig. 160. plate 28); and a lighter object will be carried along the surface of the water following the line D C. This happens because in the twists of rivers, where the current rushes from D to C, its reflected motion <term>moto reflesso: the reaction of water as it bounces off a bank or obstacle, creating a secondary current</term> returns from A to B.

### CHAPTER XXXIX.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Of things carried between two waters <note>This refers to an object suspended between two converging currents</note>, only that which is in the midst of two currents of equal motion will proceed without being turned upside down. This occurs because it will be pushed equally on all its sides by the forces of two currents that are equal to one another.

### CHAPTER XL.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Of things carried between two waters, that which is in the midst of two unequal currents will undergo the greatest revolution <term>rivoluzione: the tumbling or rolling motion of an object</term> between bottom and top. This occurs because the part that is struck by the faster current is turned more, becoming the lead, and immediately the opposite part

### CHAPTER XLI.

#### *On the same subject.*

An object will be deprived of lateral revolution <term>rivoluzione laterale: a spinning or rotating motion around a vertical axis, like a top</term> if it moves between two currents of equal motion. Conversely, an object will be in continuous lateral revolution if it moves between two unequal currents. This is proven by the two reasons previously stated; because if the currents are equal, one does not overcome the other, and if they are unequal, not only do they overcome each other, but also—according to the thirteenth [proposition] of the fourth [book] <note>Leonardo frequently cites his own numbered scientific axioms or "rules" from his various notebooks</note>—they create different motions from the surface to the bottom.

### CHAPTER XLII.

#### *On the same subject.*

An object of irregular shape <term>disuniforme figura: an asymmetrical or non-uniform body</term> that is carried by water of a uniform course, while being within the surface of the water, will always keep turning. But an object of uniform slant <term>uniforme obliquità: a shape with consistent angles or symmetry</term> will never turn. This occurs because the water, striking the irregular parts of the moving object, creates different impacts, which cause various revolutions; this does not happen to a uniform moving object <term>mobile uniforme: a balanced or symmetrical object in motion</term>.

### CHAPTER XLIII.

#### *On the same subject.*

When the upper and lower parts of a moving object <term>mobile: any body or object in motion</term> are struck by an equal current in the water, that object will undergo lateral revolution. This is proven by the twentieth [proposition] of the second [book], which states that from the middle of the river to the banks, and from the banks to the middle, many reflections <term>riflessi: secondary currents or "rebounds" of water hitting the banks or obstacles</term> are always flowing.

### CHAPTER XLIV.

#### *On the same subject.*

Of things carried by the water, that which has a smaller shape will have a greater revolution. This happens because the large revolutions...

### CHAPTER XLV.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Of objects carried by the flow of water which are equal in width, those that sink deeper will experience less revolution <term>rivoluzione: the spinning or rotating motion of the object</term>. This happens because these generated revolutions vary from the surface to the bottom of the water, in which as many revolutions are generated as there are shallows and depths in the riverbed. Therefore, of necessity, an object carried by the water that sinks very deep is contested by many various heights and revolutions; for this reason, it remains hesitant, and often it does not obey any single one, and if it does obey, it obeys the most powerful.

### CHAPTER XLVII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Of objects equal in shape and quantity, that which is driven by more powerful water obeys the revolutions of the water more; and this is because the more powerful water has greater impetus and force.

### CHAPTER XLIX.

#### *On an object in the water moved by the wind. —*

That object which sinks deeper into the water is moved less by the wind that strikes the part of it remaining out of the water. This occurs because the water offers greater resistance to the wind than the air does, and because in that medium where there is greater resistance, the moved object is more delayed; therefore, etc.

### CHAPTER L.

#### *Which object floats upon the water.*

That object floats most upon the water which is of lesser weight than the water. This is proven because a heavy body <term>grave: a mass or weight</term> does not descend where it finds resistance; therefore, if an object lighter than water <term>lieve: a light body</term> finds resistance in a medium heavier than itself, we shall say that the object floats upon the water all the more as it is lighter than the water, etc.

### CHAPTER LI.

#### *Which object sinks into the water.*

That object sinks further into the water which weighs more than said water. This is proven because, as was stated in the eighteenth [proposition] of the first [book], a heavy body descends toward the center <note>Leonardo refers to the center of the Earth, the natural destination for all "heavy" things in Aristotelian physics</note> where it finds no resistance; therefore, if the water is lighter than the object thrown into the water, it cannot offer resistance to it, and if this is so, necessity dictates that the object which has greater weight than the water sinks further into it.

<meta>Continuing from page 409, Leonardo transitions from discussing how the shape of a hull affects movement through water and wind resistance to the fundamental principles of buoyancy and displacement, essentially exploring what would later be known as Archimedes' principle.</meta>

<page-num>410</page-num>

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

### CHAPTER LII.

->*What object in the water remains level with said water.*<-

That object will remain upon the water with its surface flush with the water's surface, which in its total gravity <term>gravità: in this context, the density or weight of the object</term> is equal to the weight of the water surrounding it. This follows from the two preceding chapters. For if an object of greater weight sinks into the water, and one of lesser weight floats, then that of equal weight will share an equal surface <note>meaning the top of the object will sit exactly at the water line</note>. And it is proven: because if water within water does not weigh—as was proven in the first [book] <note>Leonardo argues that a volume of water has no weight when submerged in a body of water because it is in equilibrium</note>—then a weight of equal gravity to the water does not weigh while within the water, and if this is so, it will share a common surface.

### CHAPTER LIII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

A boat supported by the water sinks into the water just until its weight is equal to the weight of the water that surrounds it <note>this is a clear articulation of the principle of displacement: a floating body displaces its own weight in fluid</note>. This is evident from the previous chapter, because if it were of greater weight it would sink into the water and be submerged, and if it were of lesser weight it would float higher; therefore, it sinks until its weight is equal to that of the water.

### CHAPTER LIV.

->*On the same subject.*<-

The total weight of the boat, positioned at the water level, is made equal to an equal volume of water, accounting for the lightness <term>levità: the upward force or buoyancy</term> of the air that stays below it [within the hull], which keeps it at such a height. This proposition remains proven in the first [book] by the thirty-sixth [proposition]. Since, to make the air within the boat remain level with the water surrounding it, it is necessary that however much the air in the boat exceeds the surrounding water in lightness, the weight of the boat must proportionately exceed the weight of the water, so that between the lightness of the air and the gravity of the weight in the boat, a mixture of gravity is created equal to that of the water.

<vocab>gravity, weight, water, boat, surface, lightness of air, hydrostatics</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains the principles of hydrostatics, specifically why objects sink, float, or sit flush with the water's surface based on their weight relative to the water they displace. He correctly identifies that a boat's buoyancy is a result of the combined "lightness" of the air inside it and the "gravity" of the vessel itself reaching equilibrium with the water.</summary>
<keywords>buoyancy, displacement, hydrostatics, boat design, gravity, equilibrium, weight of water</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from page 410, Leonardo continues his investigation into the physics of buoyancy. Having previously established that a boat sinks until it displaces its own weight in water, he now examines the mechanics of how that water rises and how the density of the liquid—as well as the air contained within the hull—affects the boat's ability to float.</meta>

<page-num>411</page-num>

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

### CHAPTER LV.

->*On the same subject.*<-

A boat sinks into the water that supports it just as much as the water surrounding the boat gains weight over the air of said boat, which is equal to the weight supported. And the reason is this: because while the boat is driven into the water by its own weight, it is clear that as the boat sinks, the water surrounding it rises. And if this is so, that water acquires greater weight <note>Leonardo is observing that as the water level rises around the hull, the "column" of water exerts more pressure</note>, and the boat, by reason of the air contained within it, acquires greater lightness <term>levità: buoyancy or the tendency to float</term>. Therefore, etc.

### CHAPTER LVI.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Water supports a greater weight the heavier <note>original: "grave"; in this context, Leonardo refers to density</note> it is. This is proven in the twenty-second [proposition] of the first [book], where it says that the water of the sea and of turbid rivers <note>referring to muddy or sediment-rich water</note> offers more resistance to the weights they carry because they are heavier than other water. And I add that, besides this, there is the reason mentioned in the previous chapter; because if the water surrounding the boat could not rise in such quantity that it acquired a weight equal to the weight of the boat pressing upon it, there is no doubt that it could not support the boat with all its weight; therefore, I say that water supports a greater weight the heavier it is.

### CHAPTER LVII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

The more air the boat has within it, the less of its own weight it will give to the surrounding water; and conversely, the less air the boat has within it, the more weight it will give to the surrounding water. This is proven by the fifty-fourth [chapter] above, which says that the total weight of a boat positioned at the water level is made equal to an equal volume of water, calculating for the lightness of the air that stays beneath it <note>meaning the air trapped inside the hull below the waterline</note>, which keeps it at such a height; and consequently, the air enclosed within the boat lightens the boat in all its weight.

<summary>Leonardo explains that a boat's buoyancy is a result of the water it displaces rising to create a counter-weight, and notes that denser liquids (like saltwater) provide better support for heavy loads.</summary>
<keywords>buoyancy, displacement, water density, salt water, fluid mechanics, air volume, Archimedes' principle</keywords>

<header>LEONARDO DA VINCI</header>

### CHAPTER LVIII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

The more air a boat contains within itself, the more weight the water will support; and conversely, the less air a boat contains, the less weight the water will support. This principle arises from the previous one and is proven by the same fifty-fourth [chapter], etc.

### CHAPTER LIX.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Of waters of equal depth, that which is narrower will support less weight upon itself. This is proven because, as the boat sinks into the water due to the weight it contains, the water level rises. But there is this difference: when the water that rises is wide—<note>original: "verbigrazia"</note> for example, by a span <term>palmo: an old unit of measurement based on the width of a hand</term> because the boat sinks toward the bottom—then for every span the boat sinks, the water rises by a span and acquires great weight. And the more weight it acquires, according to the fifty-sixth [chapter] above, the more weight it supports. 

But when the water is narrow, because there is only a small total amount of water that rises as the boat sinks, it still acquires little weight and can support little weight. And for this reason, the water at the bottom of a smaller vessel, which surrounds the weight placed over the air with its water, does not press upon that air as much as the weight placed above presses upon that water. This is unlike the water in the larger vessel, which is made so high above that air that it supports the weight; it has acquired so much weight through that height that it is powerful enough to push the air upward—along with the weight placed above it—just as that weight is powerful enough to press the water downward (fig. 162, plate 29).

### CHAPTER LX.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Of water of equal width, that which is shallower <note>original: "più bassa"; literally lower or shallower</note> will support less weight; and conversely, that which is deeper <note>original: "più alta"; literally higher or deeper</note> will support more weight. This is manifest from the previous [chapter] and is proven by the fifty-sixth, which says: water supports a greater weight the heavier <note>In Leonardo's physics, "heavier" often refers to the total pressure or density of the water column</note> it is. Therefore, if water acquires more weight the deeper it is, you will say that of water of equal width, that which is shallower will support less weight, etc.

<summary>In these chapters, Leonardo explores the mechanics of buoyancy, specifically how the volume of air inside a hull and the dimensions of the surrounding water determine a vessel's capacity to carry weight. He argues that a larger, deeper body of water exerts more upward force than a narrow or shallow one.</summary>

<keywords>hydrostatics, buoyancy, displacement, air volume, water pressure, vessel capacity, Leonardo da Vinci, physics of water</keywords>

## CHAPTER LXI.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Every ship in the water exerts weight only along the line of its motion. This is proven by the fifty-third [proposition], which states that a boat supported by water sinks into the water until its weight is equal to the weight of the [displaced] water. Therefore, if it is true—as it most certainly is, according to the thirty-first [proposition] of the previous section—that water within water has no weight, you would say that the ship within the water has no weight because it is equal in weight to that water. And because water does not move unless it descends, and it does not descend unless it is higher <note>meaning at a higher elevation or pressure level</note>, as was proven in the first [book]; and if it is higher, it weighs more; therefore, water only weighs along the line of its motion, and for this reason, the ship in the water also only weighs along the line of its motion, etc.

## CHAPTER LXII.

->*On a light object supported near a waterfall.*<-

A body of light quality <note>such as a piece of wood or cork</note> which finds itself between the <term>percussione: the impact where falling water strikes the surface</term> and the <term>risaltazione: the rebounding or resurfacing of the water after the initial impact</term> will never change its position, provided the river has a steady movement. This happens because its lightness mimics air, which, when it is submerged by falling water, circles back near the fall. Nor can said light object, having resurfaced, be carried far away, because it remains surrounded by those same <term>bollori: the turbulent, churning bubbles or "boiling" motion of the water</term>, which rise in the same way, as seen in (fig. 163, plate 29).

<vocab>ship, water, motion, weight, impact, rebounding, lightness, churning bubbles</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains that a ship's effective weight is directed by its motion within the medium of water and describes the physics of how light objects become trapped in the turbulent eddies of a waterfall.</summary>
<keywords>hydrostatics, buoyancy, fluid dynamics, waterfalls, weight distribution, Leonardo's experiments, turbulence</keywords>
