# BOOK EIGHT

## ON THE WATER-OUNCE AND ON PIPES
<note>The "water-ounce" (oncia dell'acqua) was a traditional unit used to measure the flow of water in pipes and canals, essential for Renaissance engineering and irrigation.</note>

---

### CHAPTER I.

->*Which part of a full cistern is under the greatest pressure.*<-

Every liquid body that is capable of motion will struggle against and push more upon the part of its enclosure which is most distant from the center of its surface than any other part of its <term>ricettacolo: the container or vessel holding the liquid</term>. This occurs (fig. 164. plate 29) because between that specific part and the center of the water’s surface, there exists a greater quantity and a longer line of water than any other imaginable line extending from said center to any other part of the container.
<note>Leonardo is describing hydrostatic pressure. He observes that the further a point is from the center of the top surface—essentially, the deeper or more diagonal the path—the greater the weight and "push" of the water at that point.</note>

### CHAPTER II.

->*Whether a vessel, pierced in the bottom with equal holes, will pour more water from one than from another.*<-

The question is asked: if a vessel has its bottom pierced with equal holes like a <term>crivello: a sieve or strainer</term>, which of the holes will pour out more water in an equal amount of time. To experiment and establish a rule, you shall proceed as follows: First, make all the holes in the bottom and plug them all except for one. Weigh the water that flows from it during the time it takes to fall one <note>original: "braccio," a measurement roughly equal to 23 inches or 58 cm</note>, or whatever duration you prefer. Then, plug that hole again and do the same for the others, one by one, plugging those you have already tested.

But ensure that additional water replenishes the vessel as it pours, without any <term>percussione: the impact or shock caused by falling water</term>, so that the incoming water does not exert force on any part of the vessel receiving it, and that the vessel always maintains the same weight of water <note>Leonardo is describing a "constant head" experiment, ensuring the water level remains steady so the pressure does not change during the test</note>. To do this, the vessel that receives the replenishment water must be separate from the one that pours it. Meanwhile, through the aforementioned experience, you will conclude from the previous chapter that the hole in the part of the bottom most remote from the center of the water’s surface will pour more. This is because it is pushed with greater force, as is proven (fig. 165. and 166. plate 29).

<vocab>bottino: cistern or conduit, ricettacolo: receptacle/container, crivello: sieve, percussione: impact/shock, oncia dell'acqua: water-ounce (unit of flow)</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo begins a technical study of water pressure and flow measurement, arguing that pressure is greatest at the point furthest from the center of the surface and proposing a controlled experiment to measure flow rates through a sieve-like vessel.</summary>
<keywords>hydrostatics, water pressure, experimental method, water-ounce, fluid dynamics, constant head, cisterns, Leonardo's pipes</keywords>

## CHAPTER III.

#### *The proportion of water pouring from different reservoirs.*

If there are two vessels, each of which is of a uniform width throughout, and their heights are double one to the other, I say that if they are full of water and then their equal <term>spiracoli: apertures or outlets</term> are opened at their lowest depth at the same instant, then the proportions of their discharges at every degree of time will increase toward infinity within a finite time. 

This is proven: let AB and CP (fig. 167. plate 29) be the proposed vessels, each of uniform width, and let the height of one be double that of the other—that is, AB is twice as high as vessel CP. Let the outlets be made equal at the lowest depth of these vessels, namely outlets B and P. I say that if they are opened at the same time, the double proportion <note>A 2:1 ratio.</note>, which is the smallest proportion among all the proportions <note>Leonardo likely means the starting ratio is the simplest or smallest initial difference.</note>, will grow to an infinite magnitude in all degrees of time until the small vessel is empty. 

And this arises because when the smaller and larger vessels begin the <term>fuga: the escape or outflow</term> of their water, the outflow of one is double the other; and if we divide the height of the larger vessel into twelve equal degrees, and the smaller vessel into six of those degrees, and then each of those degrees is divided into another twelve minutes, and each minute into another twelve minutes; here the proportions will never agree. This is because when the larger vessel has decreased its water by one degree of height, the smaller vessel will have decreased its water not by half a degree, but by much less, because its motion has become slower due to the diminishing and lack of weight of the water above its outlet, as it has only half the water of the larger one.
<note>Leonardo is observing that as the water level drops, the pressure—and thus the flow rate—decreases. Since the smaller tank has less "head" of water to begin with, its pressure drops to zero much faster than the larger tank's pressure drops to its halfway point, causing the ratio between their flow rates to change constantly.</note>

## CHAPTER IV.

#### *Of reservoirs, which outlet throws water the farthest.*

Of outlets of equal width of air <note>Likely referring to the diameter of the opening exposed to the air.</note>, and with centers at equal depth, that one which is of a larger shape will throw its water the farthest. However, the distance of the centers of their <term>percussione: the point of impact where the water hits the ground</term> shall be at an equal distance from their respective outlets. 

The outlet NM (fig. 168. plate 30) has its center A as far from the top N and bottom M of its opening as the outlet OP has its center B. But the lower boundaries of the one and the other are different; for the bottom edge of the larger outlet is further from the surface than that of the smaller outlet, and therefore it is more burdened by the water pressing upon it than [the other]...
<note>Leonardo is explaining that even if the centers of two holes are at the same depth, a larger hole extends further down. Therefore, the water at the bottom of the larger hole is under higher hydrostatic pressure than any part of the smaller hole, causing the stream from the larger hole to project further.</note>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes how the height of water in a cistern affects the speed of its discharge, noting that flow ratios change as vessels empty. He also examines how the size and depth of an outlet aperture influence the distance the water is projected due to pressure.</summary>

<keywords>hydrostatics, water flow, pressure, discharge rate, reservoirs, apertures, Leonardo's physics, fluid mechanics</keywords>

it is not the smaller <term>spiracolo: aperture or outlet</term>, and for this reason it is more powerful, and consequently it pushes the lower part of its water further than that which is pushed by the smaller outlet, etc.

### CHAPTER V.

->*Of the water that pours from the reservoir, the upper part is overcome*<-
->*by the lower .*<-

The water that pours from the <term>bottino: reservoir or cistern</term> into the air—its lower part overcomes and surpasses the upper; and this is because it is more powerful, as it remains weighted down by the upper part.

### CHAPTER VI.

->*On the same subject .*<-

Always regarding the water that pours from its reservoir into the air, its upper part obeys the lower part; and this is due to the previous [argument], which shows how the upper part is overcome and carried along by the lower. The lower part, by being pressed by the upper, becomes more powerful than that upper part, which has nothing pressing upon it.

### CHAPTER VII.

->*On the same subject .*<-

Water that pours through outlets of continuous width <note>Leonardo is likely describing a tall, vertical rectangular slit rather than a circular hole.</note> will have its upper and lower extremities failing to follow their destined course. Instead, the upper increases in power just as much as the lower decreases it. If outlet A throws its water to E, and outlet B throws it to D; it follows that if the two outlets A and B were made continuous throughout the entire space AB (fig. 169. <term>tavola: plate</term> 30), all the water from such an outlet would throw itself to C. This happens because the part that previously went from B to D is then burdened by the weight AE, which bends it toward C; and the water AE, which previously fell to E, is then supported by the motion of BD.

<note>original vocabulary:
spiracolo — aperture or outlet
bottino — reservoir or water-collection tank
aggravata — weighted down or burdened
infra l'aria — through the air
tavola — plate (referring to the technical illustrations)</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains how the pressure from the upper layers of a water column accelerates the lower layers, causing a unified stream to follow a different trajectory than individual jets would. He argues that the weight of the water above "pushes" the water below, creating a collective momentum.</summary>
<keywords>hydrodynamics, water pressure, trajectories, fluid mechanics, cisterns, outlets, gravitational weight</keywords>

### CHAPTER VIII.

->**Making a cut in the reservoir at the surface,**<-
->**which water exits first.**<-

When water is in a <term>bottino: a reservoir or cistern</term>, or a channel with banks of equal height, and between these banks there is a square cut—similar to the <term>vacui: gaps or openings</term> found between the <term>merlons: the solid upright sections of a castle battlement</term>—the water within the channel that is directly opposite this "vein" <note>The "vein" refers to the specific stream or current of water flowing toward the exit.</note> will move toward it, while the rest will be slower. This is because, as the water exits the reservoir, it becomes lower than the water to its right and left; therefore, it is necessary for the water on the right to drop and move to the left, and the water on the left to move to the right.

### CHAPTER IX.

->***On the same subject.***<-

Here, the exit is given to the water near its surface, and the question is asked: which part of the water's surface will take on the fastest or slowest motion in providing water to such an exit? To establish a rule, you shall place particles of things that float and are of equal size, such as certain tiny seeds of herbs, and place them in a circle equidistant from the exit. Observe the first one that reaches the mouth while the water is still, looking at the circle, and in this way, you will create a rule (fig. 170. <term>tavola: plate</term> 30).

### CHAPTER X.

->**Which water pours first from the outlet situated below the surface.**<-

If I allow the water enclosed in the square to escape at point B (fig. 171. plate 30), it is a clear thing that nothing will exit through the aforementioned hole except the water that is directly above the hole in a perpendicular line. The reason for this is that above B, at point A, you will see a small hole form <note>Leonardo is describing the "vortex" or depression that forms on the surface when a drain is opened below.</note>. One asks if the water that exits is any water other than that which is in a perpendicular line above the hole. I say no, because it is easier for the surface of the water—which is enclosed between the air and the other water—to move, than it is for the water enclosed above and below between two other layers of water. For example <note>original: "Verbi-grazia"</note>, if you have an <term>orologio: hourglass or sand-glass</term> full of powder, and its perpendicular line is full of red powder while the rest is white, the red will pour out before the white.

<vocab>hydraulics, reservoir, vein, outlet, hourglass, surface, perpendicular</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo examines how water moves toward an opening at the surface and demonstrates through an hourglass analogy that a submerged outlet draws water primarily from the column directly above it.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, surface tension, vortex formation, experimental method, reservoir design, hydraulics</keywords>

### CHAPTER XI.
#### *On the same subject.*

To see which water in a vessel is that which moves toward the exit at the bottom of said vessel, take (fig. 172. plate 30) two square glass plates, a quarter of a <term>braccio: a traditional Italian unit of measurement, literally "an arm," approximately 23 inches or 58 centimeters</term> in size. Place them close to one another, separated only by the width of two knife-backs <note>original: "coste di coltello"; Leonardo is creating a very thin, transparent tank to observe a two-dimensional cross-section of the flow</note> with uniform spacing, and seal the edges on three sides with wax. Then, through the fourth side at the top, fill it with clear water in which tiny seeds are scattered, such that they are floating throughout the entire height of this water. Next, you shall make a small hole in the bottom and allow the water to exit, keeping your eye fixed on the face of the vessel. In this way, the motion of these seeds will inform you which water is running with the greatest velocity toward the exit, and from which position it moves.

### CHAPTER XII.
#### *On the same subject.*

If you wish to test which part of the water in a pouring vessel is the part that comes out, perform such an experiment with a vessel of <term>millet: a tiny, round grain used here to simulate the movement of water particles</term>, which is slippery and minute. Plug and unplug various holes in this vessel, and observe whether the top surface of the millet drops in that part which stands perpendicularly above the exit below, or not. And if you should say that this is not a valid experiment because water is a united and continuous quantity, while millet is discontinuous and divided—to this I answer you that I wish to take that license which is common to mathematicians; that is, just as they divide time into degrees <note>intervals or units</note> and turn a continuous quantity into a discontinuous one, I too will do the same, using millet as a comparison for water.

### CHAPTER XIII.
#### *On the same subject.*

That part of the water's surface which will pour through the bottom will be lowest where it is closest to the perpendicular line of its discharge. This is proven by the reasoning of the tenth <note>referring to a previous theorem or chapter</note> passed, and by the following.

<summary>Leonardo describes experimental methods for visualizing internal fluid currents, including a thin glass observation tank and the use of millet as a discrete-particle model for continuous fluid flow.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, experimental method, visualization, glass plates, millet model, continuous quantity, mathematics of flow</keywords>

### CHAPTER XIV.

***Why a concavity forms on the surface of a pouring vessel.***

If you make a hole in the vessel at point E (fig. 173, plate 30), the wine that is located between A and B is compressed between the wine at NM and the bottom of the vessel at RF. If you make the hole at L, you remove a portion of the support <note>original: "sostentacolo"; the column of liquid that previously held up the water above it</note> for the line AB; consequently, the line at that point bends. In bending, it aligns itself with the perpendicular line above it, and as the surface of the wine—which desires to remain level—realigns itself, it attempts to fill the hollowed-out area with its own substance. However, because the wine falling in a straight line moves with greater speed than the wine flowing across the surface toward that central hollow, the hollowed-out area maintains its concavity; the "rescue" <note>Leonardo uses the word "soccorso" (help/aid), personifying the liquid as it rushes to fill the void</note> from the surface arrives more slowly than the liquid descends through the hole.

### CHAPTER XV.

***That the surface restores the drop in the wine pouring from the vessel.***

I maintain that when wine descends through a low <term>spiracle: an opening or vent; here, a drainage hole at the bottom of a tank</term>, the surface of the wine is more suited to restoring the loss of that wine than any other part of the liquid. The reason is that a body will move more easily when it is enclosed within something less resistant; the wine on the surface is enclosed between the air and the rest of the wine. However, the wine beneath the surface is enclosed between wine and wine. Therefore, because the wine on the surface exists between a light body <note>the air</note> and a heavy body <note>the liquid</note>, it will move more easily than the wine below, which is caught between two heavy bodies.

### CHAPTER XVI.

***On the true measurement of the water’s flow.***

Water pouring through an opening of the same size can vary in quantity in sixteen ways, of which:
The first is the surface of the water being higher or lower above the <term>mouth: the "bocca" or aperture through which the water exits</term> from which it pours.
The second is the water passing with greater or lesser velocity from a uniform bank <note>the side or wall of the container</note> where that mouth is made.
The third is the degree to which the lower sides of the thickness of the mouth <note>the thickness of the vessel's wall</note> where the water passes are more or less slanted.
The fourth is the variety of the slant of the sides of such a mouth.

vessel, surface, concavity, outlet, flow/wave, water aperture, velocity

<summary>Leonardo explains the physics behind the "dimple" seen on the surface of a draining liquid, noting that the downward pull of gravity is faster than the liquid's ability to level itself. He also introduces a list of sixteen variables—such as pressure and aperture shape—that change how much water flows through a hole.</summary>
<keywords>fluid dynamics, surface tension, atmospheric resistance, hydraulics, drainage, velocity, Leonardo da Vinci, scientific observation</keywords>

This list concludes with the sixteenth factor before moving into Chapter XVII, which examines the relationship between water pressure (depth) and the volume of the "water-ounce" unit of measurement.</meta>

The fifth concerns the variety of the thickness of the sides of the opening.
The sixth concerns the shape of the opening, which may be round, square, triangular, or elongated.
The seventh concerns the placement of the opening at a greater or lesser slant <term>obliquità: the angle or slope of the bank</term> of the bank relative to its length.
The eighth concerns the placement of the opening at a greater or lesser slant of the bank relative to its height.
The ninth concerns the placement of the opening in a wider or narrower part of the channel.
The tenth is whether the water in the channel moves with greater velocity at the height of the opening, or with more slowness than elsewhere.
The eleventh is whether the bottom has a bulge <term>globosità: a rounded, convex protrusion</term> or a hollow opposite the opening.
The twelfth is whether the water passing through such an opening catches the wind or not.
The thirteenth is whether the water falling out of the opening falls through the open air, or if it is enclosed on one side, or on all sides except for the front.
The fourteenth is whether the falling water, when enclosed, has a long or short distance to fall.
The fifteenth is whether the falling water, being enclosed, is thick or thin within its vessel.
The sixteenth is whether the sides of the channel from which the water descends are solid or bulging, straight or curved.

### CHAPTER XVII.

->*The quantity of the <term>water-ounce: a traditional Italian unit of flow (oncia d'acqua), often defined by the amount of water passing through a specific sized hole</term> according to the height or depth of the surface.*<-

Regarding water that does not drop below its regular surface level, the quantity of water that pours through a given <term>spiracle: an opening or vent used for drainage</term> in a given time will be proportional to the height of the water above that spiracle. I say that if opening B (fig. 174, plate 30) pours a certain quantity of water in a certain time, opening C will pour twice as much water in the same amount of time; this is because it has twice as much weight of water pressing down above it. And here, the proportions of the weights do not behave like solid, unified objects falling through the air. This is because water, by striking the air, creates a continuous "hole" through it. But a solid and unified object descending through the air must successively push the air open in front of it; the air offers some resistance and, consequently, becomes somewhat compressed. Because of this, the air does not yield a path without resistance to a moving object of a fixed length, unlike water, which is of an indefinite length. <note>Leonardo is making an early observation about aerodynamics and fluid dynamics. He suggests that a continuous stream of water "clears the path" for itself, whereas a solid object (like a stone) constantly meets new resistance from the air it must displace.</note>

<vocab>water-ounce, spiracle, bank, slant, bulge, hollow, resistance, indefinite length</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo lists the final factors affecting how water exits a vessel and introduces Chapter XVII, where he explains that the volume of water flow is directly proportional to the weight (depth) of the water above the opening. He also distinguishes between the way a continuous stream of liquid and a solid object move through the resistance of the air.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, water pressure, oncia d'acqua, fluid dynamics, atmospheric resistance, flow velocity, spiracle, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from page 420, Leonardo continues his investigation into the variables of water flow. # LEONARDO DA VINCI

## CHAPTER XVIII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

If the channel decreases in height while pouring water through its openings <note>original: "spiracoli," referring here to vents or apertures in the side of a water channel</note> placed at different distances from its bottom, as in the figure shown above, then that opening which is closer to the surface of the water in said channel loses more of its ordinary flow than the one situated below it. Let us say that for the two openings D and C, the entire height of the water AB decreases from above; in this case, C loses half of its power <term>potenza: in this context, the force of pressure or the volume of flow</term>, because AB is equal to BC. The opening D loses a third of its power, because AD <note>the scribe likely refers to the total height segment</note> contains AB three times. Thus it follows that in the same time, by removing the height of water AB, the opening C loses three parts while D loses only one. For if CA has a power of six, by removing AB, C will remain with a power of three, because half of the height has been removed; and in such a case, DA, which had a power of nine, by losing AB (which represents three), remains with a power of six. Therefore, when ABC is missing, C loses half and D loses a third, and so E and F will do proportionately.

## CHAPTER XIX.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Does a given double height above the water's support give double the water, or more, or less? The answer is that it gives double. This is proven by the load <note>the weight or pressure</note> that the water below receives from that which is added above; for the first given thickness was one ounce <term>oncia: the "water-ounce," a traditional Italian unit of flow volume</term> pressed by the weight of another ounce. By adding another ounce above, the first aforementioned ounce below has doubled the weight that previously pressed upon it, and consequently, it has doubled the motion in velocity, in the quantity of space covered, and in the abundance of water.

## CHAPTER XX.

->*On the same subject.*<-

If, above three degrees <note>units of height or "steps" of water depth</note>, another degree of water is added, the bottom degree acquires a sesquialter power <term>sesquilatera: a ratio of 3:2, or 1.5 times the original amount</term>. This is because it was previously pressed by three, and now one has grown over two <note>Leonardo is calculating the relative increase in pressure units</note>. From this, it follows that to increase the water by a specific measure, it is necessary to increase the mouths <term>bocchelli: the nozzles or discharge openings</term> in width <note>original: "per fronte," across the front</note>, and not in height or depth.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes how changes in water depth (head pressure) proportionally affect the flow rate of openings at various levels, concluding that doubling the depth doubles the flow. He ultimately advises that for precise water measurement, one should adjust the width of an opening rather than its depth to avoid complex pressure calculations.</summary>

<keywords>hydraulics, water pressure, water-ounce, flow rate, mathematical proportions, fluid mechanics, spiracles, Leonardo's physics</keywords>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

### CHAPTER XXI.

->On the same subject.<-

If a support <note>likely a weir or a sill over which water flows</note> allows the passage of a given quantity of water two inches in thickness, and a third inch is added to it, then the bottom inch doubles its power, its velocity, and its volume. This is proven by the following principle, which shows that for waters flowing over the beds of rivers of uniform slope, the proportions of the velocity of motion are the same as the proportions of their heights. Therefore, if the first inch mentioned above is pressed by another inch, and then by two inches, the pressing power is undoubtedly doubled; consequently, as stated, the velocity and the quantity are doubled, etc.

### CHAPTER XXII.

->On the same subject.<-

Regarding waters flowing over the beds of rivers of uniform slope <term>obliquità: the angle of descent or inclination of the riverbed</term>, the proportions of the velocity of motion are the same as the proportions of their height. This is proven by the fifty-ninth proposition of the second book, which states: of two straight rivers of equal width and slope, the one that is deeper will be faster.

### CHAPTER XXIII.

->On the same subject.<-

Every movement of water of equal width and surface <note>meaning the top surface of the water remains level across the width</note> flows so much faster in one place than in another as it is shallower in one place than in another. This proposition is clearly proven by the fiftieth proposition of the second book, which says: where the water is faster, it is of lesser depth, provided the river is of equal width; and by the fifty-second of the same book, which says: for water moved through an equal width and bottom, as much as it is faster in one place than another, it will be proportionately thinner <note>or "shallower"</note>. And this is manifest; for although the river may be of equal width and surface, and not of equal depth, it is nonetheless necessary that the course of said river also be of unequal movement, for the reasons that will be stated in the forty-first proposition of this book. Let us suppose that in the figure RSTU (fig. 175, plate 31) represents the equal width of the river, and that AB, CD, EF, MN...

<summary>Leonardo explains the mathematical relationships between water depth and velocity, noting that increased depth adds pressure that accelerates flow, while also observing that within a single river of constant width, the water must flow faster where the channel is shallower.</summary>

LEONARDO DA VINCI 423

...should its height be unequal; I say that NM will be the water of so much greater movement than the water AB, as NM enters into AB, which enters there four times, and three times more than in CD, and twice as much as in EF.

->### CHAPTER XXIV.<-

->*On the same subject.*<-

If the volume <note>literally "thickness"</note> of the falling water is doubled in every part of its depth, its power is doubled. Let us divide the height of the water AB into eight levels <term>gradi: steps or degrees of measurement</term>, and let us first take the bottom level, which was weighed down by the remaining height, which is seven levels. If the water above is doubled by the addition of HA (fig. 176, plate 31), you will have loaded it with eight more levels than before, which double the seven plus one more. And if you wish to account for a quarter of such a height of water, that is, two levels, you will have doubled its weight by one more, because previously there were three quarters weighing down the first quarter, and now there are seven quarters. And if you wish to divide such height AB into two levels, you have doubled the weight on the first level by one more, because previously there was only one level pressing on the first, and now there are three. And if you wish to speak of the entire height AB and double its weight, the power of this is doubled. But that extra "one" of power is no longer there.

->### CHAPTER XXV.<-

->*On the same subject.*<-

If the head <term>battente: the height of water above an opening that creates pressure</term> above the water's opening is decreased by half, then the flow from such an opening decreases in the same proportion as the weight from above is decreased; for example <note>original: "verbi gratia"</note>, if the opening was one ounce <term>oncia: a traditional unit used to measure water discharge</term>, without doubt the weight pressing that said ounce above its opening is decreased by half, and as a result, that ounce continues to decrease the volume of its water by half. Thus, the water that previously flowed out in one hour now takes two hours. And if such an opening were two ounces, and the head two, and you raised the opening by one ounce, then you decrease half of the power of that head; therefore, the flow from that nozzle <term>bocchello: a small opening or outlet</term> is not decreased by half, but by a quarter, because as stated above, decreasing half of the head, which was double the ounce pressed by it, leaves the head equal to the ounce, and in this way, according to the head, half of the pressed water remains.

<summary>Leonardo examines the physics of hydrostatic pressure, calculating how the weight of a water column ("the head") directly correlates to the power and speed of discharge from an opening.</summary>

LEONARDO DA VINCI 424

### CHAPTER XXVI.

->*The quantity of the water-inch <term>oncia: a standard unit for measuring water flow, traditionally defined by the size of a specific opening</term> according to the greater or lesser velocity*<-
->*of the water passing through a uniform embankment where*<-
->*said opening is located.*<-

The measurements of the water-inches issued through the water-outlets are greater or lesser according to the greater or lesser speeds of the water passing through said outlet. Double velocity yields double the water in the same amount of time, and likewise, triple velocity will yield a triple quantity of water in the same time, and so it would continue successively to infinity.

### CHAPTER XXVII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

That aperture or opening pours water with greater abundance when it receives the water with greater velocity. This occurs because the water does not slow down in its velocity until it has finished the course of its momentum <term>impeto: the kinetic energy or force of the moving water</term>.

### CHAPTER XXVIII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

If that aperture pours water with greater abundance, it is because it receives the water with greater velocity; therefore, among waters that pour through equal apertures during equal time, that which passes through its aperture with greater velocity will be the most abundant.

### CHAPTER XXIX.

->*The quantity of the water-inch according to the greater or lesser slope*<-
->*of the lower sides of the opening of said water-inch.*<-

Given equal and similar openings placed at the same height on the inner sides of the cistern <term>bottino: a reservoir or header tank used to collect and distribute water</term>, that which slopes downward further upon exiting its wall will pour more water; that is, C will pour more water than D (fig. 177. plate 31). This is because the water in opening C will be faster according to the twenty-seventh [rule] of the second [book], which says: *water becomes so much faster as it has a greater slope.* <note>original Italian: "l’acqua si fa tanto più veloce, quanto ha maggior declinazione"</note>

<note>Key terms: water-inch, opening, embankment, aperture, momentum, cistern, slope</note>

## CHAPTER XXX.

### *The quantity of the water-inch according to the greater or lesser angle of the sides of the outlet.*

Among equal and similar outlets <term>bocca: literally "mouth," referring to the aperture through which water is drawn</term>, the one that has its sides angled more sharply toward the oncoming current of the channel will pour more water; conversely, the one with sides angled more toward the downstream flow of the same current will pour less. That is, outlet A (fig. 178, plate 31) will pour more water than outlet B. This is caused by the fact that the reflected motion <term>moto riflesso: the way water changes direction after striking a surface</term> created by the current at point A is faster than that at point B, according to the eleventh proposition of the second book, which says: that reflected motion will be faster which reflects at a more acute angle.

---

## CHAPTER XXXI.

### *The quantity of the water-inch according to the variation in the thickness of the sides.*

Of outlets of equal width, shape, and height, the one set in a thinner wall—or rather, the one having shorter contact with the sides of its opening—will pour more water in the same amount of time. That is, A will pour more water than B (fig. 179, plate 31). This is due to the twenty-third proposition of the second book, which says: the water will be faster the more remote it is from the bank <term>argine: the embankment or barrier that creates friction against the water</term> that obstructs it; therefore, the water of the water-inch <term>oncia: a standard unit of water flow</term> will be faster if it has shorter contact with the sides of its outlet.

---

## CHAPTER XXXII.

### *The quantity of the water-inch according to the shape of the outlet.*

Among water outlets placed at equal heights below the surface of the water in its cistern <term>bottino: a header tank or reservoir used to regulate water distribution</term>, the one that has the least contact with the water passing through it will least obstruct the transit of that water. Let the equal outlets be square A and circle B (fig. 180, plate 31); I say that the water passing through the circular outlet will have less contact than the water passing through a square equal in area to that circle. This is because the line surrounding the square is longer than the line surrounding the circle; therefore, the square will pour less water than the circle by reason of its shape <note>Leonardo is observing the effect of "wetted perimeter" friction; a circle has the smallest perimeter for a given area, thus minimizing friction.</note>.

<summary>Leonardo explains how the physical design of a water outlet affects flow volume, noting that outlets angled against the current, those with thinner walls, and circular shapes deliver more water by reducing friction and resistance.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, oncia, water-inch, fluid dynamics, friction, wetted perimeter, outlet geometry, flow measurement</keywords>

### CHAPTER XXXIII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Among outlets <term>bocca: literally "mouth," the aperture through which water is drawn from a canal</term> of equal area and placed at an equal height, the one that has the greater portion of its area in its lower part, rather than its upper part, will pour more water in the same amount of time. These four outlets are equal to each other, and their edges are positioned at an equal height. A (fig. 181, plate 31) pours less from the middle downwards than B, and C less than D. In this case, the round outlet pours a smaller quantity of water than the square one <note>Leonardo observes that because water pressure increases with depth, a shape that concentrates more of its opening at a lower level—like a square compared to a circle—will discharge more water</note>.

### CHAPTER XXXIV.

->*On the same subject.*<-

When the center of a circle is at the same height as the center of a triangle of equal capacity <note>meaning equal surface area</note> as the circle, then the triangle will pour more water than the circle. But if the center of natural gravity <term>centro della gravità naturale: the geometric center or centroid of the shape</term> of the triangle is at the same height as the center of the circle, then the water poured by the triangle and the circle will be equal.

### CHAPTER XXXV.

->*Quantity of the water-inch relative to the mouth being placed at a greater or lesser obliquity of the banks along its length.*<-

The more the bank where the mouth of the water-inch <term>oncia: a standard unit of water flow used for irrigation and utility billing</term> is placed is oblique <note>slanted or angled</note> along the direction of its length, the greater the quantity of water that mouth will pour; and this is because the current will have a greater decline, and consequently a greater velocity.

### CHAPTER XXXVII.

->**Quantity of the water-inch relative to the greater or lesser width of the channel.**<-

To the same extent that you increase the river in width, you will decrease the quality of its movement <note>In this context, Leonardo uses "quality of movement" to refer to the velocity or speed of the flow</note> given an equal slope of the bed. Because, if the water entering into other water is one cubit <term>braccio: a traditional Italian unit of measurement, the "Florentine arm," roughly 58 centimeters</term> thick, and that which receives the said incoming water runs one cubit per unit of time, the channel being one square cubit, then that channel which is two square cubits in width will cause the same water to run half a cubit per unit of time. And so, step by step, as much as you increase the width of the river, so much will you decrease the velocity of its movement.

### CHAPTER XXXVIII.

->**On the same subject.**<-

To the same extent that you decrease the width of the river, you will increase the quality of its movement given an equal slope of the bed. This is the converse of the previous proposition, and it is self-evident.

### CHAPTER XXXIX.

->**On the same subject.**<-

The motion of every river, over an equal period of time, yields an equal weight of water in every part of its length. And this occurs because if the river, at its outfall <term>sboccamento: the mouth or point where a river or channel discharges its water</term>, discharges a certain weight of water in a certain time, necessity demands that an equal weight of water succeeds it from the preceding part in that same amount of time. This happens successively, one part of the water replacing the other, until one reaches the first part of the river’s length. Otherwise, if a greater amount of water were discharged at the outfall than is found at the start of the river, it would follow that the water in the middle of the channel would continually be depleted; and conversely, if a smaller amount of water passed through the same outfall than enters at its source, the water in the middle... <note>The text ends mid-sentence, following the logic that the water would otherwise overflow or back up</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains the Law of Continuity in fluid dynamics, stating that a river's velocity is inversely proportional to its width and that the volume of water passing through any section of a continuous stream must be constant.</summary>

## CHAPTER XL.

->*On the same subject.*<-

In every part of its length, over an equal period of time, the river allows an equal quantity of water to pass through, regardless of any variation in the river's width or depth; and this is made clear by the previous proposition.

## CHAPTER XLI.

->*On the same subject.*<-

A river of equal depth will have as much more speed <note>original: "fuga", meaning the velocity or rush of the current</note> in its narrower section as the greater width exceeds the lesser. This proposition is clearly proven by reasoning confirmed by experience. For when water passes through a channel one mile wide, in a place where the river [elsewhere] is five miles wide, each square mile [of the wider section] will contribute one-fifth of itself to replace the missing square mile of water in the [narrow part of] the river; and where the river is three square miles wide, each of these square miles will contribute one-third of its quantity to account for the square mile of the narrow section; otherwise, the previous statement would not be true, which says: *that the river allows the passage of an equal quantity of water in every part of its length, whatever the width of the river may be.* Therefore, a river of equal depth will have as much more speed, etc. N (fig. 182. plate 32) is the narrow mile; F G H are the three square miles; and A B C D E are the five square miles.

Example: if there is a place that has three different widths, which are joined together, and the first (smaller) width fits into the second four times, and the second fits twice into the third; I say that if there were men who filled these spaces with their bodies, and these men were in continuous motion, when the men in the largest space take one step, those in the second, smaller space will take two, and those in the third place, which is even smaller, will take five steps in that same time <note>Leonardo is illustrating the inverse relationship between the size of a space and the speed of those moving through it. While his specific numbers here serve as a general example of increasing speed, the core concept is that as the "pipe" narrows, the "crowd" must move faster to keep the flow constant.</note>. And you will find this proportion in all movements that pass through places of various widths. See the syringe <term>schizzatoio: a mechanical device, such as a squirt or piston-pump, used to eject liquid through a nozzle</term> above: when its plunger <term>maschio: literally "male," referring here to the piston or solid part of the pump that displaces the fluid</term> that pushes out the water moves one finger's width, the first water that...

### CHAPTER XLV.

->*The quantity of the ounce according to the roundness or hollowness of the bed near the opening.*<-

If the bed of the water <note>original: "fondo", the bottom of the channel or river</note> opposite the smallness of the <term>ounce</term> <note>The <term>oncia</term> (ounce) was a standard Renaissance unit for measuring water flow, defined by the amount of water passing through a hole of a specific diameter under a set pressure.</note> has roundness or hollowness, the opening will discharge less water. This occurs because the water is delayed there by the reflected motions <note>original: "moti riflessi", referring to eddies, turbulence, or back-currents</note> that are generated from the bed to the surface, as is proved in the second [book] regarding the <unclear>...</unclear> which says: *water with an uneven bed creates contrary motions from the bed to the surface, and vice versa, etc.*

### CHAPTER XLVI.

->*The quantity of the ounce according to the air trapped in the opening.*<-

That opening will discharge as much less water as there is more air mixed with the water trapped <note>original: "ingorgherà", literally 'engorged' or 'clogged'</note> in said opening; this happens because, firstly, less water passes through, and secondly, because the air delays the water in its descent by making it lighter <note>Leonardo recognizes that aeration reduces the density of the water column, thereby reducing the gravitational force driving the flow</note>.

### CHAPTER XLVII.

->*Why air is driven into the water pipes.*<-

Because [the section] AB (fig. 183. plate 32.) is lighter than CD, AB cannot provide as much water as CD consumes; therefore, air necessarily enters through CD in place of that quantity of water representing the difference between what was previously in CD and AB, and thus the water in CD remains equal to the weight of the water in AB.

### CHAPTER XLVIII.

->*The quantity of the ounce according to the fall of the water, whether through the air or through pipes.*<-

Water moving through a horizontal <note>original: "equigiacente", meaning level or lying on an even plane</note> pipe is thicker <note>In hydraulic terms, "grossa" (thick) refers to the volume or cross-sectional area of the water stream</note> than that which runs through an open channel, especially when such a pipe receives

## CHAPTER XLIX.
### *On the same subject.*

Of equal and similar openings, the one that falls freely through the air will discharge less water in the same amount of time than the one that falls enclosed on all sides, except for the front. This is proven by the preceding [proposition] <note>Leonardo argues that water falling through an enclosed pipe is shielded from the air resistance that slows down a free-falling stream</note>.

## CHAPTER L.
### *Quantity of the ounce according to the length or shortness of the pipes.*

Water that moves by direct descent through <term>pipes</term> <note>original: "canne", referring to tubes or conduits</note> of uniform length will be faster the longer the pipe is. And this is proven by the .... <note>The manuscript leaves the specific proposition number blank</note> of the fifth [book], which says that water descending in every degree of descent acquires a degree of velocity, etc. <note>Leonardo is describing the principle of gravitational acceleration: the further water falls, the faster it travels</note>.

## CHAPTER LI.
### *Quantity of the ounce according to the thickness and thinness of the pipes.*

Water that moves through a similar descent through a pipe of equal length will have a faster motion the greater the width of the pipe. This is proven because the central line of such water is further from the <term>friction</term> <note>original: "confregazione", meaning the resistance or "rubbing" of the water against the pipe walls</note> of the wide pipe than that of the narrow one; for this reason it becomes faster, and besides this, it is also faster because it is of greater weight <note>Leonardo correctly identifies that in a wider pipe, a smaller proportion of the water is slowed down by contact with the walls, while the greater mass of the water increases the downward force</note>, etc.

## CHAPTER LII.
### *Quantity of the ounce for smooth and bumpy pipes.*

Water that discharges through a similar descent through pipes of equal length will be less abundant the more the pipes are winding and <term>bumpy</term> <note>original: "globulose", likely referring to pipes with internal irregularities or bulbous sections</note>. This is clear because of the <term>eddies</term> <note>original: "reflessi", literally 'reflections', referring here to turbulent back-flows or counter-currents</note> which break the velocity and delay the water.

<summary>Leonardo examines how the physical properties of conduits—such as length, diameter, and internal texture—affect the velocity and volume of water flow, noting that friction and turbulence are the primary causes of deceleration.</summary>

### CHAPTER LIV.
->*On the same subject.*<-

Water that drops one <term>ounce</term> <note>original: "oncia"; a small unit of length, roughly 1/12 of a foot</note> per mile will have a movement of a quarter of a *braccio* per a single "time" (that is, a beat of <term>musical time</term>) <note>Leonardo frequently used the steady rhythm of music or a person's pulse to measure small intervals of time before the existence of precise mechanical clocks</note>. Water that drops two ounces per mile will have a movement of half a *braccio* per beat, and thus that which drops four ounces will move one *braccio* per beat, etc.

### CHAPTER LV.
->*The nature of water pipes.*<-

It is in the nature [of water] that a single pipe can project water an infinite distance away, because the height of the water head could be infinite—as illustrated by pipe BA (fig. 184, plate 32). Since this height can be infinite in the imagination, at every degree of height, the pipe AC acquires a degree of distance in its jet, which it makes with the water from afar, etc. <note>Leonardo is observing that the greater the vertical pressure (head), the further a horizontal jet of water will travel</note>.

### CHAPTER LVI.
->*On how many kinds of pipes there are.*<-

Pipes that discharge water are of three kinds: wide at the top and not at the bottom; wide at the bottom and not at the top; and uniform. There are two others that share these features: one that is thick in the middle and thin at the ends, and one that is wide at the ends and narrow in the middle, etc. <note>Leonardo is categorizing pipes by their internal geometry: tapering, expanding, cylindrical, bulging, or hourglass-shaped</note>.

<summary>Leonardo defines the proportional relationship between the slope of a watercourse and its velocity, while also categorizing the various geometric shapes of pipes and their effects on water pressure.</summary>
<keywords>hydrodynamics, water gradient, measurement units, braccia, musical time, pipe geometry, water pressure</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the discussion on how the height of a water source determines the distance of its jet, Leonardo now shifts to the mechanics of lifting water through pipes and the proportional relationships between the falling water (the power source) and the rising water.</meta>

LEONARDO DA VINCI 433

### CHAPTER LVII.
->*On water raised through a pipe.*<-

The stream of water that rises upward at every degree of motion from other water will be as much thinner <note>original: "sottile"; in this context, Leonardo refers to the volume or flow rate of the water being lifted compared to the volume of the source water</note> than the water which moves it, as its path is longer. Measure the water that falls by its height, and multiply it by the height to which it is to be raised, and this is the final and greatest amount that it will discharge. And as many times as the fall of the water fits into the width <note>Leonardo likely means the "extent" or "scale" of the lift</note> of its rise, so much thinner will it be than the water that ascends, etc.

### CHAPTER LVIII.
->*On the same subject.*<-

The weight of the water moved upward through any pipe <term>canna</term> <note>literally "a reed," used by Leonardo to describe a tube or conduit</note> beyond its natural level will have the same proportion to the weight of the other water that drives it, as the thickness of the pipe has to the thickness of the <term>cistern</term> <note>original: "bottino"; a reservoir or tank that provides the head of water for hydraulic pressure</note> from which it exits, provided that the thickness of the water that presses is similar to that of the cistern of water that is being pressed, etc.

### CHAPTER LIX.
->*On the same subject.*<-

It is impossible for the water that moves any instrument—that is, water which descends—to ever lift from the place where it rests an amount of water to the height from which it started that is equal to its own weight. This is proven by the eighty-fifth <note>Likely a reference to a specific proposition or theorem in one of Leonardo's other manuscripts or a classical text on mechanics</note>, which says: it is impossible for a descending weight to pull an equal weight back to the height from which it started over any length of time.

### CHAPTER LX.
->*On the same subject.*<-

The pipe through which water is pulled upward receives less detriment <note>original: "detrimento"; here meaning physical strain or wear on the conduit</note> than the pipe through which water is pushed. And this happens because, in the first case, the mover <note>original: "motore"; the source of force or the mechanism providing the lift</note> is located above, and in the second case, the mover is located below, etc.

<meta>The number 55 appears at the bottom center of the page.</meta>

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the physics of lifting water, establishing that a falling weight of water can never lift an equal volume back to its original height due to the laws of mechanics and proportional energy loss.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, water pipes, cisterns, mechanical force, suction vs pressure, Leonardo da Vinci, physics of motion</keywords>

### CHAPTER LXI.

->*On the annular pipes for the screw-pumps.*<-

The pipe of uniform width, and of annular <note>ring-shaped</note> figure (fig. 185. plate 32.) will always have its diameter dividing the water, which is enclosed within it, into two equal parts, etc.

### CHAPTER LXII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

That part of the water which is enclosed in the pipe of uniform width and annular figure (fig. 186. plate 32), will always be divided equally by the diameter of the aforesaid pipe, regardless of the slant at which it may be situated.

### CHAPTER LXIII.

->*On the same subject.*<-

If the annular pipe (fig. 187. plate 32) were divided at the upper part, and such divided parts were moved away from each other by transverse motion <note>Leonardo is describing how to "stretch" a ring into a single turn of a spiral or screw</note>, it will not prevent the diameter of such a ring from dividing the water that is inside such a pipe into two equal parts, etc.

### CHAPTER LXIV.

->*On the same subject.*<-

If the annular pipe divided at the uppermost part were placed at a slant, then the water which is enclosed within it will be divided into unequal parts by its diameter (fig. 188. plate 32). But such a part will be of greater or lesser inequality, depending on whether such a ring is situated at a greater or lesser slant, etc.

### CHAPTER LXV.

->*On the same subject.*<-

The more the <term>chiocciola</term> <note>literally "snail shell," the term used for an Archimedean screw-pump</note> that conducts the water upward is slanted, the greater the volume of water it receives into itself, and at the lower height it leaves it <note>The "lower height" refers to the fact that a more horizontal screw can carry more water in its coils but cannot lift it as high as a more vertical one</note>.

LEONARDO DA VINCI 435

And conversely, the less slanted it is, the smaller the quantity of water it contains within itself, and it carries it to a greater height, etc.

### CHAPTER LXVI.

->On the same subject.<-

A <term>chiocciola</term> <note>literally "snail," referring here to the Archimedean screw-pump</note> placed at a slant will always spill the water enclosed within it if it is not supported; and it spills it all the more quickly the less slanted its position is, and the number of turns it makes in its rotation is equal to the number of turns that compose the screw itself, etc.

### CHAPTER LXVII.

->On the same subject.<-

The central line of the width of the water enclosed in the screw-pump is further from the central line of the <term>subbio</term> <note>the central shaft or axle around which the screw is wound</note> of that screw-pump, the less slanted the position of said screw-pump is, etc.

### CHAPTER LXVIII.

->On the same subject.<-

The central line of the screw-pump never aligns with the central line of the world <note>the vertical axis pointing toward the center of the Earth's gravity</note> unless the screw-pump extends to the site of its qualities <note>the point of equilibrium or its vertical orientation</note>, etc.

### CHAPTER LXIX.

->On the same subject.<-

Among screw-pumps (fig. 189. plate 32) that have pipes of equal width, or water discharge, and equal slant over an equal length from the lowest to the highest opening of the pipe, the one whose pipe is wound around a thicker <term>subbio</term> <note>axle</note> will lift more water, etc.

### CHAPTER LXX.

->On the same subject.<-

Of an equal quantity of water, that which is lifted by the screw-pump with the greatest ease is the one which is spread over a lesser height, etc.

### CHAPTER LXXI.
->*On the same subject.*<-

Among <term>chiocciole</term> <note>Archimedean screw-pumps</note> of equal length and slant, and of the same thickness, that one will carry a smaller quantity of water to the same height which has a greater number of pipes wound around the <term>subbio</term> <note>the central axle or drum of the screw</note> (fig. 190. plate 32).

### CHAPTER LXXII.
->*On the Siphon.*<-

Every part of enclosed water desires to fall perpendicularly; and being impeded, it always exerts force and loads itself upon its <term>sostentacolo</term> <note>support or container</note>, and that support shall be more heavily burdened which is touched by a longer line <note>column</note> of water, etc.

### CHAPTER LXXIII.
->*On the same subject.*<-

When the water R (fig. 191. plate 32) is similar to the water F in height, M will perform the office of a balance-beam between these two channels, and the water MR will weigh as much as MF; and the water in vessel R will rest upon the <term>smussi</term> <note>the beveled or sloped edges</note> of its bottom, and where this vessel is <term>isfondato</term> <note>opened at the bottom</note>, the water will fall into R.

### CHAPTER LXXIV.
->*On the same subject.*<-

The water ABS (fig. 192. plate 33) will have no movement, because the water AB weighs as much as the water SA, and the line BS is full, and water that is full <note>in a state of equilibrium/fullness within a vessel</note> does not move by itself, etc.

### CHAPTER LXXV.
->*On the same subject.*<-

The water will make such movement through the <term>cicognola</term> <note>literally "little stork," Leonardo's term for a siphon</note> ABS shown above as it would make if it ran along the straight line SA, etc.

LEONARDO DA VINCI 437

## CHAPTER LXXVI.
### *On the same subject.*

The water of the <term>cicognola</term> <note>a siphon or curved tube used for transferring liquids</note> O L N (fig. 193. plate 33) will not produce a continuous motion, because the water L M, being greater in volume, weighs more than the water L O; and the water of lesser weight obeys and is pulled by the greater, etc.

## CHAPTER LXXVII.
### *On counterweights.*

If there is a balance A B F E (fig. 194. plate 33) consisting of two tubes joined at an angle at their lower parts, and the water contained within them is connected, and it has some oil on one side and simple water on the other; I say that the surfaces of the water in each tube will not remain at an equal level with the surface of the water in the opposite tube. This is proven because oil is less heavy than water, and for this reason it always stays above the water; and its weight in its tube, compared to the weight of the water that stands against it in the opposite tube connected to it, makes itself equal in total weight. But because it is stated that oil is less heavy than water, it is necessary—in order to equal and counterpoise the weight of the water that is missing beneath it—that there be a greater quantity of oil than the volume of water it replaces; and consequently, it must occupy more space in that tube than an equal weight of water would have done. Because of this, the surface of the oil is higher in its tube than the surface of the water is in the opposite tube; and the surface of the water that sits beneath the oil is lower than the surface of the water opposite it. The liquid A B is oil, the liquid C D F is water, etc.

## CHAPTER LXXVIII.
### *On the same subject.*

If the oil were half as light as the water, this instrument would have the surface of the water on one side level with the center of gravity of the oil; and let the tubes be as varied in thickness as one might wish, and the oil in whatever quantity one wishes, for such a rule will never vary from the aforementioned order, as you see in the instrument of the third figure already shown above, etc.

<summary>Leonardo explains the principles of fluid equilibrium within siphons and U-shaped tubes, demonstrating how liquids of different densities, like oil and water, reach a state of balance at different heights.</summary>
<keywords>hydrostatics, siphon, fluid equilibrium, density, oil and water, counterweights, Leonardo's physics</keywords>

### CHAPTER LXXIX.
->*On the same subject.*<-

The weight of water in the air is like the weight of an equal amount of lead in the water, and like the weight in distilled walnut oil, etc. <note>Leonardo is comparing the relative densities and the buoyant forces acting on objects within different mediums.</note>

### CHAPTER LXXX.
->*On the same subject.*<-

The counterweights that, by pressing on the <term>bottini</term> <note>original: "bottini"; here referring to the vessels or cylinders containing the water</note> of water, push such water upwards, are of three natures: that is, either of a heavier nature than the water, or of a lighter nature, or equal. Furthermore, they are of three shapes: that is, either wider than the width of the vessel, or narrower, or equal, etc.

### CHAPTER LXXXI.
->*On the same subject.*<-

Let the weight that presses be of a material that weighs four times more than an equal quantity of water would weigh according to its measurements, etc.

### CHAPTER LXXXII.
->*On the same subject.*<-

If the counterweight is of equal height to the thickness of the vessel it presses upon, then that part of the weight which acts and presses upon the water rising in the opposite tube will be proportional to the thickness of the <term>vacuo</term> <note>original: "vacuo"; refers to the void or internal bore of the tube</note> of said tube, etc.

### CHAPTER LXXXIII.
->*On the same subject.*<-

If the counterweight is ten times larger than the vessel it presses, the water that rises will lift ten times higher than the surface of the water of the counterweight, etc.

<summary>Leonardo examines the principles of hydrostatic pressure and displacement, specifically how the weight and size of a counterweight (acting as a piston) determines the height to which water will rise in a connected tube.</summary>
<keywords>hydrostatics, fluid mechanics, displacement, counterweights, density, water pressure, proportions</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page's discussion on the ratios of counterweights and the height of displaced water, Leonardo now applies these principles to the mechanical limits of lifting water, rebuking those who believe they can cheat the laws of physics.</meta>

LEONARDO DA VINCI 439

### CHAPTER LXXXIV.

#### *On the same subject.*

It is possible for a weight to descend in order to lift an equal weight back to the height from which it started, for a certain duration of time. Therefore, hold your tongue, you who wish to draw up a weight of water greater than the counterweight that lifts it. <note>Leonardo frequently used this sharp tone—"taci tu" (be silent)—to address "sophists" or amateur inventors who claimed to have discovered perpetual motion or machines that could create energy out of nothing.</note> It is true that if you lift a thousand <term>libbre</term> <note>original: "libbre"; Italian pounds, which varied by city but were generally around 330 grams.</note> to the height of one <term>braccio</term>, its descent will be able to move nearly two hundred pounds of water—and certainly no more—to a height of nine <term>braccia</term> <note>original: "braccio" (singular), "braccia" (plural); a Florentine unit of measurement based on an arm's length, approximately 58 centimeters.</note>, etc. <note>Leonardo is describing the law of work (Force x Distance). To lift a lighter weight (200 lbs) higher than the descending heavy weight (1000 lbs), the total "work" must remain balanced; mathematically, 1000 lbs dropped 1 unit can only lift 200 lbs by 5 units. His mention of "nine braccia" serves as a hyperbolic limit to show that the energy cannot be stretched indefinitely.</note>

---

<summary>Leonardo explains the conservation of work in mechanical systems, specifically stating that a counterweight cannot lift a volume of water heavier than itself, nor can it lift a lighter volume beyond a specific proportional height.</summary>
<keywords>hydrostatics, mechanical advantage, counterweights, law of work, hydraulics, physics of lifting, Florentine units</keywords>

# BOOK NINE
## ON MILLS AND OTHER WATER ENGINES

---

### CHAPTER I.
->*On mills.*<-

That water which is faster, drives its wheel more forcefully. This is proven because water has more force where it makes a more powerful <term>impact</term> <note>original: "percussione"; Leonardo uses this term to describe the kinetic energy transferred when water strikes a surface.</note>, and where it is faster, it is more powerful.

### CHAPTER II.
->*On the same.*<-

That water which gives less weight to its channel is faster; and that water gives less weight to its channel which is straighter, etc. <note>Leonardo observes that water flowing in a straight line experiences less friction and resistance against the walls of the conduit, allowing it to maintain higher velocity.</note>

### CHAPTER III.
->*On the same.*<-

The water of the mills must strike the <term>paddles</term> <note>original: "paldelle"; the blades or vanes of a water wheel.</note> and wheels at right angles; and this arises because that impact will be most powerful which is made between the most equal angles. <note>In Leonardo's geometry, an impact at "equal angles" refers to a perpendicular strike (90 degrees on both sides), which ensures the maximum transfer of force without glancing off.</note>

### CHAPTER IV.
->*On the same.*<-

That water which flows with a lesser <term>slope</term> <note>original: "pendente"; the gradient or incline of the water's path.</note> will strike the wheel further from the perpendicular of its fall (fig. 195, plate 33). And that water which strikes further from the perpendicular of its fall gives a lesser blow; and this is due to the trajectory, because the impact is made between more unequal angles, etc. <note>Leonardo is explaining that a shallow approach angle results in an oblique strike, which is less efficient than a direct, vertical, or perpendicular drop.</note>

<summary>Leonardo examines the physics of water mills, concluding that water's motive power is maximized by high velocity, straight channels, and perpendicular impacts on the wheel's paddles.</summary>
<keywords>water mills, hydraulics, kinetic energy, percussion, impact angles, fluid dynamics, paddles, engineering efficiency</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page's discussion on the physics of water impact and the importance of striking mill paddles at right angles, Leonardo now provides a specific mathematical comparison of power based on the angle (obliquity) and height of the water's fall.</meta>

->LEONARDO DA VINCI<-

## CAPITOLO V.
### *On the same subject.*

Here are demonstrated the true <term>powers</term> <note>original: "potenze"; in this context, Leonardo refers to the mechanical force or kinetic energy generated by the water.</note> of a quantity of water falling upon mill wheels through various angles from the same height to the same depth. We shall say, then, that the aforementioned water departs from height K (fig. 196, plate 33) and descends to the depth ST; its various angles are the lines KS—and I do not include this line among the oblique ones because it falls perpendicularly. The first oblique line will be KT, the second KX, and the third KY.

The water KS thus falls from site K and strikes the <term>paddle</term> <note>original: "pala"; the flat blade of the water wheel.</note> of the wheel EA in the middle of its descent. It has a power of four; and this water strikes said paddle with all its weight, because it is not supported by anything. Next is the descent of the water KT, which strikes the paddle FB at point F. This water falls one and a half times the distance of the fall of KE, as shown by line NF; but it loses half of its weight, as shown in MO against KO.

Now we must see and calculate the differences between the two aforementioned powers before we proceed further; and we shall say: if a power of four moves the paddle AE and falls one <term>braccio</term> <note>original: "braccio"; a common Italian unit of measurement (literally "an arm"), approximately 23 inches or 58 centimeters.</note> in height, with what power will the water move the paddle FB falling from a height of one and a half braccia, with a power of two? You will say thus: if the power were four, as before, and falling one and a half times higher, it would fall one and a half times more powerfully than before—that is, six. But because the power is only two, I must divide six in half, and say that half of six is three; whereby we have lost one degree of power from the first to the second wheel. And thus you shall proceed forward, and continue discussing the other wheels, etc.

## CAPITOLO VI.
### *Way to have water atop a mountain.*

This is a method to have water on the peak of a mountain, or other height. This method is performed in this manner. First, you shall make a <term>siphon-pump</term> <note>original: "cicognola"; Leonardo uses this term for various hydraulic devices, here referring to a system of pipes or a siphon designed to draw water upward through suction or pressure.</note> that starts from the base of the mountain—that is, from the first place where a well can be made with <term>living water</term> <note>original: "acqua viva"; naturally flowing or spring water, as opposed to stagnant water.</note>—and pull it up to the summit of the mountain, as appears from M to A, and make the bottom of A full of tiny holes (fig. 197, plate 34). 

Then, fill the space between A and B with fine <term>coarse sand</term> <note>original: "sabbione"; large-grained sand used for filtration.</note>, or washed river sand; then make other tiny holes in the partition that is between AB and PS, so that the water, penetrating through the sand, can penetrate into the vessel PS, and from there you will always have pure water. The <term>reservoir</term> <note>original: "bottino"; a term for a small cistern or water-collection chamber.</note>...

<summary>Leonardo calculates the loss of mechanical advantage when water falls at an angle rather than perpendicularly, concluding that height does not always compensate for a loss of weight. He also describes a hydraulic system using a siphon and sand filtration to bring clean water to a mountain peak.</summary>

<keywords>hydraulics, water mills, mechanical power, braccio, siphon, filtration, fluid dynamics, mountain water supply</keywords>

AC must be very large, and much greater than AB. But before you put in the <term>large gravel</term> <note>original: "sabbione"; refers to coarse sand or heavy gravel often used in these systems for filtration or to weight components.</note>, soak it with water, and likewise fill the vessel PS with water. When you have filled it with water up to point Q, unplug point M at the foot of the mountain.

### CHAPTER VII.

->On the same subject.<-

If you want to make water rise a mile, and have it remain atop the mountain, do as illustrated here on the back of this sheet. And if you want the water to be as thick as a leg, make the <term>conduit</term> <note>original: "condotto"; a pipe or channel for transporting water.</note> as thick as a thigh; and if it rises one mile, make it descend two miles. The momentum of the water will be so great at ABC (fig. 198. plate 35.) that it will draw up the water found in DE and turn the wheel of the <term>force-pumps</term> <note>original: "schizzaroli"; literally "squirts," these are reciprocating pump mechanisms used to move water through pressure.</note>. And know that through these pumps, no air can enter the <term>reservoir</term> <note>original: "bottino"; a cistern or collection chamber for water.</note>; because every time the <term>piston</term> <note>original: "anima"; literally the "soul," used here to mean the moving plunger or piston of the pump.</note> moves back, the <term>valve</term> <note>original: "animella"; literally "little soul," referring to the flap or check valve.</note> at the bottom of the reservoir closes. Even if it were not perfectly sealed, air still could not enter because it sits two <term>braccia</term> <note>original: "braccia"; an Italian unit of measurement (approx. 23 inches), the plural of "braccio".</note> underwater; thus, it cannot admit air unless it first admits those two braccia of water. And when you wish to fill the conduit at the start, gather a small lake of water via <term>irrigation ditches</term> <note>original: "roggie"; small canals or channels used for distribution.</note>, and plug the pipes at the bottom with earth—namely, mouth C and mouth E. Then release the lake into the conduit, and when the wheel is half a braccio underwater, seal the casing well; then suddenly unplug the conduit at the bottom at CE; and ensure the wheel is four braccia large.

### CHAPTER VIII.

->On the same subject.<-

Every large river can be led over very high mountains by the aforementioned principle of the <term>siphon</term> <note>original: "cicognola"; literally "little stork," Leonardo’s term for a siphon based on its bent, neck-like shape.</note>. If the river CDB (fig. 199. plate 36) sends a large branch to point A, and it falls back into point B, the weight of line AB will be so much greater than line AC that enough power can be "stolen" from it to serve in carrying ships over the mountains.

<summary>Leonardo describes the mechanical requirements for a siphon-and-pump system capable of moving water over high elevations, emphasizing the need for airtight seals and the use of water pressure to drive the mechanism. He suggests that these principles of physics could even allow entire rivers to be diverted over mountains for navigation.</summary>
