# LEONARDO DAVINCI

## CHAPTER IX.

### To create a perpetual motion of water.

Based on the [principles] of the past, the weight of water in the air is as the weight of an equal amount of lead in the water, or as the weight of marble in distilled walnut oil. The weight, therefore, is ten, the <term>tube</term> <note>original: "canna"; can refer to a pipe, cylinder, or rod. Here it describes the container for the weight.</note> holds nine, the lever is one, the motion toward one, and the counter-lever is one. The weight that presses in the air EF (fig. 200, plate 37) is so much water; but if such a weight sits under the water, it loses its weight, and to restore the weight under the water so that it weighs as much as water in the air, you must add lead. But to create the aforementioned motion, take distilled walnut and olive oil, and with this you shall make this motion, and its placement shall be in ABCD. The aforementioned motion will be generated by the first mover, which is GH, which by means of the lever NR and one counter-lever NO, will lift Q in EF. When GH releases its weight, EF regains its lost power and descends the space FV, and raises the lever NR to height X from where it first descended; there, having regained its heaviness, it falls again from X to R, and thus it shall be forever as long as the instrument lasts.

EF is nine in weight, and ON is one in counterweight, whereby the descent of EF pushes the liquid NS into the <term>channel</term> <note>original: "doccia"; a duct, gutter, or conduit for fluid.</note> NX. When the lightened lever NR returns to X, it becomes heavy again by one, and with that one, it descends with a power of more than nine. Because the line NR has a length of more than nine against one of the counter-lever NO, and by such power, it regains its position in R. And the weight F of nine rises up again, and remains with a power of nine, etc.

This instrument must descend straight and without any friction, so that the <term>leather</term> <note>original: "corame"; processed animal hide used for flexible seals or gaskets in Renaissance engineering.</note> is not worn away. And the hoops that surround such leather must stay on the outside, so that they prevent the excessive expansion of the leather. The empty tube AB serves as a sheath for the iron front, which holds the aforementioned instrument upright, etc. The weight increases as much as A rises to C, etc.

The same proportion that the space NM has with the space NB, the weight descended in D has with the weight that such D had at site B; it follows that, NM being eleven-tenths of NB, the weight descended in D is nine-tenths of the weight it had at height B. The tube NF is nine pounds; the permanent channel NB is eleven <term>braccia</term> <note>original: "braccia"; literally "arms," a common Italian Renaissance unit of length, roughly 23 inches or 58 centimeters.</note>; ND is eleven braccia; but it is worth ten because the site where weight D is found under M is worth a tenth of the line NM. The weight S is ten, which rests on the <term>balance beam</term> <note>original: "bilancia"; a scale or lever system used to compare weights.</note> CND against one in D: because the arm CN of the balance is similar to the arm NA, and arm NA goes ten times into the arm NM, which is equal to arm ND. Therefore, the motion of the balance NGD will remain four braccia high from the ground with the extreme low point of its counterweight, which is one—which one is in such proportion with ten...

<summary>Leonardo describes a complex hydraulic mechanism intended to achieve perpetual motion by balancing the weights of different fluids and lead components using levers and leather seals. He attempts to calculate the necessary mechanical advantage and buoyancy ratios to keep the system in constant cycles of ascent and descent.</summary>
<keywords>perpetual motion, hydraulics, levers, buoyancy, walnut oil, leather seals, units of measurement, braccia, counterweights</keywords>

--- Page 187 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues Leonardo's exploration of perpetual motion and hydraulic machines, specifically focusing on the mechanics of balances, bellows, and the use of oil as a hydraulic fluid to prevent mechanical failure.</meta>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

...counterweight S, which is attached to AN, the arm of the balance, with NM as its opposite arm. Therefore, the motion remains stationary until the weight D is nullified. Then it will rise to the first position B, where through S it will regain its lost power, and descend again to position D. The water D will pour out closer to B the less it weighs. But if you wish it to remain at its original weight, increase the thickness of the <term>tube</term> <note>original: "canna"; can refer to a pipe, cylinder, or rod. Here it describes the container for the weight or water.</note>, so that the volume of water rising to B matches the shorter descent of D; in this way, you will lift water to any height, as shown in (figs. 201, 202, plates 38 and 39). When the arm KA has descended to C, the arm KB will be at D, and when the arm KB descends to KF, the arm KA rises to KE, etc. The <term>bellows</term> <note>original: "mantice"; a device for moving air or fluid, here part of a hydraulic pump.</note> must always be filled with oil, rather than air or water. This is because air can be compressed and expanded, and water causes the <term>leather</term> <note>original: "corame"; specifically tanned leather used for flexible mechanical parts.</note> to rot. Oil does not rot the leather, though it may freeze unless it is kept underground, etc. Remember that when the movement of EA is smaller, the rod M descends less, and carries a smaller amount of material to D. For this reason, even though such material is lifted significantly from S (where it was taken) to A (where it is loaded), the quantity is so small that the benefit is of little value. Therefore, ensure that the quantity of material is greater the shorter its descent is. A small, controlled descent has a double advantage: it carries the material quite high and causes little variation in the weights on the arms of the balance. This is according to the <term>fourth rule of weights</term> <note>A principle from Leonardo’s studies on statics, derived from his interest in the "science of weights" (scientia de ponderibus).</note>, which states: the arms of a balance of equal length, joined at an angle at their pivot, will vary less in their power the more <term>obtuse</term> <note>An angle greater than 90 degrees.</note> that angle is, and the less their ends deviate in height from the level position.

### CHAPTER XI.

->Another method.<-

A (fig. 204, plate 41) is a screw that compresses the bellows.
B is a spring, which serves to turn the screw.
C produces the same effect as a counter-spring, etc.

### CHAPTER XVIII.

->*The benefit of draining marshes that border the sea.*<-

It is possible, within the same <term>deep sea</term> <note>original: "pelago"; a term for the open sea or a vast body of water.</note>, to make the surface of the water lower where the bottom has an <term>eddy</term> <note>original: "retroso"; a whirlpool or a place where the water flows back on itself.</note>, compared to the surface of the water that is being struck <note>The text likely refers to the "percussion" or force of an incoming tide or current.</note>

<summary>This page serves as a catalog of various hydraulic design variations referenced by figure numbers, followed by the start of a theoretical observation on how water levels differ in the sea based on bottom currents and eddies.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, mechanical drawings, marsh drainage, sea levels, whirlpools, coastal engineering</keywords>

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page transitions from the general observation that sea levels can be manipulated by eddies to specific mechanical designs for draining marshes using a device Leonardo calls a "cicognola" and an artificially created whirlpool.</meta>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

...from the fall of another water. Let there be an **artificial eddy** <note>original: "retroso accidentale"; an artificially created vortex or whirlpool used to lower the local water level to facilitate drainage.</note> in the sea, the surface of whose bottom is twice as deep as the surface of the lowest ground, having the bottom of the pond bordering that sea at the same level. Let A O M N (fig. 211. plate 47) be the pond with a surface equal to the surface of the sea bordering it, that is A O L H. I will make the artificial eddy E F D G in the sea twice as deep as the height AO of the pond. And I will move the **pump** <term>cicognola</term> <note>Literally "little stork"; a term Leonardo used for various hydraulic lifting devices, such as siphons or crank-operated pumps.</note> A B C D, which will necessarily pass through the <term>pivotless housing</term> <note>original: "rocca senza polo"; a supporting structure for the machinery that operates without a central fixed axle point.</note>, and will pass over the embankment of the pond P Q, and enter the bottom of the eddy, and there it will lead the water of the pond.

Furthermore, the pump N M O P R is good when the housing has a pivot, as is illustrated here, and as is also seen illustrated in the **"boat of motion."** <note>Likely referring to a specific mechanical drawing of a boat-mounted engine elsewhere in his notebooks.</note> But the other is better, because the percussion of the water strikes the bottom, and its motion also adds weight.

The motion of the eddy can be caused either by a boat, as you see, or by a mill. Let A B C be the shore of the sea, B M the mouth of the pond, M F the pond, and O H N a channel taken from the upper part of the river for the mill; it holds all the water of the river, otherwise the pond [drainage system] would not turn with the pump N D and the fall of the mill D C, and the channel of the mill's fall to the sea, etc.

The pump and the eddy must be above the mouth B M. And in practice, let the **secret of the pump** <note>original: "segreto di cicognola"; refers to the internal mechanical workings or the specialized design of the device.</note> be covered and walled in, and let the eddy be made at a distance from the sea, etc.

### CHAPTER XIX

#### On the same subject.

A N M (fig. 212. plate 48.) is the vessel of the artificial eddy, which has a diameter of one and a half **braccia** <term>braccia</term> <note>A Florentine unit of measurement; one "braccio" is approximately 23 inches or 58 centimeters.</note> at the mouth, and two-fifths at the hole, into which the mouth of the pump enters through the path A B; C D is the axle that turns under the pivot E faster than the course of the water's intake into that vessel, or at an equal speed, so that the motion of the water [is maintained], and added to such motion. And if you indeed want to make the axle faster than the motion of the axle H, you will exert more effort; that is, the axle will give more labor to its motor the faster it is. And if the speed of the water and the axle were equal, it would feel little or no effort, as I show in the fifth [book] on **flying creatures** <note>Referring to Leonardo's studies on bird flight, specifically the "Codex on the Flight of Birds," where he discusses fluid dynamics and resistance.</note> regarding power and resistance—the power of the flapping of wings, and the resistance of the air struck by such wings, etc.

The pump must push its water behind the motion of the axle, and such pushing must also be done upwards along the line of obliquity.

<summary>Leonardo describes the engineering of an artificial eddy to lower water levels and facilitate the drainage of marshes into the sea, comparing the mechanical resistance of the pumps to the aerodynamics of bird flight.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulic engineering, marsh drainage, siphons, artificial eddies, mechanical resistance, fluid dynamics, Codex on the Flight of Birds</keywords>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

...of the vessel; and between this vessel and the axle let there be a finger’s width of space, and no more, so that the excessive weight of the water does not impede the motion of said axle. The cut of the **Axle** must be oblique as the drawing shows, etc.
Vessel X must be completely sealed, except at the bottom, where the water enters only through the <term>pump</term> <note>original: "cicognola"; literally 'little stork', Leonardo's term for a siphon or a crank-operated lifting pump.</note> brought there from the pond, and hole B is where the same water exits into the sea. The handle must be all of one piece, etc.

### CHAPTER XX.
->*Method for emptying the moats of a territory, or castle.*<-

If you wish to empty the moats of a territory, you shall first make a ditch outside the embankment of said moat, which should be lower than the moats themselves, as appears in FG (fig. 213, plate 49). Then let there be the channel MF, which should be level so that the fall reaches the wheel as high as possible; let the ditch GQ be filled with enough water so that the wheel can catch the water. Then release the flow of water at MF, and the <term>buckets</term> <note>original: "casse"; literally 'boxes', referring to the water-catching compartments on a waterwheel.</note> will fill; acting as a <term>counterweight</term> <note>original: "contrappeso".</note>, they will move along, leaving their water in the <term>reservoir</term> <note>original: "bottino"; a cistern or collection tank for drainage.</note>, to match in quantity that which the <term>wheel</term> <note>original: "rota".</note> carries upward and discharges through the channel ST. Set the turns of the wheel's buckets in such a way that the water from MF, which arrives as a counterweight for the buckets, equals the weight of the water carried upward; otherwise the reservoir GZ would quickly fill up, and the wheel would stop, etc.

### CHAPTER XXI.
->*Another method.*<-

AB (fig. 213, plate 49) is the moat to be emptied; CDE is a large <term>hemp</term> <note>original: "canape"; a strong fiber used for ropes and heavy-duty fabrics.</note> cloth, curved and of such length that it can be submerged in the water.
F are lead balls that serve as a counterweight to pull the said hemp cloth underwater.
GG is a reservoir where the said cloth, once raised, pours the water; DHIK is the <term>instrument</term> <note>original: "istrumento".</note> for raising the said cloth, etc.

<summary>Leonardo describes mechanical solutions for draining moats and ponds, detailing a balanced bucket-wheel system and a heavy hemp-cloth hoist used to lift and relocate large volumes of water.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, drainage systems, waterwheel, counterweights, moats, engineering designs, hemp cloth hoist, reservoir</keywords>

# LEONARDO DAVINCI

### CHAPTER XXII.

->*Utility of cleaning and deepening a canal.*<-

Position the <term>sluice</term> <note>original: "serraglio"; here referring to a temporary barrier or weir used to redirect water flow.</note> SO (fig. 214, plate 50) in such a way that it runs along one bank of the said canal. Then, on the opposite bank, place a wooden barrier to protect it from the force of a <term>backwater</term> <note>original: "retroso"; an eddy or whirlpool caused by water hitting an obstruction.</note>, which shall be barrier DF. Across the width of the canal, ensure there is a support OD, which holds back the water of the canal so that, as it all gushes between the bank PQ and the sluice SO, it does not overflow its bed; thus, by opening the entire <term>sluice gate</term> <note>original: "cataratta"; literally 'cataract', but here Leonardo means a gate that, when opened, creates a powerful scouring flow.</note> A, the water will strike the bottom, surge upward, and radiate outward, scouring the entire site of this device. Depending on the quality of the material—whether it be lighter or heavier—the water will carry away and tear up the bottom of such a canal for a considerable distance. And such a sluice can be moved along the length of the canal more or less according to need; and with this method, two men will do the work of a hundred.

### CHAPTER XXIII.

->*To pull a timber out of the water with ease.*<-

Let a timber be pulled from the water with ease and speed. Let KH (fig. 214, plate 50) be the given timber, and B be the anchor point of a rope, which goes to catch the timber at H and returns to the hands of the man at C. Do the same from the opposite side at AOP, and at the same time let the <term>lever</term> <note>original: "lieva".</note> FG be made to grip the timber in the middle. Simultaneously, pull at CP and lower the head of the lever F. In this way, the timber will mount the bank ML by turning upon the lever FG.

### CHAPTER XXIV.

->*For loading a great weight onto a vessel.*<-

To load a very great unified weight upon a sturdy boat without using <term>winches</term> <note>original: "argani"; mechanical devices used to pull or lift heavy loads with ropes.</note>, levers, ropes, or any manual force, it will be necessary to draw such a weight to the shore of the water or sea, aligned lengthwise toward the sea along the shore. Then, let a canal be dug that passes under this weight, extending as far as the length of the boat permits; similarly, the width of this canal should be made according to the width of the boat. The boat should then be filled with water and drawn under the weight; then, once the water is pumped out, the vessel will rise to such a height that it will lift the said weight.

<summary>Leonardo describes hydraulic and mechanical methods for waterway maintenance and heavy lifting, including a system for scouring canal beds with water pressure and a clever use of buoyancy to load heavy cargo onto ships.</summary>
<keywords>hydraulics, canal cleaning, sluice gates, mechanical advantage, levers, ship loading, buoyancy, Archimedes' principle, engineering labor efficiency</keywords>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

...from the land by itself, which, once loaded in this manner, you can pull into the sea and lead to the place appointed for it (fig. 215, plate 51).

### CHAPTER XXV.

->*Of the wall for resisting the destruction of rivers.*<-

This wall, built to resist the destructive force of rivers, should be four <term>braccia</term> <note>A Florentine unit of measurement, roughly 23 inches or 58 centimeters.</note> thick (fig. 216, plate 51); and likewise, for every four cubits of length, there should be a <term>sperone</term> <note>original: "sperone"; a buttress or "spur" used to reinforce the wall.</note> of four or five cubits. These spurs should extend from the wall into the earth of the embankment and should be two cubits thick. The wall should be built with a <term>scarpa</term> <note>original: "scarpa"; a "batter" or slope where the base of the wall is wider than the top to increase stability.</note>, meaning that for every two cubits of height, it should have one inch of outward slope.

### CHAPTER XXVI.

->*Repair to a ruined embankment.*<-

If you wish to repair a ruined embankment, first create a <term>palificata</term> <note>A row of driven piles or a picket fence structure.</note> in the manner shown here (fig. 217, plate 51), then nail planks to the outside of it. Afterward, place trees or <term>onicci</term> <note>original: "onicci"; alders, a tree species known for its remarkable durability when submerged in water.</note> inside this piling, spaced a span's width from one another in every direction. At the end of four years, they will all be grafted together <note>Leonardo is likely referring to the roots and growth of the alders intertwining to create a living, reinforced barrier.</note> and will form a wall of great resistance, etc.

### CHAPTER XXVII.

->*Piling to create a good foundation in water.*<-

The piles should be between one-third and one-half cubit thick and about two and a half cubits long. They should be made of oak or <term>ontano</term> <note>original: "ontano"; alder.</note>—that is, <term>oniccio</term>—and above all, they must be green wood (fig. 218 and 219, plate 51). I have seen a section of the old wall of Pavia being refounded, built into the banks of the Ticino <note>The Ticino is a major river in northern Italy that flows through Pavia.</note>; the piles found there made of oak were as black as charcoal, while those made of alder had a red color like <term>verzino</term> <note>original: "verzino"; brazilwood, which was used in the Renaissance to produce a vibrant red dye.</note>. They were very heavy and as hard as iron, without any blemish. When you wish to drive these piles, first start the opening with an iron-tipped bar. The mallet should be as thick as an arm and two cubits long with four handles; it should be made of oak and bound with iron rings at the ends. Similarly, the piles themselves must be shod with iron "A" while they are being driven.

Once you have driven the piles, observe carefully if between said piles...

<summary>Leonardo details the construction of river defenses and foundations, emphasizing the use of sloped walls (scarpa), buttresses, and the superior durability of alder wood when submerged in water.</summary>
<keywords>civil engineering, hydraulic architecture, riverbanks, alders, oak, Pavia, foundations, piling, construction tools</keywords>

# LEONARDO DA VINCI

...should there be rising springs of water <note>original: "sorgimenti d' acqua"; referring to groundwater bubbling up during construction</note>, place a drainage conduit <note>original: "doccia"; a gutter or pipe used to divert water away from the masonry</note> above them, as shown at M. Then pour in the mortar <note>original: "calcina"; a lime-based mortar or cement</note> until it is level with the heads of the piles <note>original: "pali"; the wooden stakes driven into the earth to support the foundation</note>, which should protrude by a third of a <term>braccio</term> <note>A Florentine unit of measurement; a third of a braccio is approximately 7.6 inches or 19 centimeters.</note>. Then build the masonry above with large, square bricks, and then with ordinary bricks.

## Figure 41

Leonardo was deeply fascinated by the geometry of the spiral, seeing it both in nature—like the growth of plants or the flow of water—and in mechanical applications such as the <term>screw</term> (original: vite) or the tension of a <term>spring</term> (original: molla).</note>

This study demonstrates Leonardo’s interest in <term>mechanical advantage</term>—the use of machines to multiply human or animal strength to lift heavy loads or store energy.</note>

### Plate 20.

**113.**
<note>This diagram serves as a geometric or optical study. A horizontal line connects points **m**, **a**, and **b**. A vertical line drops from **a** to point **c**, forming a right-angled triangle **acb** with a hypotenuse <note>original: "ipotenusa"</note> extending from **c** to **b**. These simple lines were often used by Leonardo to calculate perspectives or the trajectory of light and shadow.</note>

**114.**
<note>A hydraulic sketch showing water flowing over a rectangular ledge or <term>weir|a low dam built across a river to raise the level of water upstream or regulate its flow</term>. It depicts the churning of the water at the base of the fall and the subsequent formation of surface waves, illustrating Leonardo's interest in the transition from smooth flow to turbulence.</note>

**115.**
<note>This sketch shows a diamond-shaped lattice or mesh pattern. Leonardo often explored such patterns to understand the structural integrity of materials or to visualize the repeating geometric forms found in the "braiding" of water currents.</note>

**116.**
<note>A study of <term>fluid dynamics|the science of how liquids move</term> showing the streamlines of water flowing from left to right around a central obstacle. The source of the flow is marked with three cursive characters (likely **qqq**). Points **a** and **c** designate specific currents, showing how the water separates and then seeks to rejoin after passing a barrier.</note>

**117.**
<note>A detailed hydraulic illustration showing water entering a horizontal rectangular vessel or trough. The flow curls into a series of five large, interconnected spiral <term>vortices|whirlpools or eddies</term>. This drawing is a testament to Leonardo's keen eye for the rotational motion of fluids, which he famously compared to the curling of human hair.</note>

->**Fig. 161.**<-

<note>This figure reproduces Leonardo da Vinci's pioneering studies in <term>hydrodynamics</term>—the study of liquids in motion—or <term>aerodynamics</term>. Leonardo was searching for the "solid of least resistance," or the shape that could move through water or air with the least amount of drag.</note>

1.  An upper drawing of a projectile or boat-shaped body. It features a rounded, blunt front labeled **c** and a tapering, conical tail labeled **d**. <note>Leonardo observed that a tapered "tail" often helped reduce the turbulence that slows an object down.</note>

# Plate 36.

->Fig. 199.<-

<note>This is a diagrammatic illustration of a mountain landscape. At the summit sits a small house or chapel, perhaps representing a destination or a point of observation. A broad, zigzagging path winds up the left side of the mountain, demonstrating a practical method for climbing steep terrain. On the right, a channel or pipe—illustrating a <term>siphon effect|A hydraulic process where liquid is moved through a tube from a higher level to a lower level by passing over an intermediate height higher than the reservoir</term>—descends from the upper slopes to the base. This flow is annotated with letters marking specific points: **c** at the upper bend, **d** along the descent, and **b** at the lower outlet.</note>

This diagram, labeled Figure 199, depicts a mountain with a structure at its peak. While the zigzag path on the left shows human movement across the terrain, the curved line on the right serves as a schematic for water movement. Leonardo da Vinci frequently used these types of drawings to analyze the physical properties of the earth and the mechanics of liquids, often drawing parallels between the "veins" of the earth (water) and the anatomy of the human body.

<summary>This page features Plate 36, Figure 199, which illustrates a mountain landscape used as a scientific model for zigzagging paths and hydraulic water flow (siphoning).</summary>

# Plate 40

->Plate 40.<-

->Figure 203.<-

<note>This illustration depicts a sophisticated hydraulic pump system designed to lift water. The mechanism is supported by a robust timber frame. At the base, a water wheel or gear wheel is submerged in a trough; as it rotates, it turns a crankshaft. This crankshaft, via a vertical connecting rod with adjustment holes, moves a long horizontal lever arm. This lever, in turn, operates two vertical pistons or cylinders that draw water from the large reservoir at the bottom and pump it upwards. Such designs reflect a deep interest in automating labor-intensive tasks like drainage or irrigation.</note>

# Plate 49.

<note>The page features a detailed technical engraving of a hydraulic lifting machine, a design pioneered by Leonardo da Vinci. This device, known as a spiral pump, consists of a wheel equipped with multiple curved tubes. As the wheel rotates, these conduits scoop up water from a lower source and channel it upward through the spiral until it is discharged into an elevated trough.</note>

<note>Key reference points on the diagram:</note>

*   **r**: <note>Positioned near the upper left section of the outer wheel housing.</note>
*   **p**: <note>Located at the intersection where the water exits the rotating wheel and enters the horizontal discharge channel.</note>
*   **q**: <note>Indicates the water level in the lower supply reservoir.</note>
*   **m**: <note>Located at the exit point of the elevated horizontal channel, where the water is delivered to its destination.</note>

### Fig. 211.
<note>This hydraulic study depicts submerged vessels and water tanks, exploring the fundamental principles of <term>hydrostatics</term> (the study of fluids at rest) and how pressure influences fluid movement.</note>

**Left Section:**
In this portion of the diagram, a pear-shaped vessel is shown submerged within a larger tank. A vertical pipe, labeled **0**, rises from the base of the vessel, while an internal mechanism or valve is marked with the letter **r**. The boundaries of the surrounding tank are indicated by the labels **A** and **l**.

**Central and Right Section:**
The center of the drawing shows two vertical jets or tubes, labeled **p** and **q**, situated within a central basin. To the right, a second bulbous submerged vessel is depicted. Its various components are identified by labels: the top of the vessel is marked **e**, the center is **d**, and the openings at the bottom are denoted by **g** and **h**.

---

<note>This mechanical diagram illustrates a vertical shaft assembly. Such designs were often conceived by Leonardo for use in propulsion systems or as part of a horizontal water wheel.</note>

The drawing shows a curved horizontal frame that supports a vertical axle. A gear or drive wheel is mounted onto this axle to transmit power. At the bottom of the shaft, a large blade resembling a paddle or a rudder is attached, likely intended to push against water. To the left of this mechanism, a decorative spiral or swirl is sketched, and the number **70** is written to the right of the device.

---

<note>This diagram focuses on fluid dynamics, specifically illustrating how water moves from a reservoir into a discharge channel.</note>

The diagram features a large oval reservoir, labeled **b**. On the left side, there is an intake or vertical section defined by points **a**, **b**, and **c**. These sections meet at a junction marked **m**. From this point, water flows into a long outlet pipe or <term>conduit</term> (a channel for conveying water), labeled **k**, which extends horizontally to the right. The specific path of the flow is tracked through points labeled **p** and **n**.

**Technical Vocabulary:**
<term>Hydraulics</term> (the study of liquid flow), <term>Mechanics</term> (the study of machines and motion), Plate, Channel, Reservoir.

<meta>Following the previously blank folio, this page begins a new chapter concerning the physical properties of heat and air currents.</meta>

->**ON THE MOTIONS OF VAPORS**<-

### Chapter III. — *On the causes that accelerate the motion of smoke.*

The motion of smoke and vapors becomes faster for three principal reasons: the first is the force of the heat that drives them <margin>Compare Aristotle, *Meteorology*</margin>; the second is the narrowness of the place through which they pass; the third is the thinness <note>The author uses 'rarità' to describe low-density or "rare" air.</note> of the air that receives them.

<column-break/>

The more vigorous the fire, the more the vapors expand and <term>dissipate<note>The author uses 'svanire' to describe the rapid dispersal or thinning of smoke into the atmosphere.</note></term> with force. If, then, the duct or chimney is narrow, the smoke, finding obstacles at the sides, strives to exit with greater speed along the straight path.

| Medium | Effect |
| :--- | :--- |
| Intense heat | Rapid expansion |
| Narrow chimney | Increased speed |
| Thin air | Easy ascent |

<insert>Note that a contrary wind can impede such motion.</insert> As <unclear>Cardano</unclear> <note>Girolamo Cardano was a 16th-century Italian physician and mathematician known for his encyclopedic works on nature.</note> writes in his book *On Subtlety* <note>original Latin title: "De Subtilitate"</note>, smoke is a mixed body.

<summary>This chapter outlines the three physical conditions—thermal force, spatial confinement, and atmospheric density—that govern the velocity of rising vapors. The author cites classical and Renaissance scholars to support these observations on fluid dynamics.</summary>
<keywords>smoke, vapors, heat, fluid dynamics, Aristotle, Girolamo Cardano, chimneys, thermodynamics</keywords>
